### I / 一

===Original===

When Martin Birck had got the white cap, his first errand was to go into a cigar booth to buy a cane of cinnamon wood and a package of cigarettes. The young girl who stood in the shop had black eyes and a thick bang. Her exterior corresponded but imperfectly with the ideal of his dreams, which belonged to a more blonde and Gretchen-like sphere; but when she congratulated him pleasantly on his white cap and at the same time regarded him with a look full of kindliness, despite the fact that he had never before been in her shop, he suddenly felt all warm about the heart, caught her dirty hand, which lay outstretched across the counter display of Cameo and Duke of Durham, and tenderly kissed it. However, he repented almost at once. He had no doubt behaved badly. He did not, to be sure, imagine that the young girl was completely innocent﻿—she had no doubt a lover, possibly several; but that was no reason why anyone at all had the right to come in from the street and kiss her hand just like that. He was embarrassed and didn’t know what to say or do, till he finally plucked up courage to select a cane, light a cigarette, and go out.

Queen Street was still wet after the last shower, little ladies with jogging bustles lifted their skirts to jump over puddles, which mirrored the blue above; stylish gentlemen with thin angular legs and canes like Martin’s swung their top-hats in pompous salutation, revealing in the act heads so close-clipped that the scalp shone through. Over the roofs and chimneys of the gray houses the restless white spring clouds hurried in fluttering haste, and far down at the bottom of the street the sunlight quivered between churches and towers.

Martin stopped in front of every store window to see the reflection of his white cap. He could not understand how he had become a student. Up to the last he had believed he would be flunked. His surprise was the more joyous when he received his student certificate the same as the others, and especially when he came to the closing lines, “In consideration whereof the aforesaid M. Birck has been adjudged worthy to receive the certificate: *Graduated with honor*.” These words caused his heart to swell with deep gratitude toward his corps of teachers, for although he considered himself fairly proficient, it was far beyond his expectations to find this idea shared by his instructors. During the last terms he had seldom known his lessons. Often he had not even been able to bring himself to read them over in the ten-minute intermission before classes or to slip a couple of loose leaves from his textbook into his Bible so as to study them during morning prayers, while the lector in theology stood on the platform and talked bosh﻿—a resource which ordinarily even the most frivolous of his comrades would not fail to use. He would, however, have liked to gratify his parents with good marks, although for his own part he had not any great ambition in that direction; but during the last years there had come over him a dull apathy for everything connected with school, against which he could do nothing. It was so hard for him to take it in full earnest. Whenever, contrary to his custom, he had distinguished himself in this or that subject, he was almost ashamed within himself, as if he had done something stupid. As often as he was supposed to dig down into the paltry details in which textbooks delight, he felt himself as ridiculous as the man who, when his house was on fire, saved the poker.

Now that the poker was saved, however, he was so overjoyed that he could have sung; he felt that he was happy and free, as he hastened home with his white cap, home to the blossoming street of his childhood. But the street was no longer the same as before. From a single plot the cherry tree still stretched its branches out over a mossy board fence; everything else was great red brick buildings and small commonplace meetinghouses. The rowdy Franz could no longer disturb what idyllic atmosphere was still left, for he had grown up and become big, and had now been for some time behind the bars of Langholm jail.

===Chinese===

马丁·比尔克（Martin Birck）戴上白帽之后，头一件事就是走进一家雪茄烟亭，买一根桂皮木手杖和一包香烟。站在店里的年轻姑娘有一双黑眼睛和一头浓密的刘海。她的外表与他梦中的理想并不十分相符——那理想属于更金发碧眼、更格蕾琴（Gretchen）式的圈子；可当她亲切地祝贺他得了白帽，同时又用充满善意的目光打量他——尽管他此前从未进过她的店——他突然觉得心里一阵温热，一把抓住她那只脏手——那手正摊开搁在摆满卡梅奥（Cameo）和达勒姆公爵（Duke of Durham）香烟的柜台上——温柔地吻了一下。不过他几乎立刻就后悔了。他无疑失礼了。诚然，他并不以为这年轻姑娘全然天真无邪——她多半有个情人，也许还不止一个；可这也不是随便什么人都有权从街上走进来、就这样吻她一下手的理由。他窘住了，不知道说什么、做什么才好，直到最后鼓起勇气挑了一根手杖，点上一支香烟，走了出去。

一阵骤雨过后，王后街（Queen Street）还湿着，臀部衬垫颠颤的小个子太太们提起裙子跳过水洼，水洼里映着头顶的蓝天；时髦的先生们迈着细瘦棱角分明的腿，拄着和马丁相仿的手杖，煞有介事地挥动大礼帽致意，这一挥之间便露出剪得极短的头发，短得头皮都透了出来。灰色楼房的屋顶和烟囱之上，躁动不安的白色春云扑簌簌地匆匆掠过，而在街道远远的尽头，阳光在教堂与塔楼之间颤动闪烁。

马丁在每家店铺的橱窗前都要停一停，看看自己白帽的映影。他不明白自己是怎么当上大学生的。直到最后他都以为自己会落榜。因此当他和别人一样领到升学证书时，那份惊喜便格外浓烈，尤其是读到结尾那几行：“鉴于上述情形，该生 M. 比尔克经评定合格，准予颁发证书：*以优等成绩毕业*。”这几行字使他的心中涌起对全体任课教师的深深感激，因为尽管他自认为相当有几分才学，却发现老师们也持同样看法，这实在远出他的意料。最后几个学期他很少把功课弄明白过。他常常连课间十分钟里把功课匆匆过一遍都提不起劲，或者把课本里散落的几页夹进《圣经》，好在晨祷时偷看——那时神学讲师正站在讲台上胡说八道——这一招，就连他同伴中最轻浮的家伙通常也绝不会忘了用。不过，他倒是愿意用好分数让父母高兴，尽管就他自己而言在这方面并无多大抱负；可是最近几年，一种对一切与学校有关之事的沉沉倦怠攫住了他，他拿它毫无办法。他实在难以把学业全然当真。每当一反常态地在某门功课上出人头地，他心里几乎感到羞惭，仿佛做了什么蠢事似的。每逢他该埋头钻研教科书津津乐道的那些琐碎细节时，他就觉得自己可笑得像那个房子失火时却抢救拨火棍的人。

如今拨火棍总算抢救出来了，他高兴得几乎要放声歌唱；他揣着白帽匆匆往家赶，回到童年那条繁花盛开的街上，只觉得自己又幸福又自由。可这条街已不复旧貌。只有一处院落里，樱桃树仍把枝条伸出长满青苔的木板栅栏；其余的一切都成了高大的红砖楼和平庸的小礼拜堂。无赖弗朗茨（Franz）再也无法搅扰这里残存的田园气氛了，因为他已长大成人，如今已在朗霍尔姆（Langholm）监狱的铁栅后面待了一些时日。

### II / 二

===Original===

Home was quieter and more empty than before. Maria, Martin’s sister, had been married a year ago to a doctor who lived far away in the country, and grandmother was no longer there.

In the evening Martin and his companions were to have a supper at Hasselbacken. Martin’s father gave him five crowns to offer to the joy of youth, and his mother took him aside and said: “Martin, Martin, you must promise me to be careful tonight and not be led into any foolishness. Don’t make a point of emptying your glass every time anyone drinks a toast with you, or you’ll lose your head. The best thing would be just to pretend you drank. And I must tell you, Martin, that there is a class of dreadful women who do nothing else but try to lead young men to their destruction. You must beware of them especially. Dear Martin, if I only knew you had given yourself to the Lord and had your thoughts fixed on Him, I shouldn’t be anxious about you; but I know you don’t do that. Their very breath is poisonous; if you only stand on the street and talk to such a woman, you may catch the most frightful diseases that no doctor in the world can cure.”

===Chinese===

家里比从前更安静、更空落了。马丁的姐姐玛丽亚（Maria）一年前嫁给了一位住在遥远乡间的医生，祖母也不在了。

晚上马丁和同伴们要在哈塞尔巴肯（Hasselbacken）吃晚饭。马丁的父亲给了他五克朗，让他拿去给青春的欢乐添彩；母亲把他拉到一边说："马丁，马丁，你得答应我，今晚要小心，别被人引进什么蠢事里去。别每逢有人同你碰杯就非把杯中酒干掉不可，不然你会失态的。最好是装作喝下去了。还有我得告诉你，马丁，有一类可怕的女人，她们别的什么都不做，专想着把青年男人引向毁灭。你对她们要格外当心。亲爱的马丁，我只要知道你已把自己交给了主、一心思念着他，就不会为你担忧了；可我知道你不肯这样。那种女人连呼吸都是有毒的；哪怕你只是站在街上同这样一个女人说话，也可能染上最可怕的病，世界上没有一个医生治得好。"

===Original===

“Mother dear,” said Martin, “you’re always getting off on that.”

He took up his white cap, said goodbye and went.

His mother followed him with troubled eyes, and when he was gone, sat down in a dark corner and wept. For she knew she was going to lose him as mothers always lose their sons.

===Chinese===

"亲爱的妈妈，"马丁说，"你怎么又扯到这上头去了。"

他拿起白帽，道了别，走了。

母亲用不安的目光送他出门；他走后，她在一个阴暗的角落里坐下，哭了。因为她知道，自己就要失去他了，一如母亲们总是失去她们的儿子。

### III / 三

===Original===

Martin thought of his mother as he went along the Avenue on the way to the Park. How could the relations between them have become what they were? To her he was still a little child. When he first began to speak to her of his religious doubts, she pretended to believe that it was something he had got from outside, from bad comrades or some wicked book. Later things reached such a point that he could no longer talk to her about anything but the most ordinary subjects﻿—about shirts and socks and buttons to be sewed on. If their conversation ever took a serious turn, they treated each other mutually as little children. Thus, without his meaning it or noticing it before it was too late, he got a condescending tone that hurt her, so that after such a conversation a thorn remained in the heart of each.

She often lay awake at night weeping and sorrowing over his unbelief. She herself, however, was of the earth in her thoughts, her hopes, and the whole of her being. She believed in hell of course, because she believed in the Bible; but she could never seriously imagine that her son or anyone at all whom she knew and associated with would go to such a horrible place. It was not therefore on account of his soul that she grieved most but for his future here on earth, since she had observed that things did not ordinarily go well in the world with those who contemned God and religion. Some of them got into prison, others left their country to go among strangers, and all aroused distrust and ill-will among respectable folk. She feared that her son might come to be one of these, and it was this idea which kept her awake at night and left her with swollen eyes. She had no more precious dream than that he should be “like other folk,” as most people are, if possible better and above all happier, but still on the whole as they were. She could imagine that her son might become a poet, she could even wish it, for she loved poetry; the tears came into her eyes when he read her some of his poems; but she pictured it that he would sit at some office work on weekdays, and only on Sundays or in his free hours write some verses about sunsets, which he would send in to the Swedish Academy and get a prize, so that he would become at the same time a great poet and a respected business man with an assured income. She believed in full seriousness that he would be more highly thought of among poets if he was in an office and had a title than if he just wrote. That was how it had been with all the real poets. Tegnér was a bishop, and even Bellman had at least had a position in the lottery bureau. As an example that Martin should especially take to follow, she used to mention a poet whom she had known when she was young, who was now an auditor in the Court of Exchequer and wrote verses about everything that was grand and beautiful, about the sea and the sun and the king, and had been decorated with the Order of Vasa. Such a life she considered noble and to be emulated, and when her dreams of her son’s future were at their highest, it was something of this sort she imagined.

===Chinese===

马丁沿着林荫大道往公园去，一路想着母亲。他们之间的关系怎么会变成这样？在她眼里，他还是个小孩子。他最初开始向她谈起自己的宗教怀疑时，她装作相信那是他从外面沾染来的——来自坏伙伴或是哪本邪恶的书。后来事情发展到这一步：除了最平常的话题——衬衣、袜子、该缝的扣子——他再无法同她谈别的。他们的谈话一旦沾上严肃的色彩，双方便彼此把对方当小孩子对待。就这样，并非出于本意，等察觉时已太迟，他养成了一种居高临下的口气，那口气伤害着她，以致每回这样的谈话之后，两人心里各留下了一根刺。

她常常夜里躺着睡不着，为他的不信而流泪、忧伤。然而她自己在思想、希望乃至整个存在上，都是属乎尘世的。她当然信地狱，因为她信《圣经》；可她从来不能认真想象，她的儿子、或任何她认识并与之来往的人，会落到那样一个可怕的地方去。所以她最悲伤的倒不是为他的灵魂，而是为他在世上的前程，因为她看得出来，凡藐视上帝和宗教的人，在世上通常都没有好下场。他们有的进了监狱，有的离乡背井流落到陌生人中间，而且全都在体面人中间惹起猜疑与恶意。她生怕儿子也会变成这样的人，正是这个念头使她夜里睡不着觉、眼睛哭得浮肿。她最珍贵的梦想，莫过于他"和别人一样"——像大多数人那样，如果可能，更好些，尤其是更幸福些，但总归和常人一样。她能想象儿子成为诗人，甚至愿意他成为诗人，因为她爱诗；他给她念他自己写的诗时，她眼里噙着泪水；可她设想的是：他平日坐在某个机关里办公，只在星期天或空闲时刻写几首咏落日的诗，寄给瑞典文学院，得一笔奖金，这样他同时既是大诗人，又是有稳定收入的、受人尊敬的实业家。她一本正经地相信，如果他在机关里任职、有个头衔，他在诗人中间就会比单纯写作更受敬重。所有真正的诗人都是这样的。特格纳尔（Tegnér）是主教，就连贝尔曼（Bellman）好歹也在彩票局有个职位。作为马丁尤其应当效法的榜样，她常提起一位她年轻时认识的诗人，如今在审计法院当审计官，写一切宏伟美好事物的诗——写海、写太阳、写国王——还得过瓦萨勋章（Order of Vasa）。这样的生活她认为是高尚的、值得效法的；每当她对儿子前程的梦想升到最高处，她想象的就是诸如此类的东西。

===Original===

But Martin dreamed other dreams. He wanted to be a poet. He would write a book; a novel or a lyric sequence, or best of all a drama of ideas in the same verse form as *Brand* or *Peer Gynt*. He would devote his life to searching for the truth and giving to mankind what he found or thought he had found of it. He would also become famous, a great man; he would earn a lot of money, he would buy a little house for his father and a new silk dress for his mother﻿—her old one was worn and faded. He would be envied by men and sought after by women, but of all the women in the world he should not love more than one, and that one a woman who loved another man. This unhappy love should give his thoughts depth and bitterness and his poems wings. But he had a dark feeling that while he sought for truth he should only find truths, and that while he gave them to men in verse more wonderful than any music or in a clear and cold prose with words like sharp teeth, he would despise himself for reaping honor and gold for the morsels he had found by accident while he was seeking for something else. This self-contempt would eat into his soul and make of him an empty husk. But he would not let the world note anything; he would paint his cheeks, pencil his eyebrows and hold up his head, and at the very moment when he himself most deeply despised his poetry and set it below the humblest manual labor, he would inspire men most and be elected to the Swedish Academy to succeed Wirsén. With a countenance immobile as a mask he would give the usual flowery oration on his predecessor. Never again after that would he set pen to paper. In a strangely colorful and disordered life he would seek to deaden his despair. No sin should be unknown to him; in broad daylight he would drive in an open carriage through the streets with harlots and buffoons, and he would pass the nights in drinking and play. Till one gloomy October night he wearied of his mad and empty life, made a fire in his stove and burned his papers, emptied a glass of dark red wine spiced with a strange herb, and went to sleep to awake no more.﻿ ﻿…

Or perhaps it was unnecessary that his life should end so tragically. When he thought it over more carefully, this seemed to him even a trifle banal. He might just as well move to a small town, to Strengness or Grenna. There he could live alone with a parrot and a black cat. He might also have an aquarium with goldfish. Behind closed shutters he would dream away the day, but when night came he would light candles in all the rooms and pace back and forth, back and forth, meditating on the vanity of life. And when the townfolk passed his house on the way home from their evening toddy at the rathskeller, they would stop to point at his window and say: “There lives Martin Birck. He has taught like a sage and lived like a fool, and he is very unhappy.”

---

All this and a lot more Martin Birck thought as he went out the Avenue across the park on the way to Hasselbacken.

===Chinese===

可马丁做的是另一样的梦。他要当诗人。他要写一本书；一部小说，或一组抒情诗，最好呢，是一部像《布兰德》（Brand）或《培尔·金特》（Peer Gynt）那样以诗体写成的思想剧。他要毕生致力于寻求真理，把寻到的、或自以为寻到的那一点交给人类。他还要成名，成为伟人；他要挣很多钱，给父亲买一栋小房子，给母亲买一件新的绸衣裳——她那件旧的已经磨损褪色了。他要遭男人艳羡、被女人追逐，但普天之下的女人，他只爱一个，而那一个却爱着另一个男人。这场不幸的爱情要给他的思想以深度和苦味，给他的诗以翅膀。可是他有一种阴暗的预感：当他寻求真理时，他只会找到一堆零碎的真理；当他以胜过一切音乐的美妙诗行、或以词句如利齿般清晰冷峻的散文把它们交给世人时，他会为了自己偶然捡得的碎屑换来荣誉与黄金而鄙视自己。这种自我鄙视会啃噬他的灵魂，把他变成一具空壳。但他不会让世人察觉分毫；他要涂红双颊、描黑眉毛、昂起头来，正当他心底最鄙视自己的诗、把它看得比最低贱的体力劳动还不如的时刻，他最能鼓舞世人，被选进瑞典文学院，接替维尔森（Wirsén）的席位。他会以一张面具般纹丝不动的脸，照例向前任发表一篇华丽的颂词。此后他再不提笔。他要在一个光怪陆离、放浪不羁的生活里麻醉自己的绝望。没有任何一种罪他不该熟知；他要在光天化日之下，带着娼妓和小丑乘敞篷马车驶过街道；他要在饮酒和赌博中消磨一个个长夜。直到一个阴沉的十月之夜，他厌倦了自己疯狂而空虚的生活，在炉子里生起火，烧掉手稿，喝下一杯掺着奇异香草的暗红色酒，睡去，再不醒来。……

不过，也许他的生命未必要这样悲惨地收场。他更仔细地想了想，觉得这甚至有点俗套。他满可以搬到一座小城去，比如斯特伦内斯（Strengness）或格伦纳（Grenna）。在那里他可以带着一只鹦鹉和一只黑猫独自生活，还可以有一缸金鱼。白天他关上窗板，把光阴消磨在梦里面；到了夜里，他在所有房间里点起蜡烛，来回踱步、来回踱步，冥想人生的虚妄。而当镇上的人在小酒馆喝过晚酒回家的路上经过他的房子时，他们会停下来指着他的窗户说："马丁·比尔克住在那儿。他像智者一样教人，像傻子一样生活，而且他非常不幸。"

---

在出林荫大道、穿过公园去哈塞尔巴肯的路上，马丁·比尔克想着这一切，还想着许许多多别的。

### IV / 四

===Original===
The orchestra struck up the opening bars of *Mefistofele*.

Martin was sitting out by the balcony railing with Henrik Rissler. They listened to the music, looked out across the terraces, and said little. Henrik Rissler had a smooth white forehead and calm limpid eyes. His glance was long and questing; it seemed to slip over the objects nearest it in order more quickly to reach those farther off. He was the only one of Martin’s comrades who had sought his company outside of school. They used to go to each other’s homes in the afternoon to talk and smoke cigarettes, and once in a while they had gone on long walks together, often in rain, snow, or wind, out to the park or through the suburbs, talking the while of everything that concerns young men, of girls and God and the immortality of the soul. Or they would go into the gas-lighted streets with the sensation of throwing themselves into the turmoil of the world, would stand in front of etchings in bookshop windows, where they admired beyond everything a lithograph entitled *Don Juan in Hades* with a motto from Baudelaire:

> The hero all the while, half leaning on his sword,
 

 Gazed at the vessel’s wake and deigned not to look up.

This picture excited their imagination, their hearts beat more quickly when in the current of humanity they brushed elbows with a pretty girl, and they believed they were living through an entire adventure every time an old painted professional threw them an ardent glance.

But the original cause of their friendship was that they had both read Jacobsen’s novel, *Niels Lyhne*, and loved it more than other books.

===Chinese===
乐队奏起了《梅菲斯托费勒》（Mefistofele）的开场曲。

马丁同亨利克·里斯勒（Henrik Rissler）坐在外面，倚着阳台的栏杆。他们听着音乐，望着露台之外的景致，很少说话。亨利克·里斯勒有一副光洁白净的额头，一双沉静清澈的眼睛。他的目光悠长而好探询，仿佛总要滑过离它最近的事物，好更快地抵达更远处。他是马丁的同学当中唯一一个在校外也来同他结交的。从前他们常在下午到彼此家中去，聊天，抽香烟，偶尔也一起走很远的路，常常冒着雨、雪或风，走到公园，或是穿过城郊，一路谈着年轻人关心的一切，谈姑娘，谈上帝，谈灵魂的不朽。有时他们走进煤气灯照亮的街道，怀着投身世界喧嚣的兴奋，站在书店橱窗前看蚀刻画；其中他们最叹赏不已的，是一幅题为《冥府中的唐璜》（Don Juan in Hades）的石版画，画上题着波德莱尔（Baudelaire）的诗句：

> 那英雄自始至终，半倚着他的长剑，
 

 凝望船尾的航迹，不屑抬头一顾。

这幅画激起了他们的想象；在滚滚人流中同漂亮姑娘擦肩而过时，他们的心跳得更快了；每当一个涂脂抹粉、老于此道的女人朝他们抛来炽热的一瞥，他们便相信自己足足经历了一场艳遇。

不过，他们结交的最初缘由，是他们俩都读过雅各布森（Jacobsen）的小说《尼尔斯·吕讷》（Niels Lyhne），而且爱它胜过别的书。

===Original===
Inside the house the others were talking and laughing around the punch-bowls, forming themselves into groups and coteries. Most of them grouped themselves after their old custom according to social and intellectual similarities and differences, which even on the school benches had united some and separated them from others; Gabel and Billfelt, Jansson and Moberg, Planius and Tullman. Others went about somewhat morosely and talked about all keeping together.

Josef Marin rapped on a bowl and called for a toast “to the ontological proof.” It was drunk with rather halfhearted acclaim. Everyone was so tired of school matters that it didn’t seem worth the trouble even to make fun of them.

Josef Marin was to be a clergyman, but he was still not quite settled in his faith.

The music played student songs, “Stand Strong!” and “Here’s to Happy Student Days!” Dusk began to fall over the tops of the trees, over the roofs and chimneys of the city and the heights of the southern mountains, the pallid dusk of spring twilight, which rarefies and uplifts all things, making them hover with the unreality of a dream world. The crowd, who were clinking glasses and drinking down on the terrace and who a little while ago could still be clearly divided into their component parts as lieutenants and students, guardsmen and girls, and townsfolk with their wives and children, had now melted together in the dusk into an indefinite mass. As though by an inexplicable caprice the murmur suddenly became silent, so that for the moment one could hear the plash of the water in the fountain and the last sleepy bird-notes from the trees. And in the west already flamed a solitary and mighty star.

===Chinese===
屋子里，其余的人正围着潘趣酒钵谈笑，聚成一群群、一伙伙。多数人照老习惯，按社会身份和智识上的同异来分伙，这种同异早在学校的长凳上就把一些人联结在一起、把他们同另一些人隔开了：加贝尔（Gabel）和比尔费尔特（Billfelt），扬松（Jansson）和莫贝里（Moberg），普拉尼乌斯（Planius）和图尔曼（Tullman）。另一些人则神情有些阴郁地走来走去，嘴里说着大家要守在一处、不要失散之类的话。

约瑟夫·马林（Josef Marin）敲了敲酒钵，提议为"本体论的证明"干一杯。大家有气无力地喝了彩，把杯干了。众人对学校的事厌烦透了，连拿它打趣都嫌费劲。

约瑟夫·马林日后是要当牧师的，可他自己的信仰还没有十分拿定。

乐队奏起学生歌曲，《坚定地站着！》和《为欢乐的学生时代干杯！》。暮色开始落到树梢上，落到城里的屋顶和烟囱上，落到南方群山的高处——那是春日黄昏苍白的暮色，它把万物稀释、托升，使它们带着梦境一般的虚幻悬浮起来。在露台上碰杯痛饮的人群，方才还分得清构成成分——尉官和学生、卫兵和姑娘、带着妻儿的城里人——此刻已在暮色里融成一团轮廓模糊的东西。仿佛出于一阵无从解释的任性，营营的人声忽然静了下来，一时间只听得见喷泉里水的溅响，和树梢间最后几声困倦的鸟啼。西方已经燃起一颗孤零零的大星。

===Original===
“Look at Venus,” said Henrik; “how she glitters!”

Martin sat contemplatively drawing on the table, and the strokes under his hand formed themselves into a woman’s arms and breast.

“Tell me,” he asked suddenly﻿—he felt that he was blushing﻿—“tell me, do you think it’s possible for a man to live chaste till real happiness in love comes to him? That’s surely what one would wish. To be with women whom one has no feeling for, who belong to another class, who have dirty linen and use ugly words and only think about being paid﻿—that must be loathsome.”

Henrik Rissler too became a little red.

===Chinese===
"你看维纳斯，"亨里克说，"她闪得多亮！"

马丁坐着想心事，手指在桌上描画，笔下的线条自己变成了女人的手臂和乳房。

"告诉我，"他突然问——他觉得自己脸红了——"告诉我，你认为一个人有没有可能保持贞洁，直到爱情里真正的幸福降临到他身上？人自然都巴望这样。去亲近那些自己毫无感情的女人，她们属于另一个阶层，衬衣肮脏，说话粗鄙，一心只想着拿钱——那一定叫人恶心。"

亨利克·里斯勒也有点红了脸。

===Original===
“It’s possible,” he said; “yes, for some it’s always possible. People are so different. But I know this much of myself, that it will hardly be possible for me. Then at least the great love mustn’t keep me waiting much longer.”

They sat silent and gazed at the star, which glittered ever more brightly in the darkening blue.

“Venus,” Martin murmured, “Venus. She’s a great and beautiful star. But I don’t see why she should have a name. Anyhow, she doesn’t come when she’s invoked.”

===Chinese===
"有可能，"他说，"是的，对某些人来说永远都有可能。人和人差别大着呢。可我对自己的了解有这么一点：我恐怕是做不到的。那么，那场伟大的爱情至少不该再叫我等太久了。"

两人默默坐着，凝望那颗星；在渐渐暗下去的蓝天里，它越发明亮。

"维纳斯，"马丁喃喃地说，"维纳斯。她是颗伟大而美丽的星。可我不明白她凭什么要有个名字。反正，人呼唤她，她也不来。"

===Original===
Martin suddenly heard a strange voice behind his chair.

“Very true,” said the voice, “very true. She doesn’t come when she’s invoked. An equally mournful and accurate observation!”

Martin turned in surprise. The stranger was a man carelessly dressed, with a student cap, a pale narrow face and black mustaches which hung down over his mouth so that it wasn’t easy to see whether he smiled or was serious. His face looked oldish for the white cap, and it was not entirely clean.

One of Martin’s companions stood beside him and made the introduction, “Doctor Markel.”

Doctor Markel had come there with an older brother of Billfelt’s. They had come from Uppsala that day, eaten dinner at Hasselbacken, and then invited themselves to share the student supper. The elder Billfelt was giving a talk inside at the moment. Martin heard something about “Uppsala” and “alma mater.”

===Chinese===
马丁忽然听见椅子后面响起一个陌生的声音。

"说得对，"那声音说，"说得对。人呼唤她，她也不来。一个同样悲哀而又同样准确的观察！"

马丁诧异地回过头去。陌生人是个衣着随便的男人，戴顶学生帽，一张苍白窄长的脸，两撇黑胡子垂在嘴上，叫人不容易看出他是在笑还是正经。衬着那顶白帽，他的脸显得有些老气，而且也不太干净。

马丁的一个同伴站在旁边，替他们引见："马克尔博士（Doctor Markel）。"

马克尔博士是同比尔费尔特的一个哥哥一起来的。他们当天从乌普萨拉（Uppsala）过来，在哈塞尔巴肯吃了正餐，随后不请自来，加入了这场学生晚餐。这时老比尔费尔特正在屋里演说，马丁断断续续听见"乌普萨拉"和"母校"之类的字眼。

===Original===
Doctor Markel sat down beside Henrik and Martin without further ceremony.

“Two young poets, eh?” he asked. “I venture to assume so, since the gentlemen sit here by themselves apart from the vulgar throng and talk about the stars. May I ask what your attitude toward life is? Do you believe in God?”

Henrik Rissler looked at the stranger in surprise, and Martin shook his head.

Doctor Markel looked entirely serious, except that there was a slight mist over his eyes, which were large and mournful.

Some of the others had come up and were now listening to the conversation. Planius and Tullman presented the same docile countenances with which they had listened in class to the exposition of the instructor. Gabel simpered sarcastically with his fine aristocratic face, and behind him Josef Marin pressed up. Josef Marin was short and slight; he looked pale and overworked. The two or three glasses of punch he had drunk had already made him a bit convivial; but now when he heard a serious question proposed and could not see that there was any joke behind it, he broke in with all the earnestness he could summon up at the moment: “I believe in God. But I don’t conceive Him as a personal being.”

Doctor Markel seemed pleasantly surprised.

===Chinese===
马克尔博士毫不客气，就在亨利克和马丁身旁坐下了。

"两位年轻诗人，嗯？"他问。"我斗胆这样猜想，因为两位先生远离那群俗众，独自坐在这儿谈星星。可否请教二位对人生的态度？你们信上帝吗？"

亨利克·里斯勒诧异地望着陌生人，马丁摇了摇头。

马克尔博士看上去全然一本正经，只是眼睛上蒙着一层薄薄的雾气；那双眼睛很大，含着忧郁。

又有几个人围拢来，听着这场谈话。普拉尼乌斯和图尔曼摆出上课听教师讲解时那副恭顺的面孔。加贝尔那张秀气的贵族脸浮着讥诮的浅笑，他身后挤过来的是约瑟夫·马林。约瑟夫·马林矮小瘦弱，脸色苍白，一副操劳过度的样子。下肚的两三杯潘趣酒已经让他有几分酒酣耳热；可这时他听见有人提出一个严肃的问题，又看不出里头藏着什么玩笑，便拿出此刻所能召集起来的全部认真，插进来说："我信上帝。但我不把他设想为一个人格性的存在。"

马克尔博士似乎喜出望外。

===Original===
“Oh, you are a pantheist, charming! That’s what you must be too”﻿—he turned to Martin﻿—“you who are studying to be a poet. For poets and those who want to seduce girls﻿—and that all poets wish﻿—I cannot sufficiently recommend the pantheistic conception. Nothing can be more suited for turning the head of a young girl than the pantheistic rhapsodizing with which Faust answers Gretchen’s simple question, ‘Do you believe in God?’ If he had answered as simply and unaffectedly as she asked, ‘No, my child, I don’t believe in God,’ you may be sure the girl would have crossed herself, run home to her quiet chamber, and turned the key twice in the lock. Instead he answers that he both believes and disbelieves﻿—which gives the impression of deep spiritual conflict﻿—and that God is really a name for the feeling that two lovers have when they lie in the same bed. This he says with much feeling and in beautiful language, so that it does not shock her modesty; on the contrary, she thinks he talks like a priest, and the rest we know﻿—And for a poet﻿—But first allow me as an elder student.﻿ ﻿…”

With easy familiarity Doctor Markel drank brotherhood with all who were within range and then continued:

===Chinese===
"哦，您是个泛神论者，妙极了！您也必定得这样"——他转向马丁——"您这位学着要当诗人的人。因为对诗人，也对想勾引姑娘的人——而这正是所有诗人的心愿——我无论怎样极力推荐泛神论观念都不为过。要搅得一位少女神魂颠倒，再没有什么比得上浮士德回答格蕾琴那个天真问题时所用的那套泛神论的宏论了，她问：'你信上帝吗？'倘若他照她发问那样简单坦然地回答：'不，孩子，我不信上帝，'那你尽可相信，姑娘准会在胸前画个十字，跑回她安静的小闺房，把钥匙在锁孔里转上两圈。他却回答说，他既信又不信——这给人留下深刻的精神冲突的印象——又说，上帝其实是个名称，指两个恋人躺在同一张床上时的那种感觉。这些话他说得情深意切、辞采斐然，于是并不惊犯她的羞怯；恰恰相反，她倒觉得他说起话来像位教士，往后的事我们就都知道了——至于对诗人嘛——不过先请允许我，以一个老学生的身份。……"

马克尔博士熟不拘礼地同手边够得着的人都碰了杯、结了义，然后接着往下讲：

===Original===
“For a poet, pantheism is a pure godsend, a regular goldmine. If he is a churchman, he will be given the Order of Charles XIII and a good income, but will only be read by missies and be ridiculed by the liberal papers, which have the largest circulation. If he is an atheist, he will be considered a shallow and superficial fellow, a poor sort, and he will have a hard time to borrow money. No, a poet should believe in God, but in a god who is out of the ordinary run, something not yet existent, never before shown in any circus, that one can never really get hold of, for then the game would be up. The pantheistic god is exactly the raw material needed for such a being. That is the ideal for a god. Each and every one can carve him to his own taste, he is never without humor, he never punishes and of course never rewards either, he takes the whole show easily, which comes from the fact that he lacks a small characteristic that even the simplest of the town rowdies possesses to some extent: namely, personality. That’s just the choice thing about him. To a personal god one must stand in a personal relation; that is, one must become a religionist. To be a religionist is excellent if one has just come out of Langholm jail and needs to be rehabilitated in society. Otherwise it is unnecessary. You see my drift, gentlemen: to stick to a personal god entails a lot of unnecessary trouble, to be without a god entirely is ticklish. Therefore one must have an impersonal god. Such a god sets the imagination going and comes out finely in poetry without in return entailing any obligation. With such a god one will be regarded by cultured circles as a person of noble and enlightened thought and may become pretty nearly anything from an archbishop to the editor of a radical newspaper.

“In formal style this god may be called the Allfather, in common speech the Lord. As a matter of fact he doesn’t need any name, it is with him as with that star off there: no matter how one calls him, he won’t come.”

The gesture with which Doctor Markel sought and, as it were, beckoned to the star met only a dark and sullen firmament, for great clouds had gathered, the star was gone, it had grown dusky as an autumn evening, and some big raindrops now began to fall on the railing.

===Chinese===
"对诗人来说，泛神论是纯粹的天赐，一座地地道道的金矿。如果他做个教门中人，会被授予卡尔十三世勋章（Order of Charles XIII），有一份好收入，但读他的只有小姑娘们，他还要被发行量最大的自由派报纸讥笑。如果他做个无神论者，人家会认为他是个浅薄轻浮的家伙，是个没出息的货色，他连借钱都难。不，诗人应当信上帝，但信的是一个超乎寻常的上帝，一样尚不存在的东西，任何马戏班子里都从未展出过的东西，一样人永远没法真正抓住的东西——因为一抓住，戏就穿帮了。泛神论的上帝正是造就这样一个存在所需的原材料。那是上帝的理想形态。人人都可以照自己的口味雕琢他，他从不失幽默，他从不惩罚，当然也从不奖赏，他把整台戏应付得从容自如，这源于他缺少一种小小的品性，就连城里最不起眼的小流氓都多少具有的那种品性：也就是，人格。这正是他的妙处。对一个人格神，人必须立于人格的关系之中；也就是说，必须成为宗教徒。做宗教徒是极好的，如果一个人刚从朗霍尔姆监狱出来、需要重新做人的话。否则就大可不必。先生们，你们听出我的意思了吧：死守一个人格神，会招来数不清的多余麻烦；完全不信神，又颇为棘手。所以必须有一个非人格的神。这样的神激发想象，入诗也光彩，却不附带任何义务作为回报。有这样一位神，文化界会把您看作思想高贵、开明的人物，您差不多什么都能当上，从大主教到激进报纸的主编。

"在正式的行文里，这位神可以称作全能之父，在日常的言语里，就称作主。其实他用不着任何名字；他同那边那颗星是一个情形：随人怎么呼唤，他也不来。"

马克尔博士寻星、又仿佛朝它召唤的那副手势，只招来一片阴沉愠怒的天穹：大片乌云聚拢了，星星不见了，天色昏暗得像秋天的傍晚，几颗大的雨点开始落在栏杆上。

===Original===
Doctor Markel’s lecture was not well received. Josef Marin, who had been drinking more punch meanwhile and had become even paler than before, muttered something to the effect that he ought to have a smack on the jaw. The others got up in groups and discussed whether they should go home.

The elder Billfelt took in the situation, rang for the waiter and ordered champagne. He raised his glass and returned thanks in well-rounded periods for the cordiality with which he and his friend, Doctor Markel, representatives of Uppsala and alma mater, had been received by the future alumni. He then paid for the champagne and went off with Markel.

“Your brother is a gentleman,” said Gabel to Billfelt.

===Chinese===
马克尔博士的这番高论不大受欢迎。约瑟夫·马林这中间又喝了几杯潘趣酒，脸色比先前更加苍白，嘴里咕哝了一句什么，大意是该在他下巴上揍一拳。其余的人三三两两站起身，商量着该不该回家。

老比尔费尔特看清了局势，摇铃唤侍者，点了香槟。他举杯致答词，用圆熟铺陈的长句，感谢未来的校友们如此热忱地接待了他和他的朋友马克尔博士——乌普萨拉和母校的代表。随后他付了香槟的账，同马克尔一起走了。

"你哥哥是位绅士。"加贝尔对比尔费尔特说。

===Original===
---

It rained as if the heavens were opened. They crowded into a street car to go into the city and have coffee. Most of them voted to go to the Hamburg Bourse.

Martin, who had always believed the Hamburg Bourse was a place where the German merchants of Stockholm assembled to do business, found himself to his surprise entering a café that seemed to irradiate a fabulous magnificence. Here and there on the couches sat some of his former teachers and a lot of oldsters who lifted their glasses and nodded genially.

===Chinese===
---

雨倾盆而下，仿佛天上的窗户全开了。大家挤进一辆电车，进城去喝咖啡。多数人投票主张去汉堡交易所（Hamburg Bourse）。

马丁原一直以为，汉堡交易所是斯德哥尔摩的德国商人聚在一起做买卖的地方；这时却吃惊地发现，自己走进了一家咖啡馆，那地方仿佛焕发着一种传说般的富丽光彩。沙发上这儿那儿坐着他的几位从前的老师，还有许多老头儿，他们举一举杯，和蔼地点头致意。

===Original===
Coffee and liqueurs were brought in. There was talk of future plans. Most of them were to study law and expected to spend the summer in reading up. Enthusiasm rose, and rash promises were made to keep in touch and not forget each other. At one end of the table Gabel and Billfelt swore eternal friendship; at the other Jansson expatiated on his feeling for Moberg. It was only with difficulty that Josef Marin could be restrained from prophesying. When Josef Marin prophesied he would read out long rigmaroles of stuff, marriage announcements from the *Daily News* mixed with bits from Tegnér’s “Svea” and Norbeck’s *Theology*, all recited in the solemn monotone with which he imagined Elisha had chastised Ahab, and Ezekiel foretold the destruction of Israel and Judah. It was one o’clock, getting on towards two, and various members of the party had already said good night and gone off, especially those who seriously meant to read up for law. The crowd was thinning, the electric light had long ago been turned off, only a couple of gas jets were still burning, and the waiters stood with the air of martyrs as they yearned for sleep and pourboire. There was nothing to do but break up.

Outside, the glimmer of dawn had already begun to spread over the streets and squares. It was no longer raining, but the air felt moist and cold and misty, and through the mist the clock-face of Jacobs church shone like a moon in a comic paper.

===Chinese===
咖啡和利口酒端上来了。大家谈起往后的打算。多数人要学法律，打算用一个夏天来啃书。热情高涨起来，人们轻率地许下诺言，要保持联络，永不忘怀。桌子一头，加贝尔同比尔费尔特起誓结下永恒的友谊；另一头，扬松絮絮不休地倾诉他对莫贝里的情谊。约瑟夫·马林呢，好不容易才拦住，没让他发预言。约瑟夫·马林发预言的时候，会朗朗诵出长篇大论的胡话：《每日新闻》（Daily News）上的结婚启事，掺上特格纳尔《斯维娅》里的片断和诺贝克（Norbeck）的《神学》，全用一种庄严的单调腔调念出来——他想象中，以利沙斥责亚哈、以西结预言以色列和犹大覆亡时，用的就是这种腔调。一点钟了，眼看快两点，一行人中已有几位道过晚安先走了，尤其是那些真心打算夏天啃法律书的。人渐渐稀了，电灯早已熄灭，只剩两盏煤气灯还亮着；侍者们站在一旁，一副殉道者的神气，一心盼着睡觉和小费。除了散伙，没有别的办法。

外面，黎明的微光已经开始在街道和广场上铺展。雨已经不下了，可空气又湿又冷，雾气迷蒙；透过雾，雅各布教堂（Jacobs church）的钟面像滑稽画报上的一轮月亮似的亮着。

===Original===
It was hard to separate, and the company walked some distance down along the car tracks past the opera house. Out of Lagerlunden came a group of poets and journalists, and Martin looked at them reverently, wondering whether it would ever be vouchsafed him to become one of them. The student caps gleamed white in the night, whereupon moths came fluttering from right and left, slipping their arms under those of the young men and tempting them with promises of the greatest happiness in life, until amid convivial mirth and harmless joking they arrived at Charles XII’s Square, for Josef Marin had the fixed idea that he must prophesy before Charles XII. But while he was prophesying, Gabel caught the prettiest girl around the waist and began to waltz with her around the statue, Moberg followed and trod a measure with an elderly bacchante, and Martin stood with a pounding heart staring at a pale little piece of mischief with eyes as black as charcoal and wondered if he dared go up to her. But while he was wondering, Planius put an arm around her waist and scampered off, and Martin stood alone and watched them whirl about in the mist, pair after pair. But the morning breeze from the south now began to clear the mist, driving it across the river like white smoke, and the cross on St. Katarina’s cupola burned like the morning star in the first rays of the dawn.

A policeman loomed up from down by the docks and gradually came nearer, one of the girls set up a cry of warning, and the crowd dispersed in all directions. A stout nymph took Martin by the arm and went along with him.

===Chinese===
大家难分难舍，一行人沿着电车轨道往下走了一段，走过歌剧院。拉格尔伦登（Lagerlunden）餐馆里走出一群诗人和记者，马丁满怀敬意地望着他们，心想自己此生是否也有幸厕身其间。学生帽在夜色里泛着白光，于是飞蛾们从左右两厢扑扇而至，把手臂挽进年轻人的臂弯，用人生最大的幸福引诱他们；就这样，在欢洽的笑闹和无伤大雅的玩笑声中，一行人来到了卡尔十二世广场（Charles XII's Square），因为约瑟夫·马林有一个执念：他必须在卡尔十二世面前发一番预言。可正当他预言的时候，加贝尔搂住那个最漂亮的姑娘的腰，围着雕像同她跳起华尔兹来；莫贝里跟着，同一位上了年纪的酒神女祭司踏起舞步；马丁心口怦怦直跳，站在那里呆望着一个脸色苍白的小淘气，她的眼睛黑得像木炭，他盘算着自己敢不敢走上前去。可就在他盘算的当儿，普拉尼乌斯伸出胳膊搂住她的腰，一溜烟跑开了；马丁独自站着，望着一对又一对的人在雾里旋转。可这时，南来的晨风开始驱散雾气，把它像白烟似的赶过河去；圣卡塔琳娜教堂（St. Katarina）圆顶上的十字架，在黎明的最初光线里像启明星一样燃烧。

一个警察从码头那边隐隐现身，渐渐走近。一个姑娘发出一声警报似的尖叫，人群向四面八方散去。一位体态丰腴的小仙子挽住马丁的胳膊，同他一道走。

===Original===
“I must hold your arm, ducky,” she said, “or the cop will pull me in. Besides, you might like to come home with me, eh? I’ve a right nice place, you’ll see. I have a big lovely bed and sheets I embroidered myself. I sit and embroider mornings mostly. One must have some fun for oneself, and I can’t stand playing cards with mamma day out and day in like the other girls, and they swear and carry on and act vulgar. I don’t care about that sort of thing; I like nice agreeable boys like you. If you’re real nice and come to me and come often, I’ll embroider you a nightshirt for a keepsake﻿—Oh, you haven’t any money! The hell, you say; that’s another pair of galoshes! Then you must come again when you have some. Just ask for Hulda. But tell me, is it true there’s a girl at Uppsala that’s called Charles XII?”

“Not that I know,” answered Martin.

“Well, goodbye then﻿ ﻿…”

It was not quite true that Martin had no money; he still had a few crowns left from the honorarium for a poem published in the *Home Friend* and had only made the excuse so as not to hurt Hulda’s feelings.

===Chinese===
"我非得挽着你的胳膊不可，宝贝，"她说，"不然条子会把我抓进去的。再说，你说不定愿意到我家去，嗯？我那地方可好了，你见了就知道。我有一张大得可爱的床，还有我自己绣的床单。我多半白天坐着绣花。人总得给自己找点乐子；我可爱不了别的姑娘那一套，成天成天地陪妈妈打牌，还骂骂咧咧、闹闹哄哄、粗里粗气。那种事我不稀罕；我喜欢你这样和和气气、讨人喜欢的小伙子。你要是真好，肯到我这里来，常来，我就给你绣件睡衣留个纪念——哦，你没钱！真见鬼，你说；那可就是另一码事了！那你有了钱一定得再来。就找胡尔达（Hulda）。可你告诉我，乌普萨拉真有个姑娘叫作卡尔十二世吗？"

"这我没听说过。"马丁答道。

"那么，再见了……"

马丁说没钱并不全是实话；他还有几个克朗，是发表在《家庭之友》（Home Friend）上的一首诗的稿费剩下来的，他那样推托，只是为了不叫胡尔达伤心。

### IX / 九

===Original===

Years passed.

… Martin was roaming about in the twilight. The streets and squares lay white, snow was falling softly and silently. A man went in front of him on a zigzag course lighting a lamp here and a lamp there.

Martin went along without a purpose; he hardly knew where he went.

Suddenly he noticed that he was crying as he walked. He did not clearly know why. He did not ordinarily find it easy to cry. Some snowflakes must have caught in his eyelashes, and his eyes had got wet.﻿ ﻿… He turned off into a side street and came to a bit of park, he brushed past a couple half snowed in on a bench, and proceeded on among the trees, where it was lonely and empty and the branches drooped heavily under the wet snow.

===Chinese===

许多年过去了。

……马丁在暮色里游荡。街道和广场一片白，雪轻轻地、无声地落着。一个人走在他前头，七弯八绕地，在这里点一盏灯，在那里点一盏灯。

马丁漫无目的地走着；他几乎不知道自己走到了哪里。

忽然他发觉，自己一边走一边流着泪。他不很明白是为什么。他平日并不容易哭。想必是雪花粘在了睫毛上，眼睛才湿的。……他拐进一条小街，来到一小片园地，擦过一对在长椅上被雪半掩了的情侣，又往树木深处走去，那里孤寂而空旷，湿雪沉沉地压弯了枝条。

===Original===

… Strange! A hovel in an alley, a smoking lamp. Two naked arms which bent and reached forward to the window, and the sound of curtains coming down. The girl, who was humming the latest popular tune while she slowly and unconcernedly hung up her red bodice﻿—he hummed too so as not to speak aloud﻿—was she pretty or ugly? He did not know, he had hardly set eyes on her. It was not she for whom he longed.

He had sat at home in the dusk, the icy blue dusk of a March afternoon, twisting and turning over an old poem that never would get itself finished. Then all at once he had begun to think of a woman. He had met her at noon as he came from his work, and he had felt the encounter as a sudden intoxication. She was walking in the full sunlight, and many men turned their heads after her as she went. But she seemed to notice or suspect nothing. She was very young﻿—eighteen or possibly twenty. She was neither expensively nor humbly dressed, but she carried her head carelessly and easily, perhaps too a little proudly. Slender and straight, she went on her way, her brown hair shining in the sunlight, and now and then she smiled to herself. He followed her at a distance; she went up to Östermalm and vanished at last in a gateway.

So it was that she had come before his mind again in the twilight, as he sat in his rocking-chair and hunted for rhymes; and she left him no rest﻿—he threw down his pen and went out. There was no longer sunshine; it was snowing. He came to the large gray house where he had seen her go in; he walked to and fro on the pavement directly opposite and saw a window light up here and a window there. Who was she? He remembered he had seen her speak to a man he knew. He went up the steps and read the names on the doors, until at last, deciding that he was childish and stupid, he pulled up his coat collar and went back into the snow. He took by the arm the first girl that gave him a meaningful glance and went home with her.

===Chinese===

……奇怪！小巷里一间破屋，一盏冒着烟的灯。两条赤裸的胳膊弯下去，朝窗口伸过去，接着传来窗帘放下的声响。那姑娘一边哼着最新流行的曲子，一边慢条斯理、满不在乎地挂起她那件红胸衣——他也跟着哼，免得开口说话——她好看还是难看？他不知道，他几乎没有正眼瞧过她。他渴望的并不是她。

他本来坐在家里的暮色里，三月午后那冰蓝色的暮色里，翻来覆去打磨一首怎么也收不了尾的旧诗。忽然间，他想起了一个女人。中午他下班回来时遇见过她，那次相遇于他像一阵突然袭来的醉意。她走在饱满的阳光里，一路许多男人回头看她。她却似乎毫无察觉，也毫无疑心。她很年轻——十八岁，至多二十岁。她穿得既不华贵，也不寒酸，可是她的头抬得随意而轻巧，也许未免还有点高傲。她苗条挺直地走她的路，棕色的头发在阳光里发亮，不时独自微笑。他远远地跟着；她往奥斯特马尔姆（Östermalm）去了，最后消失在一个门洞里。

就这样，暮色里她又浮上他的心头，那时他正坐在摇椅里搜寻韵脚；她不给他安宁——他掷下笔，出了门。外头已经没有阳光；下着雪。他来到那座灰色大楼前，就是他看见她走进去的那座；他在正对着大门的人行道上来回踱步，看这一扇窗亮了，那一扇窗亮了。她是谁？他想起曾见她同一个他认识的男人说话。他走上台阶，去念门上的名牌，直到最后认定自己幼稚又愚蠢，才竖起大衣领子，回到雪里去。他挽住第一个向他递来意味深长的一瞥的姑娘的胳膊，跟她回家去了。

===Original===

Now he was standing there in her room. He stood stiffly and silently surveying her as she took off her clothes and chatted and hummed. He hardly asked himself whether she was pretty. He only knew that she might have been prettier without tempting him more and uglier without tempting him less. She showed the marks of her calling. She was still young, and yet one saw that she had long ago tired of choosing and rejecting among her customers. With the same habitual motions of her hand, the coarse hand of a working girl, she hung up her vulgar bodice for anyone who asked it of her, for lieutenant or clerk, minister of justice or waiter, making no distinction between them unless possibly that in her heart she preferred the waiter, since he was less haughty than the others and understood her better.

Whence did she come? Perhaps from a back yard with an ash barrel and a privy, perhaps from a village in the woods. The latter seemed likelier; there was still something of the wood girl in her eyes. Glad among other glad children, she had run bare-legged on the slopes and picked strawberries. Early her contemporaries had taught her to bite of the forbidden fruit. So she had come to the city and had fared as did many others. It was perhaps not a necessity in itself; she might have become a workman’s wife if she had wanted, but she had decided that their lot was harder and without much thinking had gone the way that was smoothest to her feet. With a little more intelligence and better luck she might also have become a tradesman’s wife, such as goes to the square with her maid and bargains for her boiled beef and horseradish.

“Well,” she said, “aren’t you going to undress?”

He stared at her fixedly, and suddenly had no idea of the whole thing, why he had come and what he wanted of her. He muttered something about not feeling very well, laid several crowns on the dressing-table, and departed. She didn’t get angry, only looked surprised and didn’t throw any taunt down the stairs after him.

===Chinese===

此刻他正站在她的房间里。他僵直地、一声不响地打量她，看她脱衣裳、闲聊、哼曲子。他几乎不问自己她美不美。他只知道，她再美几分，也不会多撩动他一分；再丑几分，也不会少撩动他一分。她的行当在她身上留下了印记。她还年轻，然而看得出，她早已厌了在客人中间挑挑拣拣。她用同一副惯熟的手势——一双做工姑娘的粗手——把那件俗气的胸衣挂起来，挂给随便哪个开口要的人，中尉也好，职员也好，司法大臣也好，堂倌也好，她对他们一视同仁，顶多心里偏爱堂倌一点，因为他不像别的客人那么傲慢，也更懂她。

她是从哪儿来的？也许出自一个堆着灰桶、搭着茅厕的后院，也许出自林中一座村庄。后一种似乎更可信；她眼睛里还留着几分林间姑娘的神气。她曾夹在别的快活孩子中间快活着，光着腿在坡上跑，采草莓。年纪还小，同龄人便教会她去咬禁果。就这样她进了城，际遇同许多别人一样。这本身未必出于必要；她要是愿意，本可以做工人的妻子，但她认定那样命更苦，没多想，便走了那条脚下最省力的路。聪明一点、运气好一点，她说不定还能当上买卖人的太太——就是那种带着女仆上广场、为煮牛肉和辣根讨价还价的太太。

“喂，”她说，“你不脱衣服吗？”

他直勾勾地盯着她，忽然对这整件事没了主意：他为什么来，他要她什么。他咕哝了几句身上不大舒服，把几枚克朗放在梳妆台上，走了。她没有恼，只露出惊讶的样子，也没有冲着他的背影从楼梯上甩下一句奚落话。

===Original===

---

It snowed continuously. Would it never end, this winter? It was now getting on to the end of March, the trees drooped with the snow and it was bitterly cold.﻿ ﻿…

Martin was weary, he sat on a bench under one of the white trees and let the snow deposit itself in drifts on his hat and shoulders.

“What are we doing with life, we mortals?”

The life he led, the pitiful joy he sought and sometimes found, seemed to him at that moment like the fantasy of a madhouse. Nevertheless that life was the normal life. Most of the men he knew lived thus. He was twenty-three. In the four or five years he had been in the game he ought to have got used to it.﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===

---

雪下个不停。这个冬天难道永远不会完？如今已快到三月底，树被雪压得低垂，天冷得刺骨。……

马丁乏了，坐在一棵挂满雪的树下长椅上，任雪在他帽子上、肩头积成一堆堆。

“我们这些凡人，拿生命在做什么？”

他过的这种生活，他追寻而有时竟能寻着的那点可怜的欢乐，此刻在他看来就像疯人院里的幻梦。然而这种生活正是正常的生活。他认识的男人多数都这样过活。他二十三岁。他混迹此道已有四五年，本该习惯了。……

===Original===

No, he didn’t understand humanity nor did he understand himself. He often listened to the talk of his friends and acquaintances about these things. He had noted that the most respectable of the young men, and of the old for that matter, believed in two kinds of love, a pure kind and a sensual kind. Young women of the better sort were to be loved with the pure kind, but that meant betrothal and marriage, and that one could seldom afford. As a rule, therefore, it was only girls of means who could inspire a pure love; outside of that the feeling was more at home in lyric poetry than in reality. The other sort, on the contrary, the sensual, a man might and should possess about once a week. But this side of existence was not considered to have a serious meaning; it was not anything that could render a man happy or unhappy; it was simply comic, the material for funny stories, an equally pleasant and hygienic diversion when one had received his salary and drunk his bottle of punch. But in the intervals the entire sexual life interested but slightly the respectable and decent class of men; they found its functions unbeautiful and disreputable, or, as they otherwise put it, bestial, since they could not exercise them without feeling themselves like beasts.

This was the prevalent opinion throughout the community, and such conditions were explained in that this way of living was the healthiest and wisest, not of course in the sermons of the clergy, the speeches of the politicians, or the leading articles of the newspapers, but in the enlightened judgment between man and man in all circles. It was considered necessary in order that young men might preserve their health and good spirits and that young women of the better classes might preserve their virtue. The young men accordingly drank punch, visited girls of the streets, became fat and florid, and succeeded not only in putting up with this life as with a sort of wretched substitute, but it appealed to them to such a degree that often even after they were married they did not scorn to make excursions to their old haunts, which had become so endeared. The girls of the better class meanwhile were allowed to preserve their virtue and beyond that were not asked for their opinion, but for some of them their precious jewel became at last too heavy to carry.﻿ ﻿…

“What have we done with our life, we mortals?

“Happiness, the joy of youth, whither has it gone? Life is regulated for the old, therefore it is a misfortune to be young. It is regulated for the thoughtless and stupid, for those who take the false for the true or even prefer the false, because it is a disease to think and feel, a childish disease which one must go through before one becomes a man.”﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===

不，他不理解人类，也不理解自己。他常听朋友和熟人谈论这类事情。他注意到，最体面的年轻人——老年人也不例外——信奉两种爱：一种纯洁的，一种肉欲的。上等人家的小姐，应当用纯洁的那种去爱，可那意味着订婚和结婚，而这又是难得负担得起的。所以照例只有有钱的小姐才能唤起纯洁的爱情；出了这个圈子，这种感情在抒情诗里比在现实中更自在。至于另一种，肉欲的那种，一个男人每星期大约可以、而且应该享用一回。可是存在的这一面被认为没有什么严肃的意义；它不是能使人幸福或不幸的东西；它干脆是可笑的，是笑话的素材，是领了薪水、喝过一瓶潘趣酒之后一种既愉快又卫生的消遣。可是在间歇的日子里，整个性生活很难引起这班可敬体面的先生的兴趣；他们觉得那些行径不美、不体面，或者照他们的另一种说法，是兽性的，因为他们行使它的时候，没法不觉得自己像畜生。

这是全社会盛行的意见。这样的状况之所以得到解释，是因为这种活法最健康、最明智——当然不是在牧师的布道里、政客的演说里或报纸的社论里，而是在各个圈子里人与人之间开明的裁断之中。它被认为是必要的：为的是让年轻人保得住健康和好兴致，让上等阶级的年轻女子守得住贞洁。于是年轻人们喝潘趣酒，逛街头的姑娘，长得肥头大耳、红光满面，他们不但把这种生活当作一种寒碜的替代品将就了过去，而且它竟如此投合他们，以致往往成了家，也仍不耻于到那些处出感情的旧巢穴去走动走动。而上等阶级的姑娘们呢，她们被允许保住贞洁，除此之外，没有人来征求她们的意见；只是对她们中间的一些人来说，那件珍贵的珠宝到头来变得太沉，捧不动了。……

“我们这些凡人，把生命做成了什么？

“幸福，青春的欢乐，都到哪儿去了？生活是替老年人规定的，所以年轻是一种不幸。它是替不动脑子的蠢人规定的，替那些把假的当真、甚至偏爱假货的人规定的，因为思考和感受是一种病，一种孩子气的病，人在长成大人之前非生过一场不可。”……

===Original===

The apparition of a woman glided slowly past the bench where he sat, and scarcely had it passed when it stood still, turned its head, and fixed upon him two great dark eyes.

He rose, shook off the snow, and went away.

He walked quickly, for he was cold.

===Chinese===

一个女人影缓缓飘过他所坐的长椅，刚一过去便站住了，回过头来，将两只又大又黑的眼睛定在他身上。

他站起来，抖掉身上的雪，走开了。

他走得很快，因为他冷。

===Original===

He thought about life and books. During his adolescence a new literature had broken forth, which was at war with the prevalent morals of the community and endeavored to change them. Now it had grown silent. Little had been accomplished, almost nothing, and already it was losing its hold. What the new writers had fought for and in behalf of which they had taken and given such hard blows now suddenly belonged to the “ ’Eighties” and as such had once for all been tried and condemned, weighed in the balance and found too heavy. Instead the blue flower of poetry exhaled its perfume around him as never before. Once again the old words rang like new; earth returned to the golden age, the woods and waters were filled afresh with centaurs and nymphs, knights and damsels roamed into the sunset, and Song herself, with eyes wide awake and bright after her long sleep, stood forth again in the midst of the people and chanted as she had not done in a hundred years. Martin loved this poetry, its rhythms and words stole into the verses he himself sat and tinkered with in the dusk, and yet all this was strangely foreign to him. The world was just the same all the while, everything went its usual way, and no victory was won. Was this the time to sing? It was true that, when he looked more closely, he discovered ideas at the bottom of this new poetry also, and these ideas too were in open warfare against current morality. But only a few readers noted this and hardly anyone attached any importance to it. It was just verse.

It was verse, and as a form for ideas poetry was and remained on about the level of the royal opera. There too the baritone might bellow against tyrants without thereby running any risk of missing his Vasa decoration, there too seduction scenes were played by artificial light without anyone’s taking umbrage; what in ordinary life was called by ordinary citizens bestial was conceived of by the same people with regard to *Faust* and *Romeo and Juliet* as poetic and pretty and thoroughly suitable for young girls. It was the same with poetry. Ideas, when woven into verse and beautiful words, were no longer contraband; they were not even noticed.

Would a man never come who did not sing, but spoke, and spoke plainly!

===Chinese===

他想着生活，也想着书。在他的少年时代，一种新文学破土而出，向社会盛行的道德宣战，竭力要改变它。如今它喑哑了。成事寥寥，几乎什么也没有成就，而它的影响已在流失。新作家们为之奋斗、为之挨了也回敬了那么多沉重打击的东西，如今突然归入“八十年代”名下，就此被一劳永逸地审过、定罪，放上天平称过，嫌太重了。取而代之的是，诗的蓝花在他四周吐出芬芳，胜过以往任何时候。古老的词语重新铮铮然，恍如新铸；大地回到了黄金时代，树林和川泽重新住满了半人马和宁芙，骑士和少女漫游着走进落日，而歌吟女神本人在长眠之后，睁着一双久睡方醒、清明透亮的眼睛，重新站到人们中间歌唱，唱得一百年来不曾这样唱过。马丁爱这种诗，它的节奏和字句悄悄溜进他自己黄昏里坐着修补的那些诗行，然而这一切于他又有一种奇异的隔膜。世界始终是老样子，一切照老路运转，什么胜利也没有赢得。这是歌唱的时候吗？不错，他细看之下，发现这种新诗的底子里也有思想，这些思想同样在同当前的道德公开交战。可是只有寥寥几个读者注意到这一点，而且几乎没有谁把它当回事。它不过是诗罢了。

它不过是诗，而作为思想的形式，诗歌从前和现在都差不多停在皇家歌剧院的水准上。在那里，男中音尽可以对着暴君吼叫，而不必冒一点错过瓦萨勋章的风险；在那里，诱拐的场面在人工灯光下搬演，也没有谁见怪；日常生活中被普通市民称作兽性的东西，同样这批人，面对《浮士德》（Faust）和《罗密欧与朱丽叶》（Romeo and Juliet），却看成是有诗意的、美妙的、完全适合年轻小姐的。诗也是一样。思想一旦织进诗句和漂亮的辞章，就不再是违禁品；它甚至根本不会被人看见。

难道永远不会来一个不唱、而是说话、并且说得明明白白的人吗！

===Original===

---

He had come out on Strand Avenue. The ice on Nybro Inlet had just been broken, a tug was now forcing its painful way along between the cakes of ice. To the left several newly built millionaire barracks towered up in the snowy mist, in one of which the electric lights and polished glass prisms already gleamed from a long suite of rooms, and in a large hall a white shimmering maze of dancing couples moved behind the muslin curtains.

Several lonely wanderers had paused in a group as if rooted to stare at the paradise above them. Martin also stopped a minute and proceeded with his thoughts. Several measures of the waltz had reached his ears; it was the “Blue Danube”; he walked on humming it and couldn’t get it out of his head.

O Eros, Eros! The harlot’s room and the festal hall up there.﻿ ﻿… In both temples the same god was worshiped, and in both temples he was worshiped by the same men. But the women!

===Chinese===

---

他走到了斯特朗瓦根（Strand Avenue）。尼布罗湾（Nybro Inlet）上的冰刚破开，一艘拖船正在浮冰中间艰难地挤出一条路来。左边，几座新盖的百万富翁营房在雪雾里高高耸起，其中一座的一长排房间里已经亮起电灯，磨光的玻璃棱镜闪闪发亮；一间大厅里，薄纱窗帘后面转动着一座白晃晃的、由一对对舞侣组成的迷宫。

几个孤身的游荡者聚作一小堆，像生了根似的停在那里，仰望头顶上这座天堂。马丁也停了片刻，又接着想下去。几小节圆舞曲传进他耳朵；是《蓝色多瑙河》；他一路哼着走下去，怎么也甩不掉它。

哦，厄洛斯，厄洛斯！妓女的房间，和上头那节日的厅堂。……两座庙里供奉的是同一位神，而且两座庙里礼拜他的也是同一批男人。可是那些女人呢！

===Original===

He did not dance, and yet he loved balls. He enjoyed standing in a doorway and watching the others whirl by. What atmosphere was there around all their festivals of youth which fascinated him and made him meditative and sick with longing after the impossible? Look at the women! Held close in the arms of the men, with eyes half-shut and mouths open, the most innocent young girls flitted past in dresses which exposed or emphasized their young panting bosoms. What were they thinking of, what were they dreaming of? There were some no doubt who thought of nothing, dreamed of nonsense, and had no other longing than to stir their legs and keep in motion, regular young girls after the hearts of their mothers and aunts. But they were surely not all so. The daughters of men could not have changed so extraordinarily since the not too remote times when youths and maidens carried phallic images in procession, singing holy songs. What did they talk about, these young girls, when they sat together and whispered in a corner? “She is secretly engaged to him”; “He’s in love with her, but she’s fond of someone else.” What was in the books they read? The same thing: People who were in love with each other, and how it turned out, and who got whom. To “get,” what did that mean? That one found out on the bridal night.

But the years passed, and the bridal might have to wait. The young girl got to be twenty-five, she was nearly thirty, and still she danced at balls with half-closed eyes, but her mouth was no longer open; she now knew that this looked unseemly, so she held it convulsively shut, a bloodred streak. Would it never come, the great, the wonderful experience? Her glance was that of a drowning woman. “Save me, I’m sinking, I’m going under! Youth is so short. Look! my color is already fading, my bosom is sinking in, and my young flower is withering!” She tried being provocative and bold, she was afraid she had been too timid before, perhaps that was not the right way.﻿ ﻿… But the gentlemen were already laughing at her covertly when they drank healths over their punch, and some of them mocked her in public. Others understood her better and thought within themselves that she might make a good wife and an ardent mistress. But they had no desire to marry, and to seduce a girl of family would be a risky business. When they left the ball they could easily and without any ado find the way to their old place, to the room with the smoking lamp, or with a red night-lamp hanging from the ceiling.

“What are we doing with our lives, we men, and what are we doing with *theirs*?”

===Chinese===

他不跳舞，却热爱舞会。他喜欢站在门口，看别人一团团旋过去。他们青春的这一切节日四周，笼罩着怎样一种气氛，引得他出神，害得他为那不可能的东西害起相思的病？瞧那些女人！被男人们紧紧搂在怀里，眼睛半阖，嘴巴张开，最天真的年轻姑娘们穿着或是暴露、或是突出那年轻的、起伏的胸脯的衣裳，一个个飘然而过。她们在想什么，她们在梦什么？无疑有几个什么也不想，净梦些没要紧的，除了动动两条腿、不停地转下去再无别的渴望——正是妈妈们姨妈们心里那种正牌的好姑娘。可她们断不会全是如此。人类的女儿们，总不至于从那并不太久远的时代——那时少男少女们抬着男根偶像游行、唱着圣歌——到如今，就变得这般面目全非。这些年轻姑娘，她们坐在角落里凑在一起咬耳朵的时候，都谈些什么？“她同他私订了终身”；“他爱她，她心里却另有别人。”她们读的书里写的是什么？还是那一套：人们彼此相爱，结果如何，谁得到了谁。“得到”，这是什么意思？这个，要到新婚之夜才见分晓。

可是一年年过去，婚礼也许还得等。年轻姑娘到了二十五，快三十了，仍旧半阖着眼在舞会上转，只是嘴不再张开了；她如今知道那样看着不雅，便死死抿住它，抿成一道血红的细线。那伟大的、美妙的经历，难道永远不会来了吗？她的眼色，是一个溺水女人的眼色。“救救我，我在往下沉，我要沉下去了！青春这么短。看！我的脸色已经在褪，我的胸脯在塌陷，我年轻的花儿在枯萎！”她试着变得挑逗、大胆，她怕自己从前太畏缩了，也许那样不对。……可是先生们喝着潘趣酒互相祝饮时，已经在暗地里笑她，有几个还当众讥嘲她。另一些人更了解她，心里想她也许会是个好妻子、一个热烈的情妇。可是他们不想结婚，而勾引有门第的姑娘是桩冒险的营生。离开舞会之后，他们可以轻而易举、不声不响地摸回老地方，回到那间点着冒烟油灯的、或者天花板上挂一盏红灯的房间。

“我们这些男人，拿自己的生命在做什么，又拿*她们*的做什么？”

===Original===

---

Martin turned back into the city.

On a street corner he met a poet, who was freezing in a thin yellow-green ulster. He was a few years older than Martin and already a bit famous, for he wrote with fabulous ease the loveliest verses on any theme, mostly about girls and flowers and June nights on the lowlands of Scania, whence he had come. He had a pale face and a thin red beard; and when he met a fellow-artist, his great childlike eyes took on a wild and staring expression, as if he were considering within himself: “Shall I murder him, or shall we go in somewhere and consume alcohol?”

They went up to the “Anglais” and drank green chartreuse.

The poet talked about himself. He confided to Martin that he was a decadent. He worshiped everything that was disintegrating, rotten at the core, and doomed to destruction. He hated the sun and light﻿—here he shook a clenched fist at the gas candelabra on the ceiling﻿—he loved the night and sin and all alcoholic drinks of a green shade. He had most of the well-known venereal diseases and an insane fear of crowded squares. Nothing in the world could make him go diagonally across Gustavus Adolphus Place. This disease gave him a very special pleasure, for he took it as the forerunner of general paralysis. And general paralysis was the great sleep; it was nirvana.

Martin listened absently. “Light is good,” he said to himself, “and darkness is good too. But sometimes darkness is bad, and light too.”

===Chinese===

---

马丁折回城里。

在一个街角，他遇到一位诗人，那人正裹着一件黄绿色的薄大衣冻得发抖。他比马丁大几岁，已经小有名气，因为他写诗有神乎其神的容易，随便什么题目都能写出最可爱的诗行，多半写姑娘、写花、写他故乡斯科讷（Scania）平原上的六月之夜。他脸色苍白，留着稀疏的红胡子；遇到同行的时候，他那双孩子气的大眼睛便现出一种狂乱的、直勾勾的神情，仿佛心里在盘算：“我该宰了他呢，还是同他进哪家馆子消耗点酒精？”

他们上了“英国人”（Anglais）咖啡馆，喝绿色的查特酒。

诗人谈他自己。他向马丁吐露，他是个颓废派。他崇拜一切正在解体、核心溃烂、注定毁灭的东西。他仇恨太阳和光——说到这里，他朝天花板的煤气烛台挥了挥攥紧的拳头——他爱黑夜、罪恶，以及一切带绿色调的酒。知名的性病他大半都得过，还对拥挤的广场怀有一种疯狂的恐惧。世上无论什么都不能诱使他斜穿过古斯塔夫·阿道尔夫广场（Gustavus Adolphus Place）。这种病给了他一种特别的快乐，因为他把它当作全身麻痹症的先声。而全身麻痹症就是那场大睡；那就是涅槃。

马丁心不在焉地听着。“光是好东西，”他自言自语，“暗也是好东西。不过有时候，暗是坏的，光也是。”

===Original===

“But how is it,” he asked, “that your poems are really not in any essential way different from those which generally get the prize in the Academy?”

At these words the poet’s glance darkened, his lips suddenly became thin and narrow. He took a dirty sheath-knife from his pocket, pulled it halfway out, and laid his index finger on the bare blade.

“How deep can you stand cold steel?” he asked.

“You misunderstand me entirely,” said Martin, laying his hand calmingly on the other’s arm. “I love your poems. Only I don’t see rightly the connection between them and your inner life as you have just pictured it.”

The poet laughed.

“It’s amusing to hear that you love my poems,” he said. “The things I’ve allowed to be published up to now, you see, are mere skits. Good enough for the mob. Look here!”﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===

“可是，”他问，“你的诗，同那些通常在瑞典文学院得奖的诗，实际上竟没有什么本质的不同，这是怎么回事？”

听了这话，诗人的目光阴沉下来，嘴唇忽然变得又薄又窄。他从口袋里掏出一把脏兮兮的鞘刀，抽出一半，把食指搁在裸着的刀刃上。

“冰冷的钢，你能受得了多深？”他问。

“你完全误会我了，”马丁说，把一只手安抚地放在对方胳膊上。“我爱你的诗。只是我不大看得清，你的诗同你刚才描绘的那种内心生活之间，有什么联系。”

诗人笑了。

“听说你爱我的诗，倒有意思，”他说。“要知道，到目前为止我容许发表的那些东西，不过是些游戏笔墨。给乌合之众看看尽够了。瞧这个！”……

===Original===

He took a newspaper clipping from his pocket, a review of his last volume signed by a well-known critic. This authority mildly deplored that some of the poems could not be acquitted of a certain tinge of sensualism which gave an unpleasing effect. In others again the poet struck purer tones, such as were fitted to give rich promise for the future.

“Well, that was quite friendly,” observed Martin, when he had read it.

“Friendly!” The poet again made a convulsive grab in his pocket where the knife lay. “Friendly, you say? Shouldn’t such an insect creep in the dust before the wretchedest of my poems?”

“Oh, yes,” said Martin, “yes, naturally; but since it isn’t the custom for older folks with younger﻿—”

The poet was silent, took a drink, then was silent a long while.

===Chinese===

他从口袋里掏出一幅剪报，是一位知名批评家评他上一卷诗集的文章。这位权威温和地表示惋惜：有几首诗难辞一种肉欲的色彩，效果令人不快。而在另一些诗里，诗人弹出了较为纯粹的调子，足以为未来许下丰盛的诺言。

“唔，这算很客气的了，”马丁读完说。

“客气！”诗人又朝口袋里刀待着的地方猛地抓了一把。“你说客气？这样一个虫豸，难道不该趴在我最蹩脚的一首诗面前的尘土里爬吗？”

“哦，是，”马丁说，“是的，自然该爬；不过年长者对年轻人这样爬，毕竟不合惯例——”

诗人不作声了，喝了一口，随后又沉默了许久。

===Original===

Martin drank too. The strong green liquor burned in his palate and his brain. Thereupon the woman of the morning was there, the one who walked in the sunlight and smiled. Was she asleep now, did she dream, did she smile in her dreams? Or did she twist about sleepless on her bed in longing for a man?

Should he write to her? He could easily find out her name. No. She would only show the letter to her friends, and they would titter and laugh.﻿ ﻿…

The café was nearly empty. In the farthest corner a regular customer sat alone behind a newspaper. In a mirror on the opposite wall was the vision of an old gentleman with white whiskers and a red silk handkerchief sticking out of his breast-pocket. He was fat and red and white, red by nature and white with powder, and as he leaned his chest and arms against the bar, he looked like a sphinx.

The poet emitted a sigh. Martin studied him: the face of a child under the red-bearded mask of a pirate. It occurred to him that he had possibly hurt this man’s feelings just now, and he felt the need of saying something agreeable.

===Chinese===

马丁也喝了。烈性的绿色酒液在他的上颚和脑子里燃烧。随即，上午的那个女人来了，那个走在阳光里、微笑着的女人。她这会儿睡了吗？她做梦吗？她在梦里微笑吗？还是正渴念着一个男人，在床上翻来覆去睡不着？

他该给她写信吗？他不难打听出她的名字。不。她只会把信拿给她的女友们看，她们会嘻嘻地笑个不住。……

咖啡馆几乎空了。最远的角落里，一位常客独自躲在报纸后面。对面墙上的镜子里，映出一位老绅士的身影：白色的颊须，胸前口袋里露出一方红绸手帕。他红红白白地胖着——红是天生红，白是扑粉白——当他把胸脯和胳膊倚在柜台上时，看上去像一尊斯芬克斯。

诗人发出一声叹息。马丁打量他：一副海盗的红胡子假面底下，一张孩子的脸。他忽然想到，方才也许伤了此人的心，便觉得有必要说句中听的话。

===Original===

“Do you know,” he said, “if you shaved off your beard you would certainly look like the most profligate kind of monk?”

The poet brightened up.

“I dare say you’re right,” he said, trying to get a look at himself in a mirror. “What’s more, I’ve written poems with a leaning toward Catholicism. You ought to read my poems sometime, the real ones, the ones that can’t be printed.”

“Surely,” said Martin. “Where do you live?”

The poet declared that he didn’t live anywhere. He hadn’t had any dwelling-place for three weeks, and he didn’t need any. He wrote his poems on the table of the café and slept with girls. In the house of one of them he had his green-edged traveling bag with some extra collars and the poems of Verlaine, and there too were his own manuscripts.

===Chinese===

“你知道吗，”他说，“你要是把胡子剃掉，保管像极了那种最放荡的修道士？”

诗人高兴起来。

“我看你说得对，”他说，一边设法在镜子里照一照自己。“而且，我还写过偏向天主教的诗呢。你改天真该读读我的诗，真的那些，印不出来的那些。”

“一定，”马丁说。“你住在哪儿？”

诗人声称他哪儿也不住。他已有三个星期没有任何住所，而且也用不着。他在咖啡馆的桌子上写诗，同姑娘们睡。在其中一个姑娘的住处，放着他的那只绿边旅行包，里面有几件备用领子和一册魏尔伦（Verlaine）的诗，他自己的手稿也在那里。

===Original===

Martin began to be really impressed, but he found no outlet for his thoughts, and silence once more spread itself between the two whom chance had driven together on a street corner.

The clock struck twelve, the gas was turned half down, and the poet, feeling the approach of inspiration with the darkness, began to write verses on the table.

Martin said good night.

---

Sture Square lay white and empty. The snow had ceased, the moon was up, and it was more bitterly cold than ever. To the east a new street without houses opened like a great hole in a wall. To the west a snow-covered jumble of old shanties and stone gables was spread out in the misty moonlight, and from one of the streets of sin which slunk between them echoed a woman’s laugh and the sound of a gate being opened and shut.

===Chinese===

马丁开始当真肃然起敬了，可是他寻不出一句可以出口的话，沉默又一次在两个被偶然赶到同一个街角的人中间铺展开来。

钟敲了十二下，煤气灯拧暗了一半，诗人觉出灵感正随着黑暗逼近，开始在桌子上写起诗来。

马丁道了晚安。

---

斯图雷广场（Sture Square）白茫茫、空荡荡的。雪停了，月亮上来了，天气比先前更冷得刺骨。东边，一条两旁还没有房屋的新街张开，像墙上凿出的一个大窟窿。西边，一堆覆雪的旧棚屋和石头山墙在朦胧的月色里铺展开去，从它们中间潜行的几条罪恶街巷里，传来一个女人的笑声，和一扇门开了又关上的声响。

### V / 五

===Original===

Martin lay awake a long time, unable to sleep. It was the little pale girl with the black eyes that left him no rest. She had stood there so pale and still and lonely; she had not taken anyone’s arm or laughed or chattered like the others. She had surely been seduced and deserted; she perhaps had a little child that would freeze or starve to death if she didn’t get it food and clothes by selling her body. How he would kiss her if he had her in his arms now, how he would caress her and give her the tenderest names, so as to make her forget who she was, a common streetwalker, and who he was, a chance customer like all the rest! With whom was she now? With Planius, maybe. What could Planius be to her? He was no better looking than Martin and he was as stupid as a codfish. He had been one of the worst grinds and had only had a plain “graduated” on his certificate. Why should she pick out just him? But she, to be sure, had made no choice; she had just taken the first that came along. Martin understood this and found it quite natural. She had given away her heart and soul and had no longer anything to give but her body, so why should she deny that to anyone when it was her profession to sell it and when she had already got as deep in the mire as a human being can get? Yet still, if Martin could meet her and she could get to know him, perhaps she might become fond of him and begin a new life. For her he would give up everything﻿—all his dreams of poetic fame and his future; he would choose some profession in which he could immediately earn her and his upkeep; they would be married and live far away from men in a little house by a lake deep in the woods. They would row among the rushes in a little boat and dream away the hours, they would land on an island and be together there all night, while the stars burned above their heads. He would kiss away all sorrow, all dark memories from her brow, and would be as fond of her little child as if it was his own.﻿ ﻿…

But while Martin let his fancy wander thus, he knew quite clearly at the same time that under all these reveries lay nothing but desire﻿—a young man’s hunger for a woman’s white body. And the further on into the night this lasted, while he lay awake and stared at the gray dawn light trickling in through the blinds, the more bitterly he regretted that he had said no to the other girl, the fat one.

===Chinese===

马丁醒了很久，怎么也睡不着。让他不得安宁的，是那个黑眼睛的苍白小姑娘。她当时站在那里，那样苍白、那样安静、那样孤单；她没有像别的姑娘那样挽住谁的手臂，没有笑，也没有叽叽喳喳。她准是受了勾引、又遭了遗弃；说不定还带着一个小孩子，她若不卖身子给孩子挣来吃穿，那孩子不是冻死就是饿死。这会儿她若在他怀里，他会怎样地吻她，怎样地爱抚她，唤她最温柔的名字，好叫她忘掉自己是谁——一个平平常常的街头娼妓——也叫她忘掉他是谁——一个同其余所有人一样偶然上门的客人！此刻她在谁的身边？兴许在普拉尼乌斯（Planius）身边。普拉尼乌斯于她又能算得上什么呢？他生得不比马丁体面，人又蠢得像条鳕鱼。他从前念书，是数一数二的死用功，毕业证书上却只落了个普普通通的“及格”。她凭什么偏偏挑中他？不过，说实在的，她也谈不上挑；碰上谁就是谁罢了。马丁懂得这个，而且觉得这再自然不过。她的心、她的魂早已经给了出去，除了身体再没有什么可给的了；既然卖身就是她的营生，既然她已陷进人所能陷的最深的泥沼，那她凭什么还要拒绝谁呢？然而毕竟，要是马丁能遇见她，她也能慢慢认识他，说不定她会喜欢上他，开始一种新的生活。为了她，他甘愿舍弃一切——他对诗名的全部梦想、他的整个前程；他会选一门立刻就能养活她和自己的职业；他们要结为夫妻，远离人群，住到密林深处一座湖边的小屋里。他们要划着小船在芦苇丛间穿行，把时光消磨在梦里；他们要登上一座小岛，在那里相守一整夜，头顶上星辰燃烧。他要吻去她眉宇间的一切哀愁、一切阴暗的记忆，还要疼爱她的小孩，如同疼爱自己的孩子一样。……

然而马丁一面任凭幻想这样游荡，一面又清清楚楚地知道：这一切遐想的底下，躺着的东西无非是欲望——一个年轻男人对女人白皙肉体的饥渴。夜越往深里去——他睁着眼躺在那儿，盯着从百叶窗缝里涓涓渗进来的灰白晨光——他就越是痛悔，悔自己竟回绝了另一个姑娘，那个胖胖的姑娘。

### VI / 六

===Original===

When one asks a young man who has just passed his school examinations, “What do you intend to be?” he cannot answer, “A poet.” People would turn away their heads and put their hands over their mouths. He may answer, a lawyer or a painter or a musician, for a man can train himself for all these fields at some public institution, and even in one’s apprenticeship one has a modest place in the community, a profession to follow, one already *is* something; a student at the university, or a pupil in the art school or the conservatory. It is not much, but still it is always a sop to throw to indiscreet questioners, and a conceivable future to point to in the case of these more kindly disposed. But he who is to become a poet is nothing but a mockery before God and man until he is recognized and famous. He must therefore during all his long prentice years hang a false sign over his door and pretend to be busy at something that people consider respectable.

===Chinese===

有人问起一个刚通过毕业考试的年轻人：“你打算做个什么人？”他可不能回答：“当诗人。”人们会扭过头去，抬手掩住嘴。他尽可以回答：律师、画家，或者音乐家，因为这些行当都自有公立的机构可供修习；而且还在学徒期，人在社会上就已经有了一个小小的位置，有一门职业可循，就已经*是*个什么人了——是个大学生，或者是美术学校或音乐学院的学生。这算不得什么，但终究是一块可以抛给冒失问话者的甜头；对那些心肠较好的人，也总还有一个想得出来、可以指给他们看的未来。而那个要当诗人的人，在得到承认、出了名之前，在上帝和世人面前就只是个笑柄。因此，在他漫长的学徒岁月里，他只得在自家门上挂一块假招牌，装作在忙一件世人觉得体面的营生。

===Original===

This Martin realized, he found it perfectly natural and not to be altered, and so when his father asked him what he was to be, he answered not that he meant to become a poet but that he should like to work as an extra in a government office. His father was pleased with this answer, perceiving in it a sign that his son would be as sensible and happy as himself. He had feared that Martin might want to go to Uppsala and study aesthetics and he felt within himself that he could not have refused, but he trembled at all the outlay and trouble there would be for a poor father of a family to keep a son at the university. He was therefore delighted with the reply and had nothing to remark except that Martin ought to try to enter not one office but as many as possible. That evening he invited his son to go to Blanch’s café to hear the music and drink toddy.

But the very next day he put the affair in motion, speaking with his acquaintances in various departments and helping Martin to write applications.

===Chinese===

这些道理马丁都明白，他觉得这再自然不过，也无可更改。于是当父亲问他将来要做什么时，他回答的不是想当诗人，而是愿意去政府机关里当个编外职员。父亲听了这个回答很满意，从中看出了一个征兆：儿子会像他自己一样明白事理、一样快活。他原先一直担心马丁会想去乌普萨拉（Uppsala）学美学，他心里也清楚，真到那一步他是拒绝不了的；可一想到一个拖家带口的穷父亲要供一个儿子上大学，那一切的花费和操劳，他就不由得发抖。因此他对这个答复满心欢喜，只有一点要说：马丁该设法进的不能只是一个机关，而是越多越好。当天晚上，他便邀儿子去布朗什咖啡馆（Blanch's café）听音乐、喝热甜酒。

可就在第二天，他便把这件事张罗了起来，去同他在各个部门里的熟人谈话，又帮着马丁写申请书。

### VII / 七

===Original===

Martin had to attend upon the chief of the bureau to which he most desired to submit his services at eight o’clock in the morning in a frock coat and white necktie. Cold and hungry, for he had not had time to eat, he went up the steps of a quiet house in a fashionable street and rang at the door of the general director. An attendant in gold braid announced him and opened the door of a dark private room with curtains only half up. Various articles of dress lay scattered about here and there on the chairs, a great green laticlave hung on the mirror, and at the threshold stood a chamberpot, which he nearly tripped over but checked himself in time and stood there making an awkward bow. In the middle of the room stood a venerable old man in a purple-red satin dressing-gown, gesticulating with a razor, his chin covered with lather. Then out of the red satin and the white lather proceeded a voice, which said: “You have a fine student certificate, young gentleman, but don’t forget that honesty and diligence are and will continue to be the highest requisites in government service. You are accepted and may report tomorrow to begin your duties, if there is anything to do. Above everything, be honest! Goodbye.”

===Chinese===

早晨八点钟，马丁得穿上长礼服、系上白领结，前去晋见他最想投身效力的那个局的局长。他来不及吃饭，又冷又饿，走上一条时髦街道上一座清静宅子的台阶，按响了总局长的门铃。一个缀着金绦的听差替他通报，随即推开一间昏暗内室的门——窗帘只卷起了一半。椅子上东一件西一件地散落着各式衣物，镜子上挂着一条宽大的绿色绶带，门槛边搁着一只夜壶；他险些绊在上面，好在及时收住了脚，站在那里局促地鞠了一躬。房间正中立着一位威严可敬的老先生，身穿紫红色缎面晨衣，下巴上满是皂沫，手里挥着一把剃刀打手势。于是，从那红缎子和白皂沫里发出一个声音，说道：“你这份升学证书很漂亮，年轻的先生。不过别忘了，诚实和勤勉是、而且将永远是政府公务中最高的必备品质。你被录用了，明天就可以来报到就职——要是真有什么事可做的话。比一切都更要紧的，是要诚实！再见。”

===Original===

Martin assumed that this discourteous injunction was in accord with ancient custom and refused to be daunted. He went to the office of the department, where he was given a place at a table and a thick ledger to inspect. He added up column after column. If the figures came out right, his duty was to put ticks in the margin; if they did not, he was to make notes of the fact. But they always did come out right. Martin gradually came to the conviction that there were never any mistakes in these accounts, and when this conviction became rooted in him, he gave up adding entirely and merely put in ticks. Sometimes he looked up from his real or pretended work and listened to the buzzing of the flies or the rain plashing on the windowpanes, or to the conversation and grumbling of the older men, or to a blind man playing a flute in the yard.

And he said to himself, “So this is life.”

===Chinese===

马丁揣想，这道不客气的训示想必是遵着古老的官场惯例，便偏不肯被它吓倒。他到部里的办公处去，人家给了他一张桌旁的位子和一本厚厚的账簿供他核查。他一栏接一栏地加算。数字若是对得上，他的本分就是在页边打个小勾；若是对不上，就该把这一情况记下来。可它们偏偏回回都对。马丁渐渐确信，这些账目是从来不出错的；等这个确信在心里扎下了根，他便索性不再加算，只管打勾了。有时，他从那或真或假的工作中抬起头来，听苍蝇嗡嗡，听雨点噼啪打在窗玻璃上，听年长的职员们闲谈、发牢骚，或是听院子里一个盲人吹笛。

他对自己说：“原来这就是生活。”

### VIII / 八

===Original===

But for Martin this was not life. For him it was a retreat, an asylum in which he had sought repose for a time, which he hoped to make short.

He read and thought. In books and in his own thoughts he searched for what one so often seeks in youth in order to forget in age that one has ever bothered about it: a faith to live by, a star to steer by, a concord in things, a meaning, and a goal.

===Chinese===

但对马丁来说，这并不是生活。对他而言，这是一处退居之地，一座他暂且在其中歇脚的庇护所，他希望能把这段时日过得短暂。

他读书，思索。在书本里，在自己的思绪里，他寻找那种人在青年时代常常去寻找、到老来又忘掉自己曾为它费过心的东西：一个可以安身立命的信仰，一颗可以指引航向的星辰，一种万物之间的和谐，一个意义，一个目标。

===Original===

---

===Chinese===

---

===Original===

Martin had been a Christian up to his sixteenth year. It is natural for a child to believe what his elders say is true. He had believed everything and had not doubted, and on Sundays he had gone to church with his parents. If the preacher was a good talker and a charlatan, he felt edified and moved and wished he could become such a preacher; but if it was an honest unassuming minister who preached as well as he could without making any fuss or gesticulations, he generally went to sleep.

But when he was sixteen he was confirmed. Up to then religion had been a detail of school work set side by side with other details; now it became all of a sudden the one essential, that which daily demanded his time and consideration. The question could not be appeased by the thought: “This is just a matter of the emotions,” since it was customary to weep when one “went forward.” It freely developed the claim to be the highest of all, the dominant force in life, the one thing that mattered. And Martin could not escape the discovery that if religion was the truth, then it was right in this claim, the claim to be above everything else, and he must devote all his powers and his whole soul to it; he must become religious. But if it was not the truth, then he must seek the truth wherever he could find it; he must become a freethinker. The course between, the Christianity of use and custom such as is professed and believed in by the multitude, was to him mere thoughtlessness and conventionality. This was an evasion which seemed natural to him in most of his comrades, but it never occurred to him to think that this was open to him. He stood at the parting of the ways and had to choose.

===Chinese===

直到十六岁为止，马丁一直是个基督徒。孩子相信长辈所说的话是真的，这是自然而然的事。他从前什么都信，从不怀疑，礼拜天跟着父母上教堂。倘若讲道的牧师是个能说会道的江湖派，他便觉得受了启发、动了感情，巴不得自己日后也做这样的牧师；可若换了一位朴实诚恳的牧师，不声不响、不比不画地尽其所能讲道，他倒多半要睡着。

到了十六岁，他行了坚振礼。在那以前，宗教不过是学校功课里与别的细目并列的一个细目；如今它忽然成了唯一的大事，天天索讨着他的时间与思虑。这个问题没法用一句“这不过是感情上的事”来打发，因为人“走上圣坛前”照例是要哭的。它公然提出要求，要居于一切之上，要做人生的主宰力量，要做唯一要紧的事。而马丁不能不发现：倘若宗教是真理，那它提出这个要求便没有错——要求凌驾于一切之上——他就必须把自己全部的力量和整个灵魂奉献给它，就必须成为一个虔诚的信徒；倘若它不是真理，那他就必须无论何处能找到真理都去寻找，就必须成为一个自由思想家。中间的道路，即众人所信奉所持守的习惯性的基督教，在他看来只是无心肝与随大流。这种规避之计在大多数同伴看来是自然的，可他从未想到这条路也向他敞开着。他站在岔路口，必须做出选择。

===Original===

But one night when he lay awake pondering over this, unable to sleep, while the moon shone straight into his room and the thoughts crowded into his head, suddenly it stood clear to him that he did not believe. It seemed to him that he had long realized the Christian religion was something that no one could really believe if he wished to be honest with himself. It became evident to him that the problem as to the truth of Christianity was something which he had already gone past and that it was actually a quite different problem which now disturbed him: how was it possible that the others could believe in this when he could not? By “the others” he meant not only his comrades﻿—for they did not seem to concern themselves any further in such matters, and he knew besides that one could get them to believe in a little of everything﻿—but his parents, his teachers, all the grownups, who must know more of life and the world than he did. How was it possible that he, Martin Birck, who wasn’t sixteen yet and lay in a little iron bed in the home of his parents, could think differently about the highest and most important things than did old and experienced people, and how could he be right and they wrong? This seemed to him almost as wildly absurd as the faith he had just rejected. Here he was completely at a loss; he couldn’t come to any solution. He got up out of bed and went to the window. Snow was glittering white on the roofs, it was dark in the houses, and the street lay empty. The moon stood high in the heavens, but it was a gray-white winter moon, small and frostbitten and infinitely far away, and in the moon-haze the stars twinkled sleepily and dully. Martin stood tracing with his finger on the pane. “Give me a sign, God!” he whispered. Then he stood long at the window, getting chilly and staring at the moon; he saw it glide in and become hidden behind a black factory chimney and he saw it creep out again on the other side. But he received no sign.

In the depths of his heart he did not wish for a sign either, for he felt that a conviction was something that one could not and should not have as a gift by means of a miracle. To seek for truth and be honest with oneself in the search, that was the one clue he could find.

Martin supposed that confirmation and the first communion were duties prescribed by law which he could not evade. His father had no different conception, or if he had he did not say so, for he reverenced the proverb: Speech is silver, silence is golden. Martin therefore went to communion with the other neophytes. It was a spring day with sun and tender green in the old trees of the churchyard, and when Martin heard the bells roar and sing and the organ begin the processional hymn, his eyes filled with tears and he grieved in his heart that he was not as the others and could not believe and feel as they did. And when he saw the church full of serious folk and heard the voice of the preacher enjoining the young people from the pulpit to hold fast to the faith of their fathers, he felt unrest and confusion through his inmost soul, and again the question came to trouble him: “How is it possible that all these can believe, and not I? It’s mad to think that I alone can be right against all these and against all the dead who sleep in their graves out there, who lived and died in the faith I reject. It’s mad, it’s mad! I must conquer my reason and teach myself to believe.” But when he came to the actual ceremonies and saw the ministers in their surplices going back and forth before the altar, while they dispensed the bread and wine and carried napkins over their arms like waiters, he felt faint and disgusted and could not understand that he had let himself be fooled into such mummery. And although he knew or believed that these ministers who shuffled about there in the gloom were in everyday life about as honest as most people, they seemed to him at that moment shameless hypocrites.

===Chinese===

可是有一天夜里，他躺在床上睡不着，翻来覆去想着这件事，月光径直照进他的房间，种种念头挤进他的脑海，忽然间他清清楚楚地明白了：他并不信。他仿佛觉得，自己早就明白基督教是这样一种东西——一个人若想对自己诚实，便无法真正相信它。他看清楚了，基督教之真假这个问题，其实早已被他抛在身后，如今真正困扰他的全然是另一个问题：怎么可能别人能信这个，而他却不能？他所说的“别人”，不仅指他的同学们——他们似乎并不在这些事情上多费心思，况且他也知道，让他们信点什么都能成——而是指他的父母、他的老师、所有的大人，这些人对生活和世界总该比他知道得多。他，马丁·比尔克，一个还不满十六岁、躺在父母家中一张小铁床上的孩子，怎么可能在对最高、最重要的事情的想法上，同年老而有阅历的人们不同呢？他又怎么可能对，而他们都错？这在他看来，几乎同他刚刚抛弃的信仰一样荒唐透顶。到这里他全然没了主意，得不出任何解答。他下了床，走到窗前。屋顶上积雪闪着白光，家家户户黑着，街上空空荡荡。月亮高悬在天上，但那是一轮灰白的冬月，小小的，冻僵了似的，无限地遥远，月光迷蒙之中，星星困倦而黯淡地眨着眼。马丁站在窗前，用手指在玻璃上描画。“给我一个征兆吧，上帝！”他低声说。随后他在窗前站了很久，身上发冷，眼睛盯着月亮；他看着它移过去，隐没在一根黑黢黢的工厂烟囱后面，又看着它从另一边爬出来。可他没有得到任何征兆。

其实在内心深处，他也并不希望得到征兆，因为他觉得，信念这种东西，是不能也不该靠奇迹当礼物得来的。寻求真理，并在寻求中对自己诚实——这是他能找到的唯一线索。

马丁认定，坚振礼和首次圣餐是法律规定的义务，无法回避。他父亲也没有不同的看法，或者即便有也不说出来，因为他信奉那句谚语：雄辩是银，沉默是金。于是马丁同别的初领圣餐的新教徒一起去了。那是一个春日，教堂墓园里的老树染着娇嫩的新绿，阳光普照；当马丁听见钟声轰鸣歌唱、管风琴奏起进堂赞美诗时，他眼里充满了泪水，心里悲伤，只恨自己不能像别人那样去信、去感受。当他看见满教堂神情肃穆的人们，听见牧师在讲坛上告诫年轻人要牢牢守住父辈的信仰，他内心最深处涌起不安与惶惑，那个问题又来搅扰他：“怎么可能这些人都信，唯独我不信？以为我一个人对、所有这些人不对、连同外面长眠于坟墓中的所有死者也不对——他们生也信这个，死也信这个，而我弃绝的正是这个信仰——这念头是疯了。疯了，疯了！我必须制服我的理性，教会自己去信。”可是等到行礼的时候，他看见穿着白法衣的牧师们在圣坛前走来走去，分着饼和酒，胳膊上像侍者似的搭着餐巾，他便觉得头晕、恶心，不明白自己怎么会被哄骗来参加这样的把戏。虽然他知道，或者自以为知道，这些在幽暗中挪动着的牧师在日常生活中同大多数人一样诚实，可在那一刻，他们在他眼里竟是些无耻的伪君子。

===Original===

Belief in a God and in a life after this was what Martin had left at this time of his childhood faith. But his god was no longer a fatherly god who listened to prayers and nodded approval if they were needful and intelligent, or shook his head if they were childish and stupid. His god had become cold as ice and remote as the moon he had stood staring at on the winter’s night, and Martin ceased saying his evening prayer, for he did not believe there was anyone who heard it.
