Then finally came the day when Martin realized that what he had been calling god these last days was something with which no human being could come into any relation either of love or obedience or opposition, something which could only have the name of god by a wanton play of words and a misuse of the incompleteness of language.

And when he examined his belief in immortality, he soon found that he had got far away from the blue heaven of his childhood. He had observed that all who on any ground other than that of revelation preserved their belief in a life after this also assumed a life before this, and he found such an assumption both natural and logical. Only that is eternal which has always existed. What has come into being will sometime cease to be: such was the law for everything existent. But Martin had no memory of any earlier existence, nor had he either read or heard tell of anyone who had with any gleam of probability given it out that he remembered any such state. There were, to be sure, people who asserted that they recalled their preexistence, but they regularly maintained that they had been some historic personage of whom they had read in books during their present life: e.g., Julius Caesar or Gregory VII. Only rarely could anyone remember having been a slave or a waiter or a shop-clerk. This circumstance appeared peculiar. In any event it was clear that the great majority of people, and Martin among them, had not the slightest recollection of any previous existence. He concluded from this that neither in a future life would he be able to remember anything of the present, that indeed he would not be able to verify his own identity; and he found that if one called such an existence immortality, it was again﻿—as in the question of God﻿—a weakness of thought, a play with the imperfection of language, and nothing else. And it struck him as even more bizarre to give such a name to the passage of the dead body into living nature, into plants and animals and air and water. He had no mind for such kinds of wordplay.

===Chinese===

此时马丁从童年信仰里剩下的，是对上帝和对来世的信念。但他的上帝已不再是一位慈父般的上帝——会垂听祈祷，祷词需要而明智时便点头应允，孩子气而愚蠢时便摇头——他的上帝已变得冷若冰霜、遥远得像那冬夜他凝望过的月亮，于是马丁不再做晚祷，因为他不相信有谁在听。后来终于有那么一天，马丁明白了：这些天来他称为上帝的那个东西，是任何人都无法同它发生爱的、顺从的或者反抗的关系的，它之所以还能称为上帝，全凭文字上任意的游戏和对语言不完备性的滥用。

而当他检视自己灵魂不朽的信念时，他很快发现自己早已远离童年的蓝色天堂。他注意到，凡是并非依据启示、而是出于别的理由保持着来世信仰的人，也都假定了一个前世，他觉得这假定既自然又合逻辑。唯有一直存在的东西才是永恒的。凡生成者终将消亡：这是一切存在之物的法则。可是马丁对任何更早的存在都没有记忆，也从未读到或听说过有谁哪怕带几分可信地声称自己记得这样一种状态。诚然，有些人断言自己记得自己的前世，但他们无一例外地说自己前世是某个历史人物——还是他们在今生从书里读到过的，譬如尤利乌斯·凯撒或格里高利七世。几乎没有人记得自己当过奴隶、侍者或店员。这一情况显得很蹊跷。无论如何，有一点是明白的：绝大多数人，包括马丁在内，对任何前世都没有丝毫记忆。由此他得出结论：到来生里，他同样记不起今生的任何事，甚至无法证实自己是谁；他觉得，倘若把这样一种存在称作不朽，那就同上帝问题一样，是思想的软弱，是拿语言的不完备做的文字游戏，仅此而已。至于把死者肉身化为生机勃勃的自然——化为草木鸟兽、空气和水——的过程也冠以这样的名字，他觉得更是匪夷所思。他没有心思玩这类文字游戏。

===Original===

Things went on in this way so that Martin set out in life without any other belief than that he would grow up, get old and die like a tree in the ground, as his forefathers had done, and that the green earth which he saw with his eyes was his only home in the world and the only space in which it was given him to live and act. And among the many dreams he composed about his life was that in which he was to become like a great and beautiful tree by the wayside with rich foliage, giving coolness and shelter to many. He wished to create happiness and beauty around him and to clear away illusions; he meant to speak and write so that all would have to perceive at once that he was right. To be sure he was not quite certain that truth in itself could produce happiness, but history had taught him that illusion created unhappiness and crime. Like pestilences the various religions had passed over the world, and he was astounded when he thought of all the desolation with which Christianity had marked its way through times and peoples. But he believed in full confidence that its days were reckoned, that he lived at the dawn of a new time, and he wanted to play his part in thought and poetry toward breaking the road for what was to come.

At the time when Martin believed and thought thus it still occurred to him that life, no matter how short and unstable it was, had nevertheless a sort of meaning. He felt himself to be in a state of development and growth; every day new truths arose before his mind and new beauty before his senses during his long lonely wanderings to the edge of the city or in the woods when spring had begun. And spring.﻿ ﻿… At that time spring was still a real spring﻿—not a disease, an intoxication, a fever in the blood, in which all old half-forgotten yearning and regret rises to the surface and says: “Look, here I am! Do you recognize me? I have slept long but I am not dead.” Nothing of that sort, but an awakening, a morning, a murmur in the air, and a resounding song. And at that time the thousand unsatisfied desires which he bore within him were like so many shimmering hopes and half-uttered promises, for no long years of emptiness and disappointment had yet managed to sharpen them into cutting knives which wounded and tore at the soul. And if he did not believe that all these obligations, or even most of them, would be redeemed by life, they were still like bribing possibilities, like a lever for dreams without goal or bounds; and even at the moment when the book he held in his hand or the experience he had had in the course of the day whispered warnings in his ear and advised him not to believe in happiness, these dreams were woven into a longing without bitterness and a melancholy as luminous as a spring twilight.

===Chinese===

事情就这样继续下去，于是马丁踏上人生之路时，所信的不过是这样一点：他将像地里的一棵树那样长大、变老、死去，如他的祖先们一样；他用眼睛看见的绿色大地，是他在世上唯一的家，是赋予他生活和活动的唯一空间。在他为自己的一生编织的许多梦想之中，有一个是：他要变成路旁一棵高大美丽的树，枝叶繁茂，为许多人遮阴蔽日。他愿在自己周围创造幸福与美，扫除种种幻觉；他要说话，要写作，让所有人一眼便看出他是对的。诚然，他并不能十分确定真理本身能否带来幸福，但历史教会了他：幻觉制造不幸与罪恶。种种宗教像瘟疫一样先后席卷世界，一想到基督教在它穿越时代与民族的路途上留下的满目荒凉，他便骇然。但他满怀信心地相信它的气数已尽，相信自己正生活在一个新时代的黎明，他要在思想与诗的领域里尽一份力，为将临的事物开辟道路。

在马丁这样信仰、这样思索的时期，他仍时时觉得，人生无论多么短促、多么无常，终究有它的某种意义。他感到自己正处于发展与生长之中；春天伊始，在他到城郊或树林里作漫长孤独的漫游时，每一天都有新的真理升起在他心头，新的美升起在他眼前。还有春天。……那时的春天还是真正的春天——不是一种疾病，一种沉醉，一种血液里的热病，不是一切旧日半被遗忘的渴念与悔恨都浮上水面来说：“看哪，我在这儿！你认得我么？我睡了很久，可我没有死。”全然不是那样，而是一次苏醒，一个清晨，空气里的一阵低语，一支嘹亮的歌。那时他怀着的千百种未曾满足的欲望，就像无数闪烁的希望和半吐半露的许诺，因为漫长的空虚与失望的岁月还没有来得及把它们磨成伤害、撕扯灵魂的利刃。即使他并不相信所有这些亏欠、甚至其中大半会由生活偿还，它们仍像是贿赂人的种种可能，像一副杠杆，撑起没有目标和边际的梦想；就连他手中的书本或白天的经历在他耳边低声警告、劝他不要相信幸福的那一刻，这些梦也织成一种不含怨怼的向往，一种明亮如春日黄昏的哀愁。

===Original===

Nevertheless these warnings came ever more closely together, and ever more often it happened that in the midst of the dreams youthful blood conjured up he caught himself listening to the other voice, the voice that welled up from the depths of the oldest times and was echoed in the newest books of the day, the strange voice that none of the hundred new gospels which periodically as equinoctial storms had blown through the minds of men could silence for more than a brief moment, the voice which said: “All is vanity, and there is nothing new under the sun.” Why was he alive, and what was the meaning of it all? He did not cease to ask himself these questions, for he still continuously demanded of the life which he saw with his eyes that there should be something behind it, something which could be called life’s meaning. For most of the happiness which he saw men possess and that which he saw them strive for seemed to him like the fairy gold in the story, to be withered leaves, or it appeared to him like nice playthings, something not to be taken seriously. If he turned his gaze to his own life as he lived it from day to day, he could not escape the thought that in itself it was miserable and empty and that its only worth lay in the uncertain hope that it would not remain as it was. But what he hoped for was not something that one could approach step by step with work and patience and a hundred small sacrifices﻿—competence and respect and that sort of thing﻿—what he hoped for was something indefinite and indescribable: a sunrise, a breakup of the ice, an awakening from a painful and purposeless dream.

For it was like a painful and purposeless dream that his life appeared when he looked at it with waking eyes and found it filled with shabby joy, with vulgar sorrow and ignoble anxiety. Now and then he wrote some poems and stories to earn a little money and to prove how far his words could follow his thoughts, but with every new year all he had written in the old seemed to him childish and worthless, and he felt that nothing would amount to anything which could not fill him completely with the joy of creation. Beyond this he fulfilled almost automatically the sum of actions, or more properly gestures, which usually characterize a young man in a government office or to which other circumstances may lead. He went to his work as late in the day as possible and left as early as propriety allowed. He made acquaintance with his fellow employees and shared in their amusements. He drank punch, ate suppers, and visited cheap girls of the streets; he loved music and often sat at the opera among the blackamoors and musical enthusiasts of the upper gallery, and he sang quartets and took his reward in double file when an old school superintendent hung the gilded tin funnel on a rose-colored ribbon around his neck with paternal hands.

And he said to himself: “No, I’m dreaming. This is not life.”

===Chinese===

然而这些警告一次比一次密集，青年血液召唤出的梦境正酣之际，他越来越多地发觉自己在听另一个声音，那从太古深处涌上来、又在一日最新的书籍里激起回响的声音，那奇妙的声音——一百种新福音像季风风暴定期刮过人心，也没有一种能使它沉默片刻——那声音说：“凡事都是虚空，太阳底下没有新事。”他为什么活着？这一切又有什么意义？他不停地这样问自己，因为他仍然不断向他亲眼所见的人生要求：这背后总该有点什么，有点可以称作人生意义的东西。他所见到的人们拥有的幸福、和他们追逐的幸福，多半在他看来像童话里的精灵金子，其实是枯叶，或者像好看的玩物，不当真的。倘若他把目光转向自己日复一日过着的生活，他便无法回避这样一个想法：这生活本身是贫乏而空虚的，它唯一的价值，在于它不会永远如此这个靠不住的希望。可他所盼望的，并不是那种可以靠工作、耐心和千百次小小的牺牲一步一步逼近的东西——什么差事、体面之类——他所盼望的是某种说不清道不明的东西：一次日出，一场开河，从一场痛苦而无意义的梦中醒来。

因为当他用清醒的眼睛打量自己的生活时，它正像一场痛苦而无意义的梦：里面盛满了寒酸的快乐、庸俗的悲哀和卑微的忧虑。他不时写几首诗、几个故事挣点小钱，也好试试自己的笔能把自己的思想追随到多远；可是年复一年，旧作在他眼里一年比一年显得幼稚、一钱不值，他感到凡不能使他充满创造的欢喜的东西，终究成不了什么气候。除此而外，他几乎是机械地履行那一套举动——说得更准确些是姿态——一个政府机关里的年轻人通常以此见称，或者环境使他如此。他上班尽可以晚到，下班一挨到体面许可的时辰便走。他同同事们相识，分享他们的消遣。他喝潘趣酒，吃夜宵，逛街头廉价的姑娘；他爱音乐，常坐在歌剧院顶层楼座的黑人和乐迷中间；他唱四重唱，当一位老学监伸出父亲般的手、把镀金的白铁漏斗用玫瑰色缎带挂上他的脖颈时，他便领取他的奖赏，还一领就是两份。

他对自己说：“不，我是在做梦。这不是生活。”

### X / 十

===Original===

It was late when Martin came home, and he was dead tired but could not sleep. Black butterflies fluttered before his eyes, and thoughts and rhythms came to him as he lay and stared into the dark. He raised himself in bed and relighted the candle on his bedside table, where paper and pen were at hand as always. He felt no feverish overexcitement, only a deep weariness, which pained him but did not delude. He saw clearly where his thought wavered and needed the support of a rhythm, a bit of melody; he changed and erased, and finally a poem evolved.

===Chinese===

马丁回到家时天已很晚，他累得要死，却睡不着。黑蝴蝶在他眼前翻飞；他躺在床上凝望黑暗，思绪和韵律纷纷向他涌来。他在床上支起身子，重新点亮床头柜上的蜡烛——纸和笔一如既往就在手边。他并不觉得发烧似的亢奋，只觉得一阵深深的倦怠。这倦怠使他痛苦，却不欺骗他。他清楚地看出，自己的思想在哪里摇曳、需要一段节奏、一点旋律的支撑；他改了又改、涂了又涂，最后有一首诗渐渐成形。

===Original===

> You up yonder
 

 Who are deaf and dumb!
 

 You up yonder,
 

 Who with your right hand squeeze
 

 The fresh and sweetly-smelling fruit of Good
 

 And with your left constrict
 

 The poison-dripping maggot nest of Ill,
 

 Looking upon them
 

 With equal satisfaction!
 

 You up yonder,
 

 Whose glance is dim
 

 With all the emptiness of space﻿—
 

 I have a prayer to you.
> One prayer, but one,
 

 Which you can never hear
 

 And cannot fulfill:
 

 Teach me,
 

 Teach me to forget
 

 I ever met your glance.
 

 For look!
 

 In youthful days
 

 I myself made a god
 

 In mine own image,
 

 A warm and living and aggressive god,
 

 And on a spring day I went out
 

 To seek for him through all the world and heavens.
 

 Not him I found,
 

 But you.
 

 Not life’s divinity
 

 But death’s I found under the mask of life.
> Take the memory of the sight of you
 

 Away, O horrible One! That memory is
 

 A hidden sickness, is a worm that gnaws
 

 My life-tree’s root.
 

 I know it well, with every barren year
 

 And every day that runs in vain
 

 It gnaws yet closer to my being’s nerve.
 

 It gnaws and preys upon
 

 All that in me which is of human worth,
 

 All that which dares, all that which wills and works;
 

 Nor does it spare
 

 The wondrous, brittle time-piece of the soul
 

 Which points out Good and Ill.
> Speak, you up yonder,
 

 Is it your will
 

 To re-create me after your own image?
 

 Was that the meaning hidden in your word:
 

 “He who hath seen God, he must die the death”?
 

 O horrible One,
 

 Have you the heart to infect
 

 Me, a poor child of men,
 

 With your immortal vices?

===Chinese===

> 你，高高在上，
 

 又聋又哑！
 

 你，高高在上，
 

 用右手攥紧
 

 善的鲜嫩而芬芳的果实，
 

 用左手扼住
 

 恶的滴着毒液的蛆巢，
 

 望着这两者
 

 怀着同样的满足！
 

 你，高高在上，
 

 你的目光黯淡，
 

 满盛太空的全部虚空——
 

 我有一祷，要向你祈求。
> 一祷，仅此一祷，
 

 你永远听不见，
 

 也无法成全：
 

 教我，
 

 教我忘却
 

 我曾与你的目光相接。
 

 因为，看哪！
 

 在青春年少的时日
 

 我亲手造了一位神，
 

 照着我自己的形象，
 

 一位温热的、活生生的、勇猛的神，
 

 在一个春日我出发，
 

 去走遍尘世与诸天寻访他。
 

 我找到的不是他，
 

 却是你。
 

 不是生命的神性，
 

 我在生命的面具下寻见了死亡的神性。
> 把见到你的记忆
 

 拿走吧，哦可怖者！那记忆是
 

 一场隐伏的疾病，是一条蛀蚀
 

 我生命之树根的虫。
 

 我深知，随着每一个荒芜的年头
 

 每一个虚掷的白日
 

 它都蛀得离我生命的神经更近。
 

 它蛀蚀着、吞噬着
 

 我身上一切属人的价值，
 

 一切敢作敢为的，一切有志有为的；
 

 它也不肯放过
 

 灵魂那奇妙而脆弱的时计，
 

 它指明善与恶。
> 说话呀，你，高高在上，
 

 这是你的旨意，
 

 要照你自己的形象重造我？
 

 你那句话里隐藏的，可就是这个意思：
 

 “凡见过神者，必致死亡”？
 

 哦可怖者，
 

 你竟忍心传染——
 

 传染我，一个可怜的人之子，
 

 以你那不朽的恶习？

### XI / 十一

===Original===

The afternoon sun fell across the writing table and gilded everything: the inkstand, the books, and the words he wrote on the paper. The smoke from the chimneys rose straight and tranquilly toward heaven, and in a window just opposite a young Jewess was playing with her child.

Martin was writing to his sister:

===Chinese===

午后的阳光斜落在写字台上，给一切都镀了金：墨水台、书本，还有他写在纸上的字。烟囱里冒出的烟笔直而安详地升向天空，正对面的一个窗口里，一位年轻的犹太女子正逗着她的孩子玩。

马丁在给姐姐写信：

===Original===

> Dear Maria:

> Thanks for your letter. Mamma is poorly as usual, perhaps a little better these last weeks. Papa keeps the same, only he gets more silent every year. It’s very quiet here at home, for as you know I am not one either to love idle talk. Silence is golden. Uncle Janne, Aunt Louise, etc., are still, unfortunately, alive and in health, though it doesn’t make much difference anyhow, since we are not likely to be their heirs. But they are always annoying me by asking about the prospects of my work, whether papa isn’t in line for the Order of Vasa soon, whether it’s true that your husband takes morphine, and so on. Otherwise there is no harm in them.

> You ask whether I’m writing much just now. No, very little, but on the other hand I have an appointment for a long job as amanuensis, and last night I dreamed very clearly and distinctly that papa and I got an Order of Vasa together, since the king couldn’t manage to give us each one.

> Thanks for the invitation to come to you in the summer, but it’s not likely I can get off﻿—my appointment will last over the summer. Too bad your husband is nervous. Nice your little boy is well. Remember me to all.

> Your brother **Martin**.

===Chinese===

> 亲爱的玛丽亚（Maria）：

> 谢谢你的信。妈妈还是老样子，身子不好，最近几个礼拜也许略好一点。爸爸一切照旧，只是一年比一年沉默。家里很安静，你知道，我本来也不是个爱闲聊的人。沉默是金。扬内叔叔、路易丝婶婶等人，不幸还都健在，身子骨也硬朗，不过横竖没多大关系，反正我们多半做不成他们的继承人。可他们总拿些问题来烦我：问我工作的前程如何，问爸爸是不是快轮到瓦萨勋章了，问你丈夫打吗啡的传闻是真是假，如此等等。除此之外，他们倒没什么害处。

> 你问我眼下写得多不多。不多，写得很少，不过我倒谋到了一份长期的抄写员差事，而且昨天夜里，我清清楚楚、明明白白地梦见爸爸和我一起得了一枚瓦萨勋章，因为国王没法子给我们一人一枚。

> 谢谢你请我夏天去你们那儿，可我多半脱不开身——我的差事要一直做过夏天。你丈夫神经不好，真遗憾。小家伙身子骨好，倒叫人高兴。向大家问好。

> 你的弟弟**马丁**

===Original===

He put the letter in an envelope and laid it aside.

He sat and thought about his sister.

“Is she happy?” he asked himself. And he was forced to answer: “No, she is not happy. She does not perhaps know it herself. Six years ago she was very happy, when she was married and became a doctor’s wife and had her own little home in the country to look after﻿—just what she had most dreamed of. She hasn’t had any sudden fall from the peak of happiness since then. She has just very quietly slipped down, as usually happens with the years. Her husband is amiable and talented and a clever doctor, but he offends the rich people in his district and has most of his practice among the poor. Therefore he is sometimes hard up. Besides, I am afraid his health is undermined and his disposition is sometimes rather bitter. However, he was in very good humor when he was up here last alone, without her. He amused himself as well as he could, and I fear he was a bit unfaithful.

“A curious bird, happiness.﻿ ﻿…”

During these thoughts Martin had begun again to write. He wrote slowly and half in play, with an intention here and there yet without exactly knowing whither he was tending.

===Chinese===

他把信装进信封，放在一边。

他坐着，想起姐姐。

“她幸福吗？”他问自己。而他不得不回答：“不，她不幸福。她自己也许并不知道。六年前她是幸福的，那时她刚出嫁，做了医生的太太，在乡下有了自己小小的家可以操持——那正是她梦想得最多的事。打那以后，她并没有从幸福的顶峰上一下子跌下来。她只是一点不出声地滑下来了，像岁月里常有的那样。她丈夫和气、有才干，是个聪明的大夫，可他得罪辖区里的有钱人，求他看病的多半是穷人。所以他有时手头拮据。再说，我怕他的健康已经亏空了，脾气有时也相当刻薄。不过上回他一个人上来、没带她的时候，兴致倒是很好。他尽情地玩了个痛快，我怕他还有点不忠。”

“幸福这鸟儿，真是奇怪。……”

一面想着这些，马丁又提笔写了起来。他写得慢，半带游戏，这里那里藏一点用意，自己却也不十分清楚要往哪里去。

===Original===

> “You do not know me. I met you one day in the sunlight. It is weeks, yes, months since then. You went on the side of the street where the sun shone; you went alone with head lowered and smiled to yourself.

> “It was one of those days when the snow was beginning to melt on the street and the pavement shone wet and bright. You stopped at the corner of a street, greeted an old lady and conversed with her. The old lady was very ugly and very stupid, and I imagine too a little cross, as stupid people generally are. But when you looked at her and talked with her, she at once grew less cross and less ugly.

> “A little farther up the street a gentleman saluted you, and you bowed and returned his greeting. I felt my heart become bitter with envy, and I followed him with my glance as he went on down the street. But one could not see it in him that he had just spoken to you. One could rather believe he was a lieutenant who had just saluted a major.

> “I have met you often since then. You do not know me, and it is not likely that you will ever know who I am. You go in the sunlight, I go for the most part in the shadow. I am dressed like many other men, and I always avoid looking at you so that you see it. No, you cannot find out who I am.

> “You have a lamp with a yellow shade. Yesterday you stood long at the window in the yellow glow, after you had lighted the lamp, looking at the stars. You went to the window to pull down the curtains, but you forgot about it a little while. Straight in front of your window was a star which burned more brightly than the rest. I could not see it, for I stood shut in by a little black gate opposite the house where you live; but I know that on spring evenings it stands just so that you must see it from your window. It is Venus.

===Chinese===

> “你不认识我。有一天，我在阳光里遇见了你。那是几个星期——是的，几个月以前的事了。你走在街上晒着太阳的那一边；你独自走着，低着头，独自微笑。

> “那是那种日子：街上的雪开始融化，人行道湿漉漉地发亮。你在街角停下来，向一位老太太问好，同她攀谈。那位老太太很丑，也很蠢，我猜想脾气还有点坏，蠢人多半如此。可是当你望着她、同她说话时，她立刻就不那么坏、也不那么丑了。

> “再往前一点，街上有一位先生向你打招呼，你欠身还了礼。我的心妒意发苦，目光一路跟着他往街那头去了。可是他身上看不出刚同你说过话的样子。倒不如说，会以为他是个刚向少校敬礼的少尉。

> “从那以后我常遇见你。你不认识我，你多半永远不会知道我是谁。你走在阳光里，我多半走在阴影里。我穿得同许多别的男人一样，我总留神不让你察觉我在看你。不，你查不出我是谁。

> “你有一盏黄灯罩的灯。昨天，你点起灯以后，久久站在窗口黄色的光晕里望星星。你走到窗前原是要放下窗帘的，可你一时忘了。正对你窗户的是一颗星，比别的星烧得更亮。我看不见它，因为我被关在你住的那幢房子对面的一个小黑门里；可我知道，春天傍晚它正停在你从窗口必得看见的地方。那是维纳斯。

===Original===

> “You do not know me, and I do not know you otherwise than I do the women who sometimes give me the great joy of visiting me at night in my dreams. It is therefore I speak to you so intimately. But among these women you have for some time been the only one, the others have forsaken me, nor do I feel any longing after them.

> “Read this letter and think no more of it; burn it, if you will, or hide it at the bottom of your little secret drawer, if you will. Read it and think no more of it, go out as before in the sunlight and smile in your own happy thoughts. But you are not to show it to your friends and let them giggle and snicker over it. If you do that, for three nights in a row you will not be able to sleep for bad dreams, and a little devil from hell will sit on the edge of your bed and look at you from evening till morning.

> “But I know you will not do such a thing﻿—you will not show it to anyone. Good night, my beloved, good night!”

===Chinese===

> “你不认识我，我认识你也不过像认识那些女人一样——她们有时在夜里到我的梦中来，给我莫大的快乐。所以我才这样同你亲密地说话。而在这些女人中间，你已经有一阵子是唯一的一个了，别的都弃我而去，我对她们也不再有什么渴念。

> “读这封信，读完就别再想它；愿意的话就把它烧了，或者藏进你那小秘密抽屉的最底层，随你的便。读这封信，读完就别再想它，照旧走到阳光里去，在你自己的快乐念头里微笑。只是不许拿给你的朋友们看，让她们咯咯地笑、吃吃地窃笑。你要是那样做，就会一连三夜被噩梦搅得睡不着，还会有个小魔鬼从地狱里来，坐在你的床沿上，从晚上到早晨一直盯着你。

> “可我知道你不会做这样的事——你不会给任何人看的。晚安，我的爱人，晚安！”

===Original===

Martin sat long with this letter in his hand. “What could it lead to if I sent it?” he asked himself. “To nothing, presumably. It would set her imagination off a bit, her young girl’s longing would perhaps have an impulse toward the new and unknown. She might perhaps bring herself to show the letter to her friends, seeing that faith in devils is on the wane; but she wouldn’t go so far as to burn it. She might perhaps be amused with it, she might even consider it her duty to feel offended. But in reality it would in the long run cause her joy, and if in the process of nature she was married and had children and grew old with household cares and every year sunk deeper down in the inconsolable monotony of existence, she would come to remember this letter and wonder who wrote it and if perhaps it was there that the true seed of happiness lay hid. And she would never once recall that it ever made her angry. Nor as a matter of fact does it contain anything that could properly hurt her. It shows her only that she is desired by a man, and as she is twenty and from head to foot an uncommonly beautiful and glorious creation of nature, she must already have noticed that men desire her. And that doesn’t at all make her angry, but on the contrary happy and joyous, and that is why she walks in the sunlight and smiles.”

Amid such thoughts he sat a long while weighing the letter in his hand as if it had been a human destiny, till in the end he found his hesitation ridiculous, put the letter in an envelope of thick untransparent paper, and wrote the address in a thin and noncommittal girlish style so as not to rouse any curiosity in the young lady’s family. Without revealing any special interest on his part he had succeeded in learning her name. She was a Miss Harriet Skottë. Her father had an estate in the country, in the Malar district, and she was now spending the winter in Stockholm with some relatives to study something or other, French or art-tapestry or something of the sort﻿ ﻿… in order to get engaged, to put it briefly.

Harriet Skottë. He repeated the name to himself and tried to analyze the impression it evoked. He dwelt in particular on the forename and murmured, “Harriet, Harriet.” But this gave him no impression of her nature; it roused only an indefinite conception of something English and pale and blonde, a sensation of tea fumes and benevolence and chilly bedrooms with varnished floors as at a hospital. The surname, again, only suggested family, an uncle who was on the Board of Trade, and a cousin who was a lieutenant in the Army Service Corps. But if he whispered to himself the whole name, “Harriet Skottë,” there came in a new element which quite excluded the others, then it became something quite different and new, then he felt as if she herself passed through the room with her brown hair glinting in a sunbeam.

===Chinese===

这封信在马丁手里拿了很久。“要是把它寄出去，会引出什么来呢？”他问自己。“多半什么也不会。它会撩动一下她的想象，她少女的向往或许会朝那新鲜未知的方向受一次冲动。看如今对魔鬼的信仰日渐衰微，她也许会下得了决心把信拿给朋友们看；不过还不至于走到把它烧掉那一步。她也许拿它取乐，说不定还会觉得受了冒犯、理应生气。可实际上，长远说来这信会给她快乐；倘依着自然的进程她嫁了人，生了孩子，被家务一年一年越陷越深地埋进那种无可慰藉的单调生活里，她会想起这封信，纳闷是谁写的，说不定还会想：幸福的真正种子，莫不就藏在这里。而她绝不会想起这信曾叫她生气过。事实上，信里也实在没有什么当真能伤害她的东西。它只告诉她，有个男人渴慕她；而她二十岁，从头到脚是造化一件格外美丽光彩的作品，男人渴慕她这件事，她必定早觉察到了。这根本不会叫她生气，相反会叫她欢喜快乐，所以她才走在阳光里微笑。”

就在这样的思绪里，他坐了很久，把信在手里掂来掂去，仿佛掂着的是一个人的命运，直到最后他觉得自己的迟疑可笑，便把信装进一只厚厚的、不透光的信封，用一种细细的、不着边际的女孩子笔迹写上地址，免得惹起那位小姐家里人的好奇。他没有显露一点特别的兴趣，却打听到了她的名字。她是一位哈丽特·斯科特（Harriet Skottë）小姐。她父亲在乡下梅拉伦（Malar）地区有一座庄园，她如今住在斯德哥尔摩的亲戚家过冬，学点什么——法文啦，艺术刺绣啦，诸如此类……简单说，是为了订一门亲。

哈丽特·斯科特。他把这名字在心里默念，试着分析它唤起的印象。他尤其咀嚼那个名，低声念着：“哈丽特，哈丽特。”可这并没有给他一点关于她本性的印象；它只唤起一种说不清的联想，某种英国的、苍白的、金发的东西，一种茶气、慈爱和上了清漆地板的阴冷卧室的感觉，像医院里那样。姓呢，又只让人想到家世，想到一位在贸易委员会当差的叔叔，一位在陆军辎重队当少尉的表兄。可要是他把全名低声连起来念——“哈丽特·斯科特”——便有一个新的因素进来，把其余的全排除了，于是它变成一种全然不同的、崭新的东西，于是他觉得仿佛是她本人从房间里走过，棕色的头发在一道日光里闪亮。

===Original===

---

He started at the ringing of the hall bell; he heard the maid open the front door and a familiar voice asking if he was at home. He stuffed the letter into his pocket. The next instant the door opened and Henrik Rissler stood in the doorway blinking at the sunlight, whose copper-red rays struck horizontally across the room.

===Chinese===

---

门铃一响，他吓了一跳；他听见女仆去开大门，一个熟悉的声音问他可在家里。他把信塞进口袋。转眼间门开了，亨利克·里斯勒站在门口，对着阳光直眨眼——铜红色的日光正横着射进屋子里来。

### XII / 十二

===Original===

Henrik Rissler had come down from Uppsala. He had just taken his preliminary degree and in a couple of weeks was to make a tour down in Europe while he wrote his thesis, “On Romantic Irony.” He had no independent means, but his uncle﻿—a bank lawyer, politician, and millionaire﻿—had offered to pay for the trip. This Martin already knew from Henrik’s letters. But before he started he was to rest a few weeks. He was somewhat overworked, for he had studied hard so as to get away from Uppsala as soon as possible, and he had also taken extra time to write some critical studies for a magazine and so become a little better known among the score or so of men who interested themselves in such things.

Martin had been expecting him for a couple of days and had a bottle of wine and a pack of cigarettes ready.

Henrik shaded his eyes from the sun and said: “Here everything is the same. Here time has stood still.”

“Yes, in this immediate region,” answered Martin. “Only they have built a big factory chimney over opposite. It has been quite a diversion for me in solitude. For a while I worked in competition with the masons, but I was beaten. I began on a poem when they had just begun on the chimney; now the chimney is done, but not the poem. It’s beautiful, what’s more﻿—the chimney, I mean. Especially in the evening as a silhouette. The smoke no longer belches out, one forgets its purpose; it is no longer a chimney, it is a pillar tower built by some Chaldaean prince and priest, who mounts it when night comes on and measures the course of the stars.”

“Yes,” said Henrik, “one forgets the purpose, then first it becomes beautiful.”

===Chinese===

亨利克·里斯勒从乌普萨拉下来了。他刚通过学士学位考试，再过几个星期就要动身去欧洲大陆旅行一趟，一面写他的论文《论浪漫的反讽》。他并无恒产，但他的叔叔——一位银行律师、政客兼百万富翁——主动提出负担这趟旅费。这事马丁早已从亨利克的信里知道了。动身之前他要歇几个星期。他有些用脑过度，因为为了尽早离开乌普萨拉，他发狠用功，还额外抽出时间给一家杂志写了几篇批评文章，于是在那为数二十来个留意这种事情的人中间，稍稍有了点名气。

马丁已经盼了他两天，备好了一瓶酒和一包香烟。

亨利克用手遮着阳光，说：“这儿一切都还是老样子。这儿的时间停滞不动。”

“是的，就这一带而言，”马丁答道，“只是对面竖起了一根大工厂烟囱。独居的日子里，它成了我的一大消遣。有一阵子我跟砌烟囱的工匠们竞赛，可我输了。他们刚动工砌烟囱时，我开始写一首诗；如今烟囱砌成了，诗还没有。何况它很美——我是说那烟囱。傍晚剪影里尤其美。烟不再喷出来，人就忘了它的用途；它不再是烟囱，而是一位迦勒底王子兼祭司建起的塔柱，夜幕降临时，他便登上塔顶，测量星辰的运行。”

“是啊，”亨利克说，“人忘了用途，它才头一回变美。”

===Original===

“No,” replied Martin, “it doesn’t become beautiful because one forgets its purpose, but because one invents for it another which has the prestige of old and venerable poetic tradition. But outside of that, in and for themselves, without any fancification, factory chimneys are among the most beautiful of modern structures. They promise less than they make good, and at least they are no masquerade figures either in Gothic or Renaissance.”

Henrik smiled. “You’re talking in the style of the ‘ ’Eighties,’ ” he said.

---

Henrik Rissler sat in his old place in the sofa corner, Martin sat in the rocking-chair at the writing table. They were drinking wine and talking about Uppsala, about books and women, and about a new philosopher by the name of Nietzsche. And as they talked, the sunbeam in which the motes danced like red sparks grew ever narrower and more oblique and more decidedly red.

===Chinese===

“不，”马丁反驳道，“它变美并不是因为人忘了它的用途，而是因为人替它虚构了另一个用途，一个带着古老而可敬的诗的传统的威望的用途。可撇开这个不谈，就工厂烟囱本身而言，不加点花哨想象，它也属于现代建筑里最美的一类。它们许诺得少，兑现得多，而且至少不是什么哥特式或文艺复兴式的假面舞会货色。”

亨利克笑了。“你这是在用‘八十年代’的腔调说话。”他说。

---

亨利克·里斯勒坐在沙发角落里他的老位置上，马丁坐在写字台前的摇椅里。他们喝着酒，谈乌普萨拉，谈书本和女人，谈一位名叫尼采（Nietzsche）的新哲学家。他们谈着谈着，那道尘埃像红火粒一样飞舞的日光，越来越窄，越来越斜，也越来越红了。

===Original===

Martin surveyed Henrik. He found him changed; his face was leaner, stronger, and more masculine in contour. Why had he said, “Here everything is the same, here time has stood still”? He had had an experience, but what? He was in love presumably; he would perhaps go so far as to get engaged﻿—to whom? Was it his cousin Anna Rissler? She was fond of him and he knew it. No, that couldn’t be. Was it Maria Randel, or Sigrid Tesch?

“It’s curious,” observed Henrik. “Have you felt the same thing?﻿—how painful it is to search for old associations and not to find them. To read over a book one has been fond of, or hear an opera into which one has formerly been able to put everything imaginable and a bit more﻿—and sit empty-handed, wondering where it has all gone to!”

“Yes,” Martin agreed, “it’s a strange, oppressive feeling. One feels as if it was one’s duty to stick to the past, as if one were committing an infidelity.﻿ ﻿… And one can do nothing. Why is it really so painful? Is it perhaps because there is no plaintiff in the suit, no clearly formulated claim to meet? For the plaintiff is not the book or the music which one has lost touch with, not the mood which shrinks away; the plaintiff is one’s old self, and that is dead and buried, it is supplanted and refuted by the new, it has no plea to make and yet it does make a sort of plea. Therein lies the paradox, and there is nothing as vexatious as a paradox, when it is not comic.”

Henrik took up the thread.

“Yes, you are right; it is between the old and the new self that the battle is, and as long as there is a new which is the stronger, one can always master the phantoms. There is a continuous growth. The old goes, the new comes﻿—or the old goes, that’s really the one certain thing, for how long can one be sure whether the new will come in its place? Suppose the supply should stop some day, suppose nothing under the sun should be new any more, and one only became poorer with every year and every day that passed!”

===Chinese===

马丁打量着亨利克。他发觉他变了：脸瘦了些，轮廓更硬朗、更有男子气了。他刚才为什么说“这儿一切都还是老样子，这儿的时间停滞不动”？他有过一段经历，可是什么呢？多半是恋爱了；说不定还会走到订婚那一步——同谁呢？是他表妹安娜·里斯勒（Anna Rissler）吗？她喜欢他，这他知道。不，那不可能。是玛丽亚·兰德尔（Maria Randel），还是西格丽德·特施（Sigrid Tesch）？

“说来奇怪，”亨利克说，“你也有过这样的感觉吗？——追寻旧日的联想而寻它不着，是多么痛苦的事。重读一本自己曾钟爱的书，或者重听一部从前能把自己所能想象的一切、还要再多一点的东西都倾注进去的歌剧——却两手空空地坐着，纳闷那些都到哪里去了！”

“是啊，”马丁表示同意，“这是一种古怪的、叫人憋闷的感觉。人仿佛觉得自己有义务忠于过去，仿佛不这样就等于负心。……可又毫无办法。这为什么真的这样痛苦呢？莫非是因为这场官司里没有原告，没有一条明明白白的诉讼请求需要应对？因为原告并不是那本与人断了缘分的书、那支音乐，也不是那悄然退去的旧日心绪；原告是从前的自己，而他已经死了、埋了，被新的自己取而代之、驳倒了，他没有抗辩可提，却毕竟提着那么一点抗辩。悖论就在这里，而天下再没有比悖论更气人的了，只要它不逗笑。”

亨利克接过了话头。

“不错，你说得对；仗就打在旧的自己和新的自己之间，只要新的那个更强，人就总能降服那些幽灵。生长是不断的。旧的去了，新的来了——或者说，旧的去了，这才是唯一有把握的事，谁能担保新的就一定会来接替呢？万一补给有一天断了，万一太阳底下再没有一样东西是新的，人只是随着过去的一年一年、一天一天，变得越来越穷了呢！”

===Original===

“Yes,” said Martin, “that sort of thing happens sometimes. And there are cases then in which a man digs up the oldest, the deadest, and most withered thing in his past and begins to worship it anew without seeing the caricature. That’s nearly the worst of all. Better the old saying: poor but proud.”

They sat silent a few minutes. The sun had gone, and still it was not twilight yet. It was almost brighter in the room than just before; everything in it had merely become suddenly pale.

Henrik broke the silence.

“Yes,” he said, “it’s a melancholy feeling to grow out of oneself and one’s old associations﻿—but what’s it matter so long as one grows? And what is melancholy, anyhow, if it isn’t what the rowdy said of the toothbrush, a new kind of amusement invented by the upper classes? But the melancholy is only there when it’s a matter of associations and music and ideas. It was really something else I’ve been thinking of all the time. I’ve been thinking of love and women. If one comes into that province, it isn’t only just melancholy any longer; no, one can’t get off so cheaply. A man is fond of a woman. He wants the whole of eternity to be in that feeling. And yet he can’t escape reflecting that this emotion must be subordinate to the same law of growth as everything else in the world, that some day he will weary of what he loves just as one wearies of the moonlight music in *Faust*. I have not had many love affairs, but, believe me, I have never even in my imagination begun the game otherwise than with the thought: may she be the first to tire, and not I!”

===Chinese===

“是啊，”马丁说，“这种事有时是有的。于是就有人把过去里最古老、最死气、最干枯的东西挖掘出来，重新顶礼膜拜，全看不见那已是漫画了。这差不多是所有情形里最糟的。还不如那句老话：穷，可是有骨气。”

他们默默坐了几分钟。太阳落下去了，却还没到暮色。屋子里几乎比刚才还亮些；只是其中的一切忽然都变得苍白。

亨利克打破了沉默。

“是啊，”他说，“从自己和旧日的联想里长出去，是一种忧郁的感觉——可只要人在生长，又有什么要紧？何况忧郁究竟是什么呢，还不就是那个无赖说的牙刷——上流社会发明的一种新式消遣？不过这忧郁只在联想、音乐和思想的事情上才有。我心里一直想着的其实是另一件事。我想的是爱情和女人。人一旦进了那个国度，就不再只是忧郁了事；不，人脱不了那么轻巧的身。一个男人爱一个女人。他愿意整个永恒都在这份感情里。可他不能不想到：这份感情也得服从世上万物共同的生长法则，总有一天他会对所爱之物生厌，就像人对《浮士德》里的月光音乐生厌一样。我恋爱过不了几次，可是相信我，就连在想象里，我也从没有一回不是带着这个念头开场的：但愿先厌倦的是她，不是我！”

===Original===

“I’m afraid that prayer will not be often uttered,” said Martin. “To be sure both a lover and a married man may be betrayed, but it rarely happens that they wish to be.”

“Still I’m ashamed of the prayer, for I know it comes straight from my heart’s great cowardice. How far must we not have come from the primitive simple and straightforward conception of these things to think it is happier to be betrayed than to betray! And yet that’s how I feel. What does love signify to me; what does it ever mean to a man? Why should there be anything tragic in the fact that a man is betrayed in love? If he takes it tragically he merely becomes comic. And if on the discovery that he is a cuckold he breaks off reading a good book, he deserves to be one. But women﻿—it’s a different thing with them.”

Henrik’s glance was fixed on vacancy.

===Chinese===

“这祷告恐怕不会常有人念，”马丁说，“情夫也好，丈夫也好，固然都可能被辜负，可他们难得愿意被辜负。”

“尽管这样，我为这祷告害臊，因为我知道它直接出自我心里的大怯懦。想到被辜负竟比辜负人快活，我们离开这些事情原始质朴、直截了当的观念该有多远了！可我的感觉偏偏就是这样。爱情于我算得了什么；它于一个男人又究竟算得了什么？一个男人在爱情里被辜负，这里面有什么悲剧可言呢？他若把它当作悲剧，那他只会变得可笑。倘若他因为发现自己戴了绿帽就丢开一本正在读的好书，那他活该戴。可是女人——对她们来说是另一回事。”

亨里克的目光凝视着虚空。

===Original===

“Deserted women,” he said﻿—“there’s something special about them. One can’t escape lightly from the thought of them. No, if they scold and fuss and make a row, it’s easier at once; then the whole thing becomes burlesque, one shakes it off, and is free. Then one asks oneself, ‘How did I ever come to love such a creature?’ One easily persuades oneself that one has never loved her, and so she’s out of the story. But the others﻿—it seems the most painful thing of all to me to imagine her whom I love withered and pale, discarded, put in the shadow side of life, while I myself live on.﻿ ﻿… It is a paradox, I realize﻿—it can never happen; one cannot at the same time act so and feel it so. And yet﻿ ﻿… I met an old woman just now, here on the street, right outside your door. She was old and very pale and a little comic. She was quite shabbily dressed, too﻿—one of the poor who are too proud to beg. One often sees such old women; there was nothing remarkable about her, nothing that distinguished her from any others of her kind, except that all at once, when I came close to her, she struck me as so like﻿—No, I can’t tell you straight out. There’s a young girl I’m very fond of. I’m so fond of her that we’re going to be married, perhaps very soon. It was she that the old woman was like, despite the difference in age and all the rest﻿—it was one of those indefinite resemblances that one thinks one sees the first moment, and the next it’s gone without one’s knowing in what it consists. But that moment was enough for me; a chill went through me, a shudder as if I had seen something terrible, and it seemed to me only all the worse that everything else was as usual: the sun was shining and people were on the street.﻿ ﻿… The girl I care for stood before me, she passed me, withered, discarded, a little comic. It came over me that not even the thought that I myself was dead and lying under the earth could be any consolation to me in such a case; the only conception that could bring any relief was that I was living as wretched and exhausted as she.”

They sat quiet a long while.

“Tell me,” Martin finally asked, “who is she, the girl you are fond of? That is, if it’s no secret. Do I know her?”

“Yes,” said Henrik, in a subdued voice, “you know her, and I can tell you. It is Sigrid Tesch.”

===Chinese===

“被抛弃的女人，”他说——“她们身上有些特别的东西。想起她们，人没法轻轻巧巧地脱身。不，如果她们骂骂咧咧、大吵大闹，倒一下子好办了；那样整件事就变成了滑稽戏，人把它一抖落，就自由了。那时人问自己：‘我当初怎么会爱上这么个东西？’人很容易说服自己从来就没爱过她，于是她就算出了戏。可另一些女人——对我来说，再没有比想象我所爱的女人形容枯槁、面色苍白，被弃在人生的阴面，而我自己却继续活着更痛心的了。……我知道这是悖论——它永远不会发生；人不能既这样行事、又这样感受。可是……方才我遇见一个老太太，就在街上，就在你门口。她老了，脸色很苍白，还有点可笑。她穿得也相当寒酸——是那种穷得太傲、不肯行乞的穷人。这样的老太太到处可见；她身上并没有什么特别，没有什么使她不同于同类的地方，只是当我走近她时，她忽然让我觉得那么像——不，我没法直说。有一个姑娘，我很喜欢她。喜欢到我们快要结婚了，也许很快。那老太太像的就是她，尽管年龄和其余一切都不同——那是那种说不清的相像，人乍一见以为自己看出来了，转眼它又没了，人也不知道它究竟在哪里。可那一眼对我就够了；一阵寒意穿过我全身，一阵战栗，仿佛看见了什么可怕的东西，而其余一切照旧，这在我看来反而更糟：太阳照着，街上人来人往。……我所疼爱的姑娘站在我面前，从我身边走过，枯槁了，被弃了，有点可笑。我忽然明白，在这种情形里，就连想到我自己已经死了、躺在地底下，也不能给我一点安慰；唯一能让人松口气的念头，是我活得同她一样潦倒、一样耗竭。”

他们静静地坐了许久。

“告诉我，”马丁终于问道，“她是谁，你喜欢的那个姑娘？——要是不算秘密的话。我认识她吗？”

“认识，”亨利克压低声音说，“你认识她，我也可以告诉你。是西格丽德·特施。”

===Original===

Sigrid Tesch. Martin saw before him a young and supple figure, with dark abundant hair and delicate regular features. He had met her a couple of times quite cursorily. He knew she had made an impression on Henrik, and in his own twilight thoughts she had sometimes passed by with a pallid dream smile.

So it was she then, Sigrid Tesch, who was to be Henrik’s bride.

“Yes,” said Henrik, “isn’t it inexplicable that one can dare go into such a thing as love?﻿ ﻿… And yet.﻿ ﻿…”

“Yes,” said Martin, “and yet.﻿ ﻿…”

===Chinese===

西格丽德·特施。马丁眼前浮现出一个年轻柔韧的身影，一头浓密的深色头发，一张端正秀气的脸。他匆匆见过她两三面。他知道她给亨利克留下了印象；在他自己那些朦胧的思绪里，她有时也带着一个苍白的梦一样的微笑走过。

原来是她，西格丽德·特施，要做亨里克的新娘了。

“是啊，”亨利克说，“人竟敢走进爱情这样的事情里去，这不是没法解释吗？……可是毕竟。……”

“是啊，”马丁说，“可是毕竟。……”

===Original===

They both smiled.

Henrik Rissler got up.

“It is dusk,” he said; “we can hardly see the glasses. Will you go out with me? It’s wonderful outside tonight. Oh, you want to write﻿—Well, we’ll see each other again soon. Goodbye!”

===Chinese===

两人都笑了。

亨利克·里斯勒站起身来。

“天黑下来了，”他说，“连酒杯都快看不见了。陪我出去走走好吗？今晚外面好极了。哦，你要写作——那好吧，咱们很快会再见的。再见！”

### I / 一

===Original===

Over Martin’s table in the office an electric light with a green shade swung, like a pendulum, gently to and fro on its silken cord. It had been set in motion just a moment ago when he had lighted it. He stretched out his hand to stop it, but instead waited the time when the swinging should subside and die down until it was imperceptible. Lamps were likewise screwed up over the other tables, six shining green triangles swung to and fro in the semidarkness of the room, and lean writers’ hands fumbled at the windows after the curtain cords to pull them down and shut out the snow and the winter dusk. Martin loved these green lamps, which gave out no heat or bad odor, and whose glow had the pure and cold sheen of jewels; and he longed for the day when electric light should be cheap enough to make its way down even into the homes of the poor. And just here in this big low old room with whitewashed walls, because the house was old and had a groined gateway and low small-paned windows in the entrance hall where his office was, these green lamps seemed to him to fit in even better; he saw in this a symbol of continuous development, an unbroken chain of hands and wills, from those which had wearied long since to those which were now in embryo, the new inwoven with the old. Where all is old there enters an atmosphere of wretchedness and decay, and where all is new only that can thrive and feel at home which is itself new from top to toe, from pocketbook to soul.

And Martin was not new, his clothes were not new, nor were his thoughts. He thought and knew nothing great other than that which others had taught him﻿—various old gentlemen in England and France who were now for the most part dead. If these thoughts still brought him any joy, it was mainly because the times had seemingly forgotten them long ago, as if they had been written in running water. Other winds were blowing now, winds before which he preferred to draw up his collar over his ears; everything came back and all the corpses peeped out, but he did not care to see them.

===Chinese===

马丁在机关里办公桌的上方，一盏绿罩电灯正像钟摆一样，顺着丝线轻轻来回摆动。那是他方才点亮时碰动的。他伸手要去按住它，却索性等着摆动自己平息下去、弱下去，直到察觉不出。别的桌子上也都拧亮了灯，六个亮闪闪的绿色三角在屋子的半明半暗里来回摆动；一双双瘦削的文书的手在窗边摸索着窗帘绳，好拉下窗帘，挡住风雪和冬日的黄昏。马丁喜欢这些绿灯，它们不发热，也没有难闻的气味，光晕有着宝石般纯净清冷的色泽；他盼着有一天电灯便宜下来，连穷人的家里也能点上。而恰恰在这间低矮宽大的旧屋子里——四壁刷着白灰，因为房子老，门道里是交叉拱顶的门楼和装着小格玻璃的矮窗，他的办公室就设在这里——这些绿灯在他看来显得更加相宜；他从中看出一个持续发展的象征，一条由手和意志连成的不曾中断的链，从那些早已疲惫的手，一直连到那些如今还在胚芽里的手，新的同旧的交织在一起。凡物皆旧之处，便有一种破败腐朽的气息；凡物皆新之处，只有从头到脚、从钱袋到灵魂自身全新之物才能活得兴旺自在。

而马丁并不是新的，他的衣服不是新的，他的思想也不是。他所想、所知的了不起的东西，无非是别人教给他的——多半是英国和法国的几位如今大半已经作古的老先生。这些思想倘若还能给他一点快乐，那主要是因为时代仿佛早已把它们忘掉了，仿佛它们是写在水上的。如今刮的是别的风了，遇到这种风，他宁可竖起衣领遮住耳朵；一切都转回来了，所有的尸首都探出头来，可他不想看见它们。

===Original===

The lamp had ceased to swing over his desk, and he returned to his accounting. He no longer contented himself with putting down ticks; he carefully scanned every item and added up every column. His first youthful antipathy to a mechanical task was long since conquered, and he had gradually come to learn that these figures were not, as he had first believed, entirely free from the imperfections which are inherent in everything human. On the contrary they were often encumbered with inaccuracies and mistakes; and when he now and again discovered such mistakes, he was glad at heart but felt at the same time a faint sensation of sorrow. He was glad because he had occasion to show his great zeal and because he could count upon his rightful percentage of the sum which his alertness had saved the state treasury; and he felt the dark memory of ancient sorrow when he recalled that he had desired a quite different sort of joy from life. Sometimes, too, he thought of the poor officials down at Landskrona, Ohus, or Haparanda, who had made the wrong calculations, perhaps under the influence of last night’s toddy, and who would now have to pay the difference. But this thought left him cold, for the years had taught him he must set limits to his sympathies.

It was warm in the room, the remains of a great birchwood fire glowed in the porcelain stove, for there was no inducement to spare the government’s wood in these times when one had to skimp one’s fuel at home. Von Heringslake, the chief clerk, who had an income of forty-six hundred crowns and performed his duties with the pleasant ease which comes with an independence, sat squatted in front of the stove and roasted apples over the embers. On his bald pate﻿—which his mortal enemy, Auditor Camin, asserted was the result of early dissipations but which in reality shone with the innocence of early childhood﻿—glinted the triangular reflection of a green lamp. The fragrance of roasted apples spread and stung Martin’s nostrils, and he was bitterly annoyed that he had not in all ways the same views concerning this and the future life as Heringslake, for then he would surely have been offered an apple. From Auditor Camin’s place sounded for the hundredth time the old pronouncement, “The country will never be right till we make the farmers pay for shooting licenses.” And down at the bottom table off by the door, where it was draughty and there was a wet odor of umbrellas and overcoats, the youngest generation was eagerly at work putting in ticks and trying at the same time to recount in whispers the orgies of last night and the number of punch bottles emptied.

===Chinese===

桌上的灯不再摆动了，他回到他的账目上来。他不再满足于画勾了事；他仔细审看每一个条目，把每一栏都加一遍。他早年对机械事务的反感早已克服，而且渐渐懂得了：这些数目字并不像他起初以为的那样，全然免于一切人类事物固有的不完备。相反，它们常常缠夹着差错和失误；偶尔发现这种错误时，他心里高兴，同时却又掠过一丝悲凉。他高兴，因为他有了机会显示自己的勤勉，也因为他的机警为国家金库省下的那笔款项里，他可以指望分到他应得的那一份提成；而当他想起自己原本向生活要的是一种全然不同的快乐时，便觉出一种古老忧伤的阴沉记忆。有时他也想到兰斯克鲁纳（Landskrona）、奥胡斯（Ohus）或哈帕兰达（Haparanda）那些算错了账的可怜公务员，他们也许是在昨夜热酒的后劲里算错的，如今得自己赔出差额。可这念头打动不了他，因为岁月教会了他：同情心得有个限度。

屋里很暖和，瓷炉里一大炉桦木柴的余烬还红着；如今在家里都得省着烧的年头，政府的柴火自然没有省的道理。总务科长冯·赫林斯拉克（Von Heringslake）年俸四千六百克朗，凭着无需仰仗人的那份从容，愉快而闲适地当着他的差；这时他正蹲在炉子前，在余烬上烤苹果。他的秃顶——他的死对头审计官卡明（Camin）断言那是早年放荡的结果，实际上却透着幼年般的无辜——上映着一盏绿灯的三角反光。烤苹果的香气弥漫开来，刺着马丁的鼻孔；叫他气恼的是，自己在这辈子和来世的问题上没能处处同赫林斯拉克看法一致，不然准会有一个苹果分给他。审计官卡明的座位上第一百次响起那句老断言：“等咱们叫农民掏钱买打猎执照，这国家才有救。”而在靠门的里头那张桌子上——那里有过堂风，有一股雨伞和湿大衣的气味——最年轻的一辈正起劲地画着勾，一面交头接耳，低声复述昨夜的纵饮和喝空的潘趣酒瓶的数目。

===Original===

Martin was still young, for in government service one ages slowly, but he was no longer one of the youngest and did not have to sit in the draught of the door. He had drunk brotherhood with most of his immediate superiors and in his turn did not neglect the duty of laying aside formalities with those who were younger than he. These ceremonies were wont to be performed at a general banquet in December. This was to occur in a few days, and the list of subscriptions was now being circulated in the department, but Martin did not sign it. He had other uses for his money, and there was only one of the newcomers with whom he would have cared to drink brotherhood, a young man who had a place just opposite him at the same table and in whom there was something familiar and appealing to his sympathy: namely, an absent and dreamy glance and the mechanical gesture with which he set down the ticks. Martin often used to talk to him about the way of the world and was pleased when he sometimes received intelligent answers.

As he handed over the subscription list without writing on it himself, the other looked up and asked in a tone which seemed to convey a touch of disappointment, “Aren’t you coming to the banquet?”

“No,” answered Martin, “I have another engagement. But we who are above conventional forms can assume that we have drunk brotherhood just the same.”

The other blushed a little, and they shook hands across the table.

“Tell me,” the younger man asked after a while, “why does Auditor Camin want to charge the farmers for shooting licenses?”

“I don’t really believe he wants that,” Martin replied. “He knows that shooting licenses for the farmers would raise the price of necessities even more than taxes. He is only repeating an old saw that he heard in his youth when he was an assistant. It has stuck to him because it expresses a collective antipathy, a class hatred; and commonplace men always need to hate and love collectively. Look out for that, it is one of the surest signs of an inferior point of view. He likes women, officials, leading actors, and West Gothlanders, because he is a West Gothlander himself; and he hates farmers, Jews, Northlanders, and journalists. It is true that the farmers are a bit stingy in recognizing the services which he and the rest of us perform for our country, and that is why he hates them. But in that they observe the same principle as all employers of labor: to pay as little as competition will allow. If there was a shortage in clerks, they would pay more.”

===Chinese===

马丁还算年轻，在政府机关里人老得慢，可他已不是最年轻的一辈，不必再坐在门口的过堂风里了。他同直属的上司们大多喝过结义酒，轮到自己，也不曾怠慢同比他年轻的人撤去虚礼的义务。这些仪式照例在十二月的一次全体宴会上举行。再过几天就是了，认捐的单子正在科室里传着，可马丁没有签名。他的钱另有用处；新人里头他也只愿意同一个人喝结义酒——一个同他隔桌相对的年轻人，这人身上有一样叫他眼熟、引他同情的东西：一副心不在焉的、梦似的目光，还有落笔画勾时那机械的手势。马丁时常同他谈谈人情世故，偶尔得到几句聪明的回答，便觉得欢喜。

他把认捐单递过去，自己没有签名；对面那年轻人抬起头，用一种似乎带点失望的口气问：“你不来参加宴会吗？”

“不来了，”马丁答道，“我另有约会。不过咱们这些超乎俗礼的人，不妨就算已经喝过结义酒了。”

对方微微红了脸，两人隔着桌子握了握手。

“告诉我，”过了一会儿年轻人问道，“审计官卡明干吗要让农民掏钱买打猎执照？”

“我倒真不信他要那个，”马丁答道，“他知道给农民发打猎执照，会比加税更抬高日用品的价钱。他不过是重复一句老话，年轻时当助理听来的。这话粘在他身上，因为它道出了一种集体的憎恶，一种阶级仇恨；平庸的人总得成伙地恨、成伙地爱。留神这一点，这是趣味低级最可靠的征兆之一。他喜欢女人、公务员、名演员和西约特兰人，因为他自己是西约特兰人；他恨农民、犹太人、诺尔兰人和新闻记者。不错，农民们对他和咱们其余的人为国家效的力，承认得吝啬了些，所以他恨他们。可在这一点上，他们奉行的同一切雇工的原则一样：竞争容许多少，就付多少。要是文书写字员缺了，他们也会多付的。”

===Original===

Von Heringslake, who had by now eaten his roasted apples and resumed his place at the table next to Martin, turned on his chair and surveyed him mournfully.

“You have no heart,” he said.

---

It was after three o’clock; here and there the men were gathering up their papers and going off. Martin got up, took his coat and hat, put out his green lamp, and departed. He had crape on his hat, for his mother was dead.

===Chinese===

冯·赫林斯拉克这时已吃完了烤苹果，回到紧挨马丁的座位上，转过椅子，凄然地打量他。

“你没有心肝。”他说。

---

已经三点多了；人们这里那里地收拾文件散去了。马丁站起身，取了大衣和帽子，熄掉绿灯，走了出去。他帽子上缠着黑纱，因为他的母亲去世了。

### II / 二

===Original===

He turned into Long Western Street. On snowy days such as this he nearly always took that street, because in the narrow winding rift between the tall old houses one was as if half indoors, in the lee of the worst wind gusts.

“Winter, cold.﻿ ﻿… Strange there are people who assert that they like this weather. Heringslake, who has a heart in his breast and loves his native land, regards cold as preferable to heat. But when it’s cold, he always puts on furs. The conception of hell as a very warm place clearly originated in the torrid zone. If a northerner had invented it, it would have been contrariwise a fearful place for draughts, the breeding ground of influenza and chronic snuffles. But such as the climate is, I have got used to it, and it has possibly done me excellent service of which I myself am not aware. Provisions are laid on ice in order to keep; everything is preserved longer in cold. Why not human beings as well? I once longed to be consumed in the flame of a great passion. It never came, whether because I was not deserving of so great an honor, or whatever the reason may have been. But now, afterwards, I have begun to misdoubt that such a conflagration may rather be a bonfire to amuse the spectators than any real enjoyment for the chief actor. Fire is, in any case, distinctly not my element. If a real spring sun were ever to come into my life, I should go rotten at once from being unused to the climate.”

===Chinese===

他拐进了西长街。像这样的下雪天，他差不多总走这条街，因为在高大古老的楼房之间那条狭窄曲折的夹缝里，人就像半个身子在室内，避开了最凶的风头。

“冬天，冷。……真奇怪，竟有人声称喜欢这种天气。赫林斯拉克，胸口揣着一颗心、又热爱祖国的赫林斯拉克，认为冷比热好。可天一冷，他总要裹上皮袄。把地狱设想成一个极热的地方，这观念显然发源于热带。倘若是个北方人发明了它，地狱就该反过来是个处处过堂风的可怕去处，流感与常年鼻塞的温床。不过气候既是这样，我也已经习惯了，它说不定还为我效过极好的劳，只是我自己不知道罢了。食品搁在冰上是为了保存；寒冷里一切都保存得久些。人何尝不也一样？我曾渴望被一场大激情的火焰烧毁。它始终没有来，也许因为我不配有这么大的荣幸，也许另有缘故。可是如今，事过之后，我倒疑心那样一场大火，与其说是主角的什么真享受，不如说是给看客取乐的篝火。无论如何，火分明不是我的元素。倘若我生命里真会来上一个春天的太阳，我怕要因为不惯这种气候，立刻烂掉。”

===Original===

He stopped a moment in front of a jeweler’s window. Most of the pieces were distinguished by a commonplaceness which left him no regret that he could not purchase any. Once, indeed, it was just a year ago to the day, he had bought a little ring with a green emerald. She to whom it had been given still wore it and never wanted to wear any other ring. She said she shouldn’t ever want to wear a plain gold ring. Well, in any case he couldn’t offer her such a specimen.﻿ ﻿…

“I’m ungrateful,” he said to himself, “now that at last a little sunlight has come into my life, more maybe than comes into most. But I have been frozen too long; I haven’t been able to thaw out yet.”

He had come out on Mint Square, the northerly gale blew his eyes shut with the snow, and he felt his way along, half blind, toward North Bridge. He had to stop again to get breath at Looström’s bookstore, where the celebrities of the day were exhibited in the window: Crispi, King Milan, and Taine, while between an Excellency and a forger he discovered a face that looked familiar. It was a Swedish poet, the decadent who had expounded his ideas of life at the “Anglais” over the green chartreuse. He was not there because he was a great man but because he was dead.

Martin went on toward home.

===Chinese===

他在一家珠宝店的橱窗前站了一会儿。橱窗里的东西多半以平庸见长，倒叫他买不起也不觉得遗憾。只有一回——那正好是一年前的今天——他买过一枚镶绿宝石的小戒指。接受它的那个人至今还戴着它，再也不肯戴别的戒指。她说她永远不要戴素金戒指。可不是，无论如何他也送不起她那样的样品。……

“我不知好歹，”他自言自语道，“如今我生命里总算进来了一点阳光，也许比多数人的还多。可我冻得太久了；还没能化开呢。”

他走到了铸币广场，北风卷着雪打得他睁不开眼，他半盲地摸着往北桥走去。到卢斯特伦（Looström）书店门口，他又不得不停下来喘口气；橱窗里陈列着当今的名人：克里斯皮（Crispi）、米兰国王（King Milan）和泰纳（Taine），而在一位大人阁下和一个伪造犯之间，他发现一张脸有点眼熟。那是一位瑞典诗人，那个曾在“英国人”咖啡馆就着绿色查特酒宣讲他的人生哲学的颓废派。他陈列在那里，不是因为他伟大，而是因为他死了。

马丁继续朝家走。

===Original===

“At last a man who has reached his goal! His goal was a bit unusual, and he did not reach it quite as he imagined; he never got the general paralysis of his dream, for he died simply and modestly of consumption. But I don’t suppose he was so particular as to details; as a matter of fact he only wanted to succumb, no matter how. Perhaps he was right; that’s the sort of goal one ought to set for oneself if he hopes to reach it in his lifetime. It is true one might also propose to oneself to be a millionaire or a bishop or a member of the legislature, and that goal too one can usually reach if he really wants to. Those who know how to concentrate their will with sufficient intensity on a single object are so extremely few that the competition is by no means prohibitive. Everybody wants to be rich, but most men wish at the same time to live as if they were rich already; they want to take things easy, to have a nap after dinner, drink champagne with the girlies and so on, and so they never get rich, never even become bishops or members of the legislature. He who wants to stop on the road every now and then and enjoy life a bit before he reaches his objective will never reach it; and the others, the indefatigable pilgrims, the men of will who arrive﻿—what have they left afterwards when they get there?

“On the other hand it is possibly superfluous to expend any particular effort on the objective: to succumb. That is a goal which can certainly be attained at a cheaper price; it even comes near of itself, slowly and surely. The best thing is perhaps that which the other dead man over there in the bookshop window loved so much while he lived: a big tree and tranquil thoughts. For it is not quite true, what Messer Guido Cavalcanti said when he felt death approaching, that it is as vain to think as to act. In one way it is no doubt true: namely, that the final result will always be the same black pit, and as a meditation on death Messer Guido’s words have their value. But looked at from another point of view, it is clear that he who enjoys thinking is always in this world of incalculables in a slightly better position than a man of action. Because for him the minute has its worth in and for itself, independent of the uncertainties of the future. He who wishes to become a Knight of the Order of the Seraphim or a pope and gives up everything, the pleasures both of thought and of love, to attain that object﻿—and the first sacrifice at least is inevitable﻿—and then gets a fishbone in his throat and dies before he has reached it, his life is a nullity, an intention without performance. But he whose standard lies in thought may have his life cut off at any point and it will be like the snake of popular superstition, it will still live, it will have its value even as a fragment; nay, it has never, properly speaking, assumed that it wished to be anything but a fragment. For he who is measured by the standard of thought can never set himself any human goal, or if he does, this will be arbitrary and inessential, and it is a matter of no significance whether he reaches it or not.”

---

===Chinese===

“总算有一个人达到了他的目标！他的目标有点不同寻常，而且达到的方式也不尽如他所想；他不曾捞着他梦寐以求的全身瘫痪，只是简简单单、无声无息地死于痨病。可我想他对细节不会那么挑剔；实际上他不过想要完蛋，怎么完蛋倒在其次。也许他是对的；人若指望在有生之年达到目标，就该给自己定这样的目标。诚然，人也可以立志做百万富翁、主教或国会议员，这样的目标只要真想，通常也能达到。懂得把意志足够集中地凝在一个对象上的人少到极点，竞争因此绝不拥挤。人人都想发财，可多数人同时又想过得像已经发了财一样；他们想清闲，想午饭后打个盹，同姑娘们喝香槟，等等等等，于是永远发不了财，连主教、国会议员也永远当不上。谁想在半路上三番五次停下来，没到目的地先享受一点生活，谁就永远到不了；而另一种人，那些不知疲倦的朝圣者，那些到达了的意志坚强的人——到了之后，他们又剩下什么呢？

“另一方面，为‘完蛋’这个目标花费特别的气力也许是多余的。这个目标肯定能更便宜地达到；它甚至自己就会慢慢地、稳稳地凑上来。最好的，也许还是书店橱窗那边的另一位死者生前深爱的那样东西：一棵大树和安静的思绪。因为梅瑟·圭多·卡瓦尔坎蒂（Guido Cavalcanti）感到死亡临近时说的那句话——想同做一样虚空——并不全对。从一个方面说无疑是对的：最终结果总是同一个黑洞，作为死亡的沉思，圭多先生的话自有其价值。可从另一个角度看，明白得很：在这个充满不可计算之数的世界上，以思考为乐的人总比行动家略占一点便宜。因为对他来说，每一分钟自身就有它的价值，不系于未来的无常。谁想当塞拉芬勋章骑士或教皇，为此舍弃一切、思想和爱情的快乐都在所不惜——至少头一种牺牲是免不了的——结果一根鱼刺卡了喉咙，目标没达到就死了，那他这一生就是一个零，一个没有履行的意图。而以思想为尺度的人，生命随便在哪一点上被截断都行，它会像民间迷信里的蛇一样，截断了还活着，作为残片也自有其价值；不，说正经的，它压根就没打算过要做别的，只想做一片残片。因为以思想为尺度的人，永远不能给自己立任何人间目标；就算立了，那目标也是任意的、无关紧要的，达到与否毫不要紧。”

---

===Original===

Martin had got up to Östermalm and was almost home; he was hungry and was eager for his dinner, yet he stopped at a street corner and looked up toward a window high up in a fourth story.

Yes, there was a light there; she was home then. He knew that already, anyhow, and he knew besides that she expected him after dinner. In the evening they were to go to a theater together; they were to sit in a stage box behind a screen where nobody could see them.

He had taken a mistress. Chance had brought them together. She worked in a life insurance office in the morning counting money. She worked for her living. She had, to be sure, an old father somewhere off in the country, a pensioned forester who wrote her letters three times a year; but she was self-supporting and depended upon no one. Like other young girls she had dreamed of a happiness which should be correct, and had guarded her jewel in the hope of being married. She had had her fancies and been in love with men who had not even noticed it. But these small flames had gone out when they had no fuel, and if a man not too ridiculous or repulsive had wished to offer her his hand, she could easily have persuaded herself that she loved him. But she had seen the years run away; she had danced in the winter and bicycled in the summer, and many men had let her divine by their looks and veiled words that they would gladly possess her; but no one had wanted to marry her, for she had no dowry and did not belong to a family with influence. The more economical and diffident of the men, moreover, were frightened by her elegance, for she had a sure and delicate taste and two industrious hands, and many a night she sat up by her lamp and sewed cheap remnants and old shreds into dresses, which later gave to inexperienced eyes the impression of having cost a great deal, or to the more skeptical-minded even suggested a doubt of her virtue. She was not, however, beautiful enough for the men whose feelings were governed by their vanity, nor did her nature have anything of the sweet and docile quality fitted to attract men who wished to be lords in their own home, men who had simply tired of bachelor life and therefore looked about for a nice and charming and modest and obedient wife.

===Chinese===

马丁已经上了奥斯特马尔姆，快到家了；他饿了，急着要吃晚饭，可他还是在街角停了一下，抬头朝四楼高处的一扇窗望上去。

不错，那里有灯；那么她在家。反正这他早知道，他还知道她等他饭后过去。晚上他们要一起去看戏；要坐在舞台侧面的包厢里，隔着一道屏风，谁也看不见他们。

他有了一个情妇。是机遇把他们带到一起的。她上午在一个人寿保险公司里数钱。她自食其力。不错，乡下什么地方还有个老父亲，一个领养老金的林务官，一年给她写三封信；可她是自立的，不靠任何人。像别的年轻姑娘一样，她梦想过一份规矩体面的幸福，守着自己的珍宝，盼着嫁人。她有过种种痴想，爱过一些根本没有觉察的男人。可这些小小的火苗没有柴，也就熄了；倘若有一个不太可笑、不太叫人反感的男人肯向她求婚，她本不难说服自己是爱他的。可她眼看着年头一年年跑掉；她冬天跳舞，夏天骑车，多少男人用眼色和藏着的话让她猜出他们乐意占有她；可谁也不想娶她，因为她没有嫁妆，也不属于有势力的门第。再说，那些手头俭省、心思怯懦的男人，又让她那份体面吓住了：她的趣味确实而精致，一双手勤快，多少个夜里她坐在灯下，把便宜的零头料和旧布头缝成衣裙，日后叫没经验的人看来像是花过大价钱的，叫多疑的人看了甚至疑心她的贞操。可是她长得又不够美，够不上那些感情受虚荣摆布的男人；她的天性里也没有那种甜蜜驯顺的品质，去吸引那些要在自家屋里称王的男人，那些单单过腻了独身生活、因此想找一个又好看又可人又端庄又听话的太太的男人。

===Original===

Both her own character and her outer circumstances were such that she had no great prospect of being loved for any other reason than love, and she had gradually begun to suspect that this feeling, of which so much was said and written, was really scorned and put to one side so that it was extremely rare. She had thought over all this, she had felt the minutes running through her fingers like sand, and had decided that the years to come would be still more wretched and worthless than those before and that the jewel she guarded was losing its value every day. Most of all she had been frightened at how quickly women age who live without men, except those who are so fortunate as not to feel any strong desire or lack. But she was not of these; no, she was a real woman and she knew she was. The desire which in her first youth had only been a sweet and indefinite longing, a dream of happiness of a strange and unknown sort, now burned in her veins like poison; and her first timid girlish fancy, which had hardly dared to look beyond a kiss in the twilight between bushes of roses, had developed with years into a hobgoblin much worse than those used in children’s picture-books to frighten naughty boys. Her glance became wistful and yearning, and she tried to bring herself to a decision.

She had almost given up hope of a husband; it was a lover she was seeking, and even him she sought for long in vain. It was not that there was a lack of men who would take her out to dance; there were on the contrary many, and she could make a choice. She looked around in her circle; she flirted right and left. She grew less afraid about her reputation than before and went to secret rendezvous with men who had been attentive to her some evening at a ball. But they remained strange to her, and every time an understanding was in the air, she was overcome with shame and became suddenly icy with fear and repugnance. For every time when the critical moment came, she read in the man’s eyes the ineradicable crudity of his heart. She read it as plainly as if it had stood written on white paper that what was for her a wholly new experience in life﻿—perhaps ruin, perhaps salvation﻿—was for him an amorous adventure. She read that what she was about to do was in his eyes merely a faux pas, which he could overlook only in so far as it gave him pleasure; and she read that not only did he intend to give her up very soon, but that he also meant to salve his conduct beforehand by showing her his contempt. She saw all this and tired of the game before it had begun, asking herself if she might not just as well follow the path of virtue, which in any case was clearly the most convenient, and wither into old age without will and without hope.

But when she met Martin all this became different, and when she gave herself to him she felt no more fear, because she saw that he had understood her, that his thoughts were not like those of the others, and she felt that he loved her. With him she felt no shame, nor did she feign any, for she had already sinned so much in her thoughts that the reality seemed to her innocent and pure. She was no longer young; she was getting on toward thirty, just as he was. Her complexion had already been marked by the early frost, and vanished illusions had made her bitter at heart and crude of speech. But the bitter heart beat warm and fast when it rested on his, and the ugly words did not make her mouth less sweet to kiss.

===Chinese===

她的性格和处境都是如此：除了为爱而爱，她没有多大指望被人爱；渐渐地她开始疑心，这种人人说、人人写的感情，其实是受轻贱、被撇在一旁的，因此稀有之极。这一切她都想过了；她感到光阴像沙子从指缝里漏走，便断定往后的年头只会比先前的更凄凉、更不值钱，她守着的那件珍宝一天天在跌价。最叫她害怕的，是那些没有男人一起生活的女人老得多么快——除非运气好，感觉不到强烈的欲求或缺欠的那种。可她不属于这类；不，她是个真正的女人，她也知道自己是的。初时青春里那种不过是甜蜜而朦胧的渴望、对一种陌生未知之幸福的梦想的欲，如今像毒药一样在她血管里烧；她起初那种少女的怯生生的空想——那种在玫瑰丛间的暮色里几乎不敢多想一个吻的空想——已随着年岁长成了一个妖怪，比儿童画册里吓唬顽皮孩子的妖怪坏得多。她的目光变得惆怅而渴望，她努力让自己拿定主意。

丈夫她差不多已经不指望了；她要找的是个情人，可连情人她也找了好久没有找到。倒不是缺少肯带她出去跳舞的男人；相反多得很，尽可以挑。她在自己的圈子里张望；她到处调情。她对名声的顾虑比从前少了，同在舞会上某个晚上献过殷勤的男人去赴秘密的约会。可他们始终与她隔膜，每回眼看要默契了，羞耻便压倒她，恐惧和厌恶使她突然冷若冰霜。因为每回到了紧要关头，她都能从那男人的眼睛里读出他心里那种拔除不掉的粗鄙。她读得清清楚楚，就像白纸黑字写着：对她是一番全新的人生经验——也许是毁灭，也许是得救——的事，对他不过是一场艳遇。她读出她将要做的事在他眼里只是一个失足，他能宽容它，只因为它给他快乐；她还读出他不但打算很快就甩掉她，而且存心事先就拿轻蔑给她看一看，好开脱自己的行径。这一切她都看见了，戏还没开场她就腻了，问自己是不是索性走美德那条路算了——反正那条路明摆着最省事——无志无望地枯萎到老。

可是遇见马丁之后，这一切都变了。她委身于他时不再害怕，因为她看出他懂她，他的想法同别的男人不一样，她感到他是爱她的。同他在一起她不觉得羞耻，也不装假，因为她在思想上已经犯了那么多罪，现实反倒显得无辜而纯洁。她不再年轻；她快三十了，同他一样。她的脸色已经着了早霜，幻影的破灭使她心底发苦、说话尖刻。可是这颗发苦的心贴着他的心时，跳得又热又快；难听的字眼也没有使她的嘴不那么好吻。

### III / 三

===Original===

Martin sat alone with his father at the dinner table within the same circle of yellow light which had enclosed the sleepy winter evenings of his childhood. Martin Birck and his father had seldom anything to say to each other. They thought differently about everything except the taxes on foodstuffs. This lack of agreement did not, however, cause them any sorrow; they attached no importance to it. They both knew that different generations think differently, and they found this natural. Nor did they find silence anything painful or oppressive; it was just the self-evident expression of the fact that nothing had happened which could give rise to an exchange of opinions. When they chatted together it was mostly about the improvement of government work and about new houses. For Martin’s father was interested in his city. On Sundays he often went for long walks to distant parts of the city and saw how new suburbs shot up out of the earth. He thought of how Stockholm had developed since his youth, and he found all the new houses handsome, especially if they were large and imposing with many windows and small towers at the corners. And when Martin heard his father speak of all these ugly houses and call them handsome, he thought of how unjust life was, since it remorselessly closed the way to the inner regions of beauty for the best and most useful members of the community. For the way thither went through melancholy, there was no other, and it was not idly that the Greek musician answered Alexander, “May the gods never make you so unhappy, my lord, that you may learn to understand music better than I.” Martin’s father had had a youth too full of worry and a manhood too full of strenuous responsibility to know anything of the mental depression with which life punishes those who think more about beautiful and ugly and good and evil than they do about their daily bread.

---

===Chinese===

马丁同父亲单独坐在餐桌旁，还是童年困倦的冬夜里围着的那一圈黄灯光。马丁·比尔克同父亲彼此难得有什么可说的。除了食品税，他们对一切的想法都不一样。这种不合倒并不使他们难过；他们不把它当回事。两人都知道，不同的一辈人想法不同，而且觉得这很自然。他们也不觉得沉默有什么痛苦、什么难堪；它不过是不言自明地表示：没有发生什么可以交换意见的事。他们聊起来，多半是机关公务的改良和新房子。因为马丁的父亲关心他的城市。礼拜天他常作长距离的散步，走到城里偏远的地方，看新的郊区怎样从地里冒出来。他想着斯德哥尔摩从他年轻以来有了怎样的发展，觉得这些新房子都好看，尤其是那些高大气派、开许多窗、四角带小塔楼的。马丁听父亲说这些丑房子、还夸它们好看时，便想人生多么不公道：它把通往美的内殿的路，对社团里最好、最有用的成员毫不留情地堵死了。因为通往那里的路要经过忧郁，没有别的路；那位希腊乐师回答亚历山大的话也不是白说的：“愿诸神永远别让您不幸到那个地步，我的主上，让您把音乐懂得比我更好。”马丁的父亲青年时代忧愁太满，成年后艰辛的责任太满，因此全然不知道生活加于那些把美丑善恶想得比每日面包还多的人的惩罚——精神的抑郁。

---

===Original===

On this day, as usual, Martin’s father discoursed about one thing and another over his coffee and cigar. He spoke of a men’s dinner he had attended the day before, where he had felt embarrassed on account of his Vasa decoration; for he had gone with the large official medal, which was the only one he had, whereas the other men had had the small miniatures.

“So,” he finished, “I looked like the biggest fool of the company.”

“Yes,” observed Martin, “appearances were clearly against you. But in reality the miniature medals of the others gave the clearest proof that their foolishness was greater, since because of their decoration they went to more expense than was strictly necessary.”

“Yes,” his father answered, “I thought of that too, but I felt awkward, anyhow.”

The conversation died down. Martin was thinking of various stories about decorations which he had heard, such as that about a man who had been given the Vasa medal because he had sent flowers to the royal hospital on the days when the queen was to visit it, and about one who got the North Star because he had bought a house. But it never occurred to him to tell these, because when he thought the matter over he could see that these stories, which he found so amusing, might not have quite the same effect on the elder man, who had earned his decoration by forty years of ill-paid work in the government service and could therefore hardly fail to think of it without some respect, although in conversation he might make fun of it.

===Chinese===

这一天，马丁的父亲照例就着咖啡和雪茄东一件西一件地聊。他讲起前一天参加的一个男人宴会，说他在那儿因为那枚瓦萨勋章发了窘：他佩的是大号官章——他只有这一枚——别的先生们佩的都是小小的迷你章。

“所以，”他末了说，“我看起来像全席最大的傻瓜。”

“是啊，”马丁说，“表面显然对您不利。可实际上，别人那些迷你章恰恰最清楚地证明他们更傻：为了勋章，他们花的钱超出了严格必需的数目。”

“是啊，”父亲答道，“这我也想到了，可我到底觉得别扭。”

话头断了。马丁想起他听来的各种关于勋章的故事，比如有个人因为在王后预定视察王室医院的日子往医院送花而得了瓦萨勋章，还有一个人因为买了一所房子得了北极星勋章。可他从未想过要讲这些，因为他细细一想就明白：这些他自己觉得那么逗乐的故事，对长辈未必有同样的效果；老人的勋章是四十年俸禄微薄的政府工作挣来的，他想到它时很难不怀几分敬意，尽管谈起来也可以拿它打趣。

===Original===

Silence spread out around them; the father smoked his cigar and looked out into the dark, and Martin sat in thought. He thought of the history of his home, how it, like other homes, had come into existence, grown and blossomed, and how afterwards the bonds had one after another been broken: his sister married, his mother dead. The best time, the blossom time, was mostly that when the children had just grown up and the elders were not really old. It was true he had heard old women say that the happiest time was when the children were small. Yes, that might well be﻿—for the mothers. But he remembered the years when his sister had just grown up and was about to be married. Then everything was glad in the home; they had youth, friends, music. The piano, which now was dumb, still held the waltzes and opera selections of the bygone years; and often when he lay awake at night, he could still hear the Norwegian songs they sang then: “He Leaned Above the Garden Bench” and “I Ask Thee Not for Roses from Thy Breast.” In these songs still lived a part of his youth, and they now seemed full of all the strange melancholy of the past. Then suddenly the house had became silent, more silent with every year, till one day the father sat alone with the son in an empty and shattered home.

Looking at his father, he asked himself, “What can I be to him?” “Infinitely little,” he had to answer, “almost nothing.” She whom he had loved from his youth up now lay under the earth, under a little snow-covered gray stone, and could not warm his age. The fire on the hearth was ready to die out. *He* was the one whose duty it was to kindle the new flame. He felt it was this which, in the normal course of things, the elders of the family had the right to expect of the young: to see the chain carried on, a new home, and grandchildren to rock on their knees. It was so that nature had arranged, she tried everywhere to hide the dead with new young life, as we ourselves cover corpses under flowers. Dissolution was thus more easily approached; the way went downward, to be sure, but one took it amid play and prattle, as when one started the journey. But to that great and simple craving he could answer nothing. It was true he could do several things: he did not think there was any sort of beauty in the world that was foreign to him, or any thought or shade of a thought that he could not follow, and furthermore he could look over government ledgers and inscribe signs in the margins, and drink a good deal of whisky without losing control of his mind, and perhaps a few other small matters. But he could not build a home. Not a chance, not a possibility of it. An artisan, a day laborer could do it, but not he. He could not conjure forth the four thousand crowns a year that a poor family of the middle class needed to live. If he could ever get to that point, as he well might with years, he would be old, his father dead, and she whom he loved﻿—what would have become of her?

===Chinese===

沉默在他们四周铺开来；父亲抽着雪茄，望着外面黑黢黢的夜，马丁坐在那里想心事。他想着这个家的历史：它怎样像别的家一样生成、长大、开花，随后一条条纽带又怎样先后断掉：姐姐出嫁了，母亲死了。最好的时光，花开的时光，多半是儿女刚刚长大、长辈还不算老的那些年。不错，他听老妇人说过，最幸福的是孩子还小的那些年。是的，那多半是真的——对母亲们而言。可他记得姐姐刚长大、快要出嫁的那几年。那时家里样样都欢喜；他们有青春，有朋友，有音乐。那架如今哑了的钢琴，当年还存着往昔岁月的圆舞曲和歌剧选段；他夜里睡不着时，还常常听见他们当时唱的那些挪威歌：《他俯身在花园长凳上》，《我不向你索取胸前的玫瑰》。他的青春有一部分还活在这些歌里，如今它们充满了往昔全部奇异的忧郁。后来这幢房子忽然就静了，一年比一年更静，直到有一天，父亲同儿子单独坐在一个空落落的、散了架的家里。

他望着父亲，问自己：“我能为他做什么呢？”“少得可怜，”他不得不回答，“几乎什么也不能。”他从青年时代起就爱的那个人如今躺在地底下，一块覆着雪的小小灰色石碑下面，再不能温暖他的晚年了。炉膛里的火眼看要熄了。*他*才是那个该点燃新火焰的人。他感到，这正是按常理家中的长辈有权向年轻人期待的：眼看香火有人接续，有一个新的家，有孙儿可在膝上摇晃。造化就是这样安排的，它处处设法用新的年轻生命把死者掩过去，就像我们自己用花盖住尸体一样。这样一来，消解便容易走近些；路是往下的，不错，可人是在嬉戏和牙牙学语中走完它的，正如启程时一样。可对这巨大而简单的渴望，他无从回答。不错，他会做不少事情：他不认为世上有哪一种美是他陌生的，有哪一种思想、哪一种思想的色阶他跟不上的；此外他还会查政府的账簿，在页边作记号，还能喝下不少威士忌而不失掉理智，也许还有些别的小本事。可他不会成个家。一点指望、一点可能都没有。一个手艺人、一个做日工的都办得到，他却办不到。他变不出一年四千克朗来——一个中等人家的穷日子少不了这个数目。等他真熬到那一步——随着年岁他多半熬得到——他也老了，父亲死了，而他所爱的那个人——她又成了什么样子呢？

===Original===

But it was true, he realized, that the old man did not, at least not consciously, make any such demand on him. On the contrary his father understood clearly how impossible it was. He had no hope of seeing a continuation of his line, of being able to grow old in an environment of futurity and promise and new scions. But Martin realized that just this, the fact that he could have no such hope, weighed upon him like a dark sorrow and made his twilight even more gray and empty. He had had grief enough without that. He had received small pleasure from his daughter’s marriage. Her little boy was dead, and she had lately written home that she wanted a divorce from her husband.

“The fire is dying on the hearth. Who is to kindle the new flame?”

His father went into his room for his after-dinner nap.

It was five, and Martin dressed to go to her who was waiting for him. He put on an evening suit despite the fact that they were to be alone and unseen. He had promised her that, for it was their bridal anniversary.

===Chinese===

可他也明白，老人至少没有有意识地向他提出这种要求。相反，父亲清清楚楚懂得这是不可能的。他不指望看见自己的血脉有人接续，不指望能在一种充满将来、充满指望、充满新枝的环境里养老。可马丁明白，恰恰是这个——他不可能有这样的指望——像一桩阴沉的伤心事压在老人身上，把他的暮年弄得更加灰暗、更加空虚。没有这个，他的伤心事已经够多了。女儿的婚姻没给他多少快乐。她的小男孩死了，近来她又写信回家，说要同丈夫离婚。

“炉膛里的火在熄灭。谁来点燃新的火焰呢？”

父亲进自己屋里睡饭后觉去了。

五点了，马丁换衣服，要到等他的那个人那里去。他穿上晚礼服，尽管他们要单独待着，谁也看不见。这是他答应过她的，因为今天是他们定情的周年纪念日。

### IV / 四

===Original===

She stood at her dressing table, where two narrow candles burned before the mirror. She had just arranged her rich brown hair, and before she finished her toilet she touched her face with a powder puff to subdue the color. He sat behind her in a corner of the sofa, but their glances met in the mirror and were fixed on each other in a long smile. The trembling of the candle flames and the distance, which the mirror lengthened, made this smile dark and mysterious. And far within the dusky depth behind the glass danced a green spark from the emerald on her finger.

“Shall you be ready soon?” he asked. “It’s half-past seven. I’m afraid we shall miss the ghost.”

It was *Hamlet* they were to see.

She turned and stroked his cheek with the powder puff, so that he became as white as a Pierrot.

“Silly Pierrette,” he said, wiping off the powder with her handkerchief, “don’t you see I’m pale enough as it is?”

===Chinese===

她站在梳妆台前，镜子两侧点着两支细细的蜡烛。她刚梳好那一头浓密的棕发，梳妆未完，先拿粉扑往脸上按了按，压一压颜色。他坐在她身后的沙发角落里，但两人的目光在镜中相遇，含着长长的笑意彼此凝住。烛焰的颤动，加上镜子拉长了的距离，使这笑容显得幽暗而神秘。而在玻璃后面昏暗的深处，她手指上的绿宝石迸出一点绿色的火星，在那里跳舞。

“快好了吗？”他问，“七点半了。我怕咱们要错过那个鬼魂了。”

他们要去看的是《哈姆雷特》（*Hamlet*）。

她转过身，用粉扑抹了一下他的脸，把他抹得白得像个皮埃罗（Pierrot）。

“傻皮埃蕾特，”他说着，用她的手绢擦掉粉，“你没看见我本来就够苍白了么？”

===Original===

She leaned down, pressed his head to her breast, and kissed his hair.

“I am so happy,” she whispered, “because it is my bridal day today, and because I am going to the theater with you to sit in a little nook where no one can see us.”

He caressed her hand softly. He felt a secret stab in the heart when he heard her speak so, for he knew almost to a certainty that if there had been any chance of it she would much rather have sat with him in a place where all could see them. But he did not believe that she had been thinking of this just now. Never during the past year had she let fall an allusion to marriage, and she knew only too well how impossible it was. But he on his part could never cease to feel it as a secret disgrace that it was not in his power to give her the happiness which belonged to a secure and respected social position where she would not need to conceal anything from the world. He felt thus not because there remained in a corner of his soul any idea of a duty to be performed or of any transgression that ought to be atoned for, but because he was infinitely fond of her and could have wished to make life bright for her eyes and smooth for her little foot, which had such stony paths to go that it was not surprising if at last it had trodden a bit awry.

He dismissed these thoughts, however; he did not mean to attempt the impossible; he was no strong man who could take her in his arms and break a way for them both. And she had made her own choice. She had known strong men too, the kind of men of whom women commonly say, “He’s a real man”; if she had wished she might have given her love to one of them, and he would not have despised it. But her deepest instinct had held her back with forebodings of shame and unhappiness. For, strangely enough, it was precisely the strong men who rarely acted as he could have wished to do had he been able; they were strong just because in the crisis, when there was really something at stake, their feelings always formed an alliance with their profit, and they usually knew where best to employ their strength. No, he and she had nothing else to do, lonely and chilled as they were, than gratefully and without any yearning for the impossible to warm themselves at the happiness which had fallen into their hands, blessing the day when they were driven together by the voice of their blood, which told them that they suited each other and could bring each other joy. Secretly, however, he often liked to dwell on the remote vision that some day many years hence he might be able to give her a home. The thought that by then she would be already an old woman did not frighten him. He had the feeling that, no matter how fast time flew, even if she had gray hair and wrinkles around her eyes, her young white body could never become old﻿—it would still remain young and warm as now; and no matter how the years passed and winter after winter snowed under his youth and stung his soul and his thoughts with needles of ice, his heart would always be warm as now to the beating of hers, and that always when the two met there would spring up a spark of the sacred fire which warms all the world.

===Chinese===

她俯下身，把他的头搂在胸前，吻他的头发。

“我多快活，”她低声说，“因为今天是我的婚日，因为我就要同你一起去看戏，坐在一个谁也看不见我们的小角落里。”

他温柔地抚着她的手。听她这样说，他心里感到一阵秘密的刺痛，因为他几乎有把握知道：倘若有一线可能，她宁可同他坐在一个人人看得见他们的地方。但他相信她此刻并没有想这个。过去一年里她从没有漏出过一句关于结婚的话，而且她太清楚这是多么不可能。可是在他这方面，他始终不能不感到这是一种秘密的耻辱：他没有力量给她那种幸福——一个安稳而受人尊敬的社会位置的幸福，一个无须对世人隐瞒任何事的位置。他所以这样，并不是因为他灵魂的角落里还剩着什么须尽的义务感、什么须赎的罪愆，而是因为他无限地爱她，但愿能把人生弄得让她的眼睛觉得明亮，让她的小脚觉得平坦——那只脚走过的石头路那么多，到头来稍稍走歪一步也就不足为怪了。

不过他还是把这些念头打发开了；他不想去尝试不可能的事；他不是那种能把她搂在怀里、替两个人劈开一条路的强者。何况是她自己做的选择。强者她也认识，就是女人们通常说“他是个真正的男人”的那种男人；她要是愿意，本可以把爱给那中间的一个，那人也不会看轻这份爱。可是她最深本能里的预感——羞耻与不幸的预感——把她拦住了。因为说来奇怪，恰恰是那些强者，很少肯像他倘若能够时所愿的那样行事；他们之所以强，正是因为到了紧要关头、真的有什么押上赌注的时候，他们的感情总会同他们的利益结成同盟，而且他们通常知道把力气往哪里使最合算。不，他和她，两个这样孤独、这样寒冷的人，除了就着落进他们手里的这份幸福感恩地取取暖、不再渴想不可能的东西，再没有别的路好走，只有祝福那一天——血的声音把他们赶到一起，那声音告诉他们：他们彼此相配，能够彼此给与快乐。然而私下里，他时常爱沉入一个遥远的幻景：多年之后的某一天，他兴许能够给她一个家。想到那时她已是老妇，他并不害怕。他有一种感觉：无论时间飞得多快，哪怕她头发白了、眼角起了皱纹，她年轻白皙的身体永远不会老——它还会像现在这样年轻、这样温暖；无论年月怎样过去，一个又一个冬天把他的青春埋进雪里、用冰针扎他的灵魂和思想，他的心对着她的心的跳动，永远会像现在这样温暖，而且两人每回相遇，总会迸出一点温暖着全世界的圣火的火星。

===Original===

While he was thinking all this, his eyes were following every motion of her slender white arms before the mirror. Again his smile sought hers, she nodded to him with a glimmer of secret happiness in her color underneath the powder, and deep within the dusk he saw his own face, the features sharpened to a mask-like quality by the candlelight, nodding in answer like a Chinese doll.

“There’s no hurry,” she said. “In any case we can’t creep into our little corner before a good bit of the first act is over; otherwise we might meet acquaintances in the lobby.”

“That’s true, you are right,” he answered.

He had thought of that himself too.

“One must have one’s wits about one in such a position as ours,” she nodded. “It’s a different thing from sitting with one’s nose down over a book. But isn’t it almost like magic, when one thinks about it, that we’ve actually been left in peace a whole year and that nobody knows anything? I even think people speak less badly about me now than they used to. Everybody has got so friendly toward me: the manager, the clerks, and the girls in the office. But perhaps that’s because I’ve become prettier﻿—haven’t I? They certainly see I’m happy, and that makes them kindly disposed, so that they are cheerful and nice to me without suspecting why. If they knew!﻿—”

Martin didn’t like to hear her talk of their happiness. It was a different thing to read it in her eyes and her color and to feel it in her kisses; he believed in it then, and no text could be more precious to interpret than that.
