Martin didn’t know whether *he* was the murderer or the murdered, but he fancied he heard steps on the bridge and didn’t dare to look around. Johan, however, who had a cheerful disposition, drove off his fear by striking up a jolly song. He sang to the tune of “There was an old woman by Konham Square”:

> “I go to my death wherever I go, *killivillivippombom*!”

And Martin joined in and sang along with him.

But when they got to the bridge-tender’s house, Johan was silent while Martin sang at the top of his voice:

> “I go to my death wherever I go, *killivillivippombom*!”

===Chinese===

守桥人老莫贝里有个帮手叫约翰（Johan）。约翰十四五岁，很快成了马丁最好的朋友。他给马丁做弓箭、做树皮船，马丁帮他绞吊桥。晚上得了空，他还常同马丁、玛丽亚和另外几个孩子捉迷藏、玩“树林里没有强盗”。可马丁所以那么喜欢约翰、那么了不得地佩服他，既不是为了树皮船，也不是为了这些游戏。是因为约翰总有那么多奇妙的事情好讲，都是爸爸妈妈外祖母从来不讲的。尤其是在黄昏时分，约翰的话就多起来；那时马丁同他坐在桥口的一根梁上，等轮船开来，船的灯迟早会从海角后面先绿后红地跳出来。这种时候，约翰什么都可以讲。这一回讲老莫贝里，他常看见小魔鬼在他的热酒杯里蹦上蹦下、蹦上蹦下；他坐着自言自语、搅着杯子的时候，讲的就是它们。可洛沃岛（Lovö）的牧师更邪乎。嗐，他是老斑点本人的朋友，全教区都知道。谁肯想一想都看得出来：不然他怎么上得讲坛，那样一讲一整个钟头；他哪来那么多话？况且约翰有一回上他家跑腿，进过他的屋子，亲眼看见书从地板摞到顶棚，塞得满满的。哦，可不，他跟老家伙真是一伙的！——再不，约翰就讲一个人，三年前在大路上叫人谋害了，就在近处，他把那地方说得一丝不差：“就是那儿，一边木头密得很，另一边一根电线杆子旁有棵柳树。是十一月里一个傍晚出的事，如今谁要是赶上时辰从那儿过，就能听见沟里最可怕的呻吟——可凶手一直没抓着。”

马丁听到这种事，就紧紧贴住约翰的胳膊；等轮船的灯从黑暗里照出来、越来越近，听见机器扑通扑通响、船长喊口令、他们得赶紧去绞吊桥时，他心里才轻快些。回家的路上过了桥，两个人都让鬼魂和凶杀的念头烧得兴奋，约翰对马丁说：“听，他追咱们来了！”

马丁分不清*他*是凶手还是被杀的，只觉得听见桥上有脚步声，不敢回头。好在约翰天性快活，放声唱起一支快活的歌把害怕赶跑了。他套着“孔哈姆广场有个老太婆”的调子唱：

> “我走到哪儿死就跟到哪儿，*killivillivippombom*！”

马丁也跟着一起唱。

可走到守桥人的房子跟前，约翰不出声了，马丁还扯着嗓子唱：

> “我走到哪儿死就跟到哪儿，*killivillivippombom*！”

===Original===

The bridge-tender, old Moberg, was sitting on his porch, which was embowered in hop vines, drinking toddy with two farmers in the light of a round Japanese lantern. He was an old man who drank toddy every evening, and people said he couldn’t last much longer. But he was most unwilling to die. If he heard anyone speak of illness or death, it was to him as if he had heard something indecent, or indeed it was much worse, for indecent talk rather raised his spirits than offended his ears. But when he saw Martin coming along the road and heard him singing a funeral hymn to the tune of an insolent street song, he got up and advanced along the road with tottering steps till he halted in front of Martin. Martin stopped too and was silent directly. He looked around for Johan, but Johan had vanished.

Old Moberg had become blue in the face, as he said in a trembling voice: “And this child is supposed to come of respectable people! These are strange times, I may say.”

Thereupon he went into the house, without either drinking his toddy or saying good night to the farmers, and went to bed.

But Martin was left alone on the road, and everything around him had become silent all of a sudden. He heard only the sound of the farmers’ sticks as they went off in the dark without speaking.

Martin’s parents, however, had heard the whole affair from the veranda on the side of the house.

“Martin, come in!”

Martin was as red as his collar was white. Now he’d have to give an account of who had taught him to sing such things. But he said he had thought of it himself. Father explained to Martin how dreadfully he had behaved, and Martin cried and was sent to bed. His mother cried too when she said prayers with him. She was frightened and wrought up. For children’s offenses, like those of adults, are judged more according to the scandal they have aroused than according to their inner nature, and Martin’s misdeed had caused a terrible scandal.

---

===Chinese===

守桥人老莫贝里正坐在门廊里，门廊埋在啤酒花藤里，他同一对农民就着一盏圆的日本灯笼的光喝热酒。他是个天天傍晚喝热酒的老人，人们说他撑不了多久了。可他最不愿意死。谁若当着他讲起病啊死啊，他就像听见了什么下流话，不，比下流话还要糟得多——下流话倒提他的兴致，并不刺他的耳朵。可这回他看见马丁沿路走来，听见他拿一支无赖的街头小调的调子唱葬礼赞美诗，便站起身，颤颤巍巍地顺着路迎上来，在马丁面前站住。马丁也站住了，立刻不作声。他回头找约翰，约翰已经没影了。

老莫贝里脸都憋青了，声音发抖地说：“这孩子还是好人家的种呢！这年头真是邪了，我说。”

说完他进了屋，热酒也不喝了，同两个农民晚安也没道，就上床去了。

马丁一个人留在路上，四周的一切忽然都静了。只听见两个农民拄着棍子、一声不响走进黑暗里去的笃笃声。

可这件事，马丁的父母从房子侧面的游廊上全听见了。

“马丁，进来！”

马丁的脸红得像他的领子那样白。这下他得交代是谁教他唱这种东西了。可他说是自己想出来的。父亲给马丁讲明他行为多么恶劣，马丁哭了，被打发上床。母亲同他做祷告时也哭了。她又惊又气。因为孩子的过错同大人的过错一样，受审判的与其照它内里的性质，不如照它惹起的风波；而马丁的坏事闯下了可怕的大祸。

---

===Original===

The most beautiful days of summer were gone. In the daytime there was rain and wind, and the lake turned green. And at dusk the crows flapped around the slope with the oaks and the naked tree.

When it rained, Martin was set to read “The Bee and the Dove” and “The Toad and the Ox.” He read too “Tiny’s Trip to Dreamtown.”

===Chinese===

夏天最美的日子过去了。白天有雨有风，湖变绿了。黄昏时，乌鸦绕着橡树坡和那棵秃树扑棱棱地飞。

下雨天，大人就让马丁读《蜜蜂与鸽子》《癞蛤蟆与公牛》。他还读了《小不点儿游梦城》。

===Original===

> “Little gold fishes in goodly row

 Swim through the silver sea there.

 Tiny is off to Dreamtown, ho!

 Ere it is night he’ll be there.

> “Soon, soon

 Close to the moon

 He sees its outline fleeting.

 Bright, bright

 Many a light

 Sends him a kindly greeting.

===Chinese===

> “金色的小鱼儿排成行，

 在银色的大海里游荡。

 小不点儿上梦城去喽，嗬！

 天黑以前就能到那方。

> “快了，快了，

 挨着月亮了，

 他望见它的影子掠过。

 亮了，亮了，

 一盏又一盏，

 向他道一声亲切的问候。

===Original===

> “On glides the ship, it nears the land.

 Lamps are a-gleam so pretty

 Down at the edge of the murmuring strand,

 Bells ring out from the city.”

The city! Tears came into Martin’s eyes. He had often thought of the city in the past days and had wondered if everything was the same at home. For in winter Martin longed for the green grass of summer and the strawberries in the woods, but when a flock of summer days had gone by and the green was no longer fresh and the wild roses in the meadows were gray with the dust of the highroad, he dreamed once more of the city’s gleaming rows of lamps, of Christmas and snow, and of the gray winter twilight in front of the lighted fire.

===Chinese===

> “船儿滑呀滑，快靠了岸。

 灯火闪闪多好看，

 就在低语着的沙滩边上，

 城里的钟声传出来。”

城市！马丁的眼睛里涌出泪来。前几天他常想起城市，纳闷家里是不是一切照旧。因为冬天里，马丁渴望夏天的绿草和林子里的草莓；可是一群夏天的日子过完、绿色不再新鲜、草场上的野玫瑰蒙上大路的尘土时，他又梦起城里亮闪闪的一行行路灯，梦起圣诞节和雪，梦起点起炉火之前灰色的冬日黄昏。

## Preface / 序言

===Original===

It is a sad thought that everyone cannot enjoy Söderberg, that this master of delicate and incisive realism, this prince of humorists, is﻿—for Anglo-Saxons, at least﻿—an acquired taste. But it is well to face at the outset the fact that Söderberg is a European Continental, an Anatole France of Sweden. To those who believe that a man is unvirile or at least anemic if he refuses to believe in human perfectibility this attitude toward life will seem barren and depressing, one to encourage discouragement. How much pleasanter to feel with Pippa, not only at 7 a.m. on a May morning, but at all hours and seasons, that “all’s right with the world”! To insinuate the contrary is to give sanction to those doubts which, if they overtake even the most confident of us at unguarded moments, should all the more be repressed. What is culture if it is not sweetness and light? Listen to Söderberg: “Why all this optimism when not one of the old problems is solved?” And again, one of his characters affirms, “I believe in the lust of the flesh and the incurable loneliness of the soul.”

We read fiction for pleasure. What does this new Swedish novelist offer in compensation for a somewhat despondent view of life? He himself rather hesitates to tell us and in this very hesitation we may, if the faculty be in us, discern one of his chief attractions. Söderberg is reticent because he wishes to present the truth as he sees it without exaggeration and without prejudice. He colors his picture neither with the golden glow of the untroubled believer nor with the red zeal of the revolutionary. He is honest to such a degree that he will not stress his own honesty. On the contrary, he doubts his very doubt: “How could I, a boy of sixteen, be right and all my elders and betters wrong?” And again in *Martin Birck*, “he was not quite certain that truth in itself could produce happiness, but history had taught him that illusion created unhappiness and crime.” And yet all the more from this unobtrusiveness we divine the intellectual honesty of the skeptic, which bursts out only once in the present novel: “Would a man never come who did not sing, but spoke, and spoke plainly!” Such a man has the right to “paint the thing as he sees it,” to revalue the time-honored beliefs and customs of the past in the light of his own experience.

===Chinese===

想到并非人人都能欣赏瑟德尔贝里，想到这位细腻而犀利的现实主义大师、这位幽默家中的亲王，对盎格鲁-撒克逊人至少是一种要慢慢培养的趣味，实在叫人丧气。但一开头就面对这样的事实是好的：瑟德尔贝里是个欧洲大陆人，是瑞典的阿纳托尔·法朗士（Anatole France）。在那些认为人若不信人类可以臻于完善便是缺乏丈夫气、至少也是贫血的人看来，他这种人生态度显得贫瘠而压抑，是一种助长灰心的态度。同皮帕一起感觉“世界一切都好”——不只在五月清晨七点，而是在一切时辰、一切季节——该多么痛快！暗示反面，就是给那些疑虑盖上一枚批准的图章；这些疑虑即便在最自信的人毫无防备的时刻袭来，也理应加倍地被压制。文化若不是甜美与光明，又是什么？听瑟德尔贝里说：“旧问题一个也没有解决，哪来这么多乐观主义？”他笔下又有个人物断言：“我相信肉体的欲望，和灵魂无可救药的孤独。”

我们读小说是为了快乐。这位新的瑞典小说家拿什么来补偿他那相当消沉的人生观呢？他自己倒不大肯告诉我们；而恰在这种迟疑里，我们只要天赋具备，便能看出他主要的魅力之一。瑟德尔贝里缄默，因为他要照自己所见呈现真实，不夸张，不偏袒。他既不用安适的信徒的金色光晕、也不用革命家的赤色热忱涂抹他的画。他诚实到不肯强调自己诚实的程度。相反，他连自己的怀疑也怀疑：“我，一个十六岁的孩子，怎么可能对，而我的长辈和比我强的人全错？”在《马丁·比尔克》里又有这样的话：“他并不能十分确定真理本身能否产生幸福，但历史教会了他：幻觉制造不幸与罪恶。”然而恰恰是从这种不张扬里，我们更看出这怀疑论者的智识诚实；它在本书里只迸发过一次：“会不会永远不来一个不唱、只说、而且明明白白地说的人！”这样的人才有权“照他看见的样子画它”，照他自己的经验重估往昔那些久受尊奉的信仰与习俗。

===Original===

We may, I think, trust in Söderberg’s fidelity to his vision as in that of few living writers. He collects his data carefully and transmits them simply. In that there is always stimulus to a reader who appreciates how difficult it is to do. But he might do all this and be no more than a good photographer.

As we follow the everyday run of events in *Martin Birck*, we may at first be impressed with their perfect verisimilitude and yet incline to class the author as unoriginal. In that respect, though probably in no other, the prose of Söderberg resembles the poetry of Wordsworth. Few readers will progress more than a page or two without that sense of the significant in the commonplace which is the very soul of originality. Söderberg has followed the famous counsel of Flaubert to De Maupassant: “Look at an object until you have seen in it everything that anyone else can see, and then look until you perceive what no one else has seen!” Rarely has any prose been fuller of implications﻿—emotional, psychological, moral﻿—than Söderberg’s. To reread him is invariably to be surprised at all one has missed before. One passes through life with him as one might walk through a meadow with a great naturalist or stroll through a city at night with Whistler. The trivial is clothed with meaning, the habitual is touched with magic. The world of Söderberg lives; it lives in beauty.

===Chinese===

我以为，瑟德尔贝里对他幻象的忠实，可以信得过，比得过的在世作家寥寥无几。他细心收集素材，朴素地传达出来。对一个领会得到此事之难的读者，这里头始终有一种激励。可是这一切他都做到了，也可能不过是个好摄影师。

随着《马丁·比尔克》里日常事件一路展开，我们起初也许会叹服它们完美的逼真，却倾向于把作者归入缺乏独创一类。在这一点上——大概也只在这一点上——瑟德尔贝里的散文像华兹华斯的诗。很少有读者读上一两页还不生出那种于平凡中见紧要的感觉，而那正是独创性的灵魂。瑟德尔贝里奉行了福楼拜给莫泊桑的那句有名的劝告：“看一件东西，直到你在它里面看尽了人人所能看到的一切，然后接着看，直到你看出谁也没有看出的东西！”在蕴含——情感的、心理的、道德的蕴含——之丰满上，几乎没有人超过瑟德尔贝里。重读他，总会惊讶于自己从前漏掉了多少。同他一起穿过人生，就像同一位大博物学家走过一片草场，或者同惠斯勒夜里漫步穿过一座城。琐屑之物披上了意义，惯常之物着上了魔法。瑟德尔贝里的世界活着；活在美国里。

===Original===

And as one grows more and more conscious of the author’s pregnance in matter, one is equally delighted with the perfect consonance of his manner. He gives not only the thing in itself, but the feel of the thing, the overtone. His curious felicity is never startling or precious, it is simply adequate. How far this may be recaptured in translation may of course be an open question. Here at least is an attempt from the short story “Margot”:

> It was a cool night in the early part of October. The moon was up; a cold, moist wind was blowing. The big buildings on Blasieholm formed a dark mass, whose broken and irregular edge seemed to be catching at the wisps of cloud that drove forward against a deep-blue background. The still, heavy water of Nybro Inlet mirrored a broad glittering moonpath in oily rings, and along the wharves the lumber sloops raised a thin and motionless forest of masts and tackle. In the upper air was haste and tumult; the clouds hunted each other from west to east, till over the woods of Djurgården they congested into a low black wall. It was as if Heaven were breaking camp for a journey, for a flight.

The reader of *Martin Birck* will find any number of similar passages, in description, character-drawing and the power of the author to express his own reactions on life and art.

===Chinese===

而当人越来越意识到作者取材的丰孕，也同样欢喜于他笔致的完美谐和。他给出的不只是事物本身，还有事物的感觉，它的泛音。他那奇妙的妥帖从不惊人，也不矫饰，只是恰恰合适。这在翻译里能挽回多少，当然是个悬而未决的问题。这里至少是短篇小说《玛戈》（“Margot”）里的一处尝试：

> 那是十月头里一个清凉的夜。月亮上来了；刮着又冷又潮的风。布拉西霍尔姆（Blasieholm）上的大楼连成黑压压的一团，残缺不齐的边缘仿佛正勾住一缕缕在深蓝底子上疾行的云。尼布罗湾沉甸甸的静水，用油亮的环纹映出一条宽阔闪烁的月光之路；沿着码头，运木材的单桅船竖起一片细细的、一动不动的桅杆与索具的森林。高空中一片匆忙与骚动；云你追我赶地从西往东，到尤高尔登（Djurgården）的树林上空，拥塞成一道低低的黑墙。仿佛天正在拔营，要去远行，要去逃亡。

《马丁·比尔克》的读者会发现数不清的类似段落，在写景里，在人物刻画里，在作者表达自己对人生与艺术的感受的能力里。

===Original===

What manner of man is this quiet interpreter of the life about him? Hjalmar Söderberg was born in Stockholm, 1869. The outward tenor of his way has been uneventful. After trying journalism in a provincial town he tired of “serving caviar to the Boeotians” and returned to his native city, the background of nearly all his work. He first achieved distinction in the “Storiettes,” miniature stories usually told in the first person and based on some casual incident of daily life. In this form he is unsurpassed. *Martin Birck*, his first novel, published in 1907, was partly inspired by *Niels Lyhne*, the work of his elder Danish contemporary, J. P. Jacobsen, but was mainly autobiographical. Söderberg was also influenced by the modern French novelists, especially Flaubert, Maupassant and Anatole France. The last named he translated. He wrote two other novels, *Dr. Glas* and *The Serious Game*, and two plays, *Gertrud* and *The Hour of Fate*, besides numerous collections of short stories. His last long book is *Jehovah’s Fire*, an historico-religious narrative. Some early poems and a small sheaf of criticism complete the tally of his rather moderate output. Of recent years he has been living in Copenhagen. He has never married.

How little this dry recital of facts has to do with the real case in point! The genius of Söderberg is inherent in the temperament of the man. In appearance he is homely, stoutish, and suave, a bit Bohemian but decidedly a gentleman. Quiet, observant, unpretentious, and rather indolent, he gives an impression of infinite leisure and tolerance which is largely borne out by his writing. His mind is a rich, seemingly passive soil, in which small events take root and grow, as it were, without an effort on his part. Therein lies the unique charm of his stories; their unforced, organic quality.

But in the simplicity of Söderberg there is infinite subtlety. He lets life speak through him because he realizes that in the last analysis nothing speaks as persuasively as life. In his presentation there is a skill beyond praise. With all his naturalism and tranquillity of style, he gives us great moments, moments of profound insight, of wistful loveliness, of quaint and surprising humor. After all, things do not choose themselves or arrange themselves in right relation on the canvas; they only seem to do so. Without obtruding his personality Söderberg speaks to the mind and emotions of his audience in no uncertain terms.

===Chinese===

这位静静地解说身边生活的人，是怎样的一个人呢？亚尔马尔·瑟德尔贝里 1869 年生于斯德哥尔摩。他外在的人生历程平静无事。在一座外省城市试过新闻业之后，他厌倦了“给彼奥提亚人端鱼子酱”，回到他的本城，他几乎全部作品的背景。他最初以“小故事”成名，那是些通常用第一人称讲述、取材于日常生活偶然事件的小型故事。在这一体裁里他无出其右。《马丁·比尔克》是他的第一部长篇，1907 年出版，部分受了《尼尔斯·吕讷》的启发——那是他的丹麦年长同代人 J. P. 雅各布森的作品——但主要是自传性的。瑟德尔贝里也受法国现代小说家的影响，尤其是福楼拜、莫泊桑和阿纳托尔·法朗士。最后这一位的作品他翻译过。他还写过另外两部长篇，《格拉斯医生》（*Dr. Glas*）和《认真的游戏》（*The Serious Game*），两个剧本《格特鲁德》（*Gertrud*）和《命运时刻》（*The Hour of Fate*），以及若干短篇集子。他最后的一本长书是《耶和华的火》（*Jehovah’s Fire*），一部历史宗教叙事。一些早期诗作和一小束评论，凑齐了他产量相当有限的账目。近年来他住在哥本哈根。他从未结婚。

这份干巴巴的事实清单，同真正要说的又有什么相干！瑟德尔贝里的天分就在此人的气质里。外表上，他平常、微胖、温和，有点波希米亚气，却地地道道是位绅士。安静，善观察，不装腔，而且相当懒散，他给人无限闲适与宽容的印象，这一点大半为他的写作所证实。他的头脑是一块仿佛被动的沃土，细小的事件在里面生根、生长，仿佛全不费他一分力气。他小说的独特魅力就在这里：那种毫不勉强、浑然天成的品质。

但瑟德尔贝里的单纯里藏着无限的精微。他让生活借他说话，因为他明白归根结底没有什么说得过生活。他的呈现有一种无可褒贬的技巧。以他全部的自然主义和风格的宁静，他给了我们一些伟大的时刻，深刻洞见的时刻，惆怅之美的时刻，古怪而惊人的幽默的时刻。毕竟，事物并不自己挑选自己、自己把自己安排到画布上恰当的关系里去；它们只是看着像这样。瑟德尔贝里不露个人面目，却毫不含糊地对读者的头脑和感情说话。

===Original===

What does he give us finally? First, perhaps, the delight of seeing nature and humanity clearly and the greater delight of entering imaginatively into the essence of both. His truth has the beauty of understanding. We find that life does not need to be idealized to be beautiful; it needs only to be realized. And as a corollary he gives us a sympathy in this manifestation which is not unlike that of Whitman, for it is the sympathy of acceptance. There is a tone of sadness, sometimes of almost tragic depth, in the knowledge of “what man has made of man,” and with it a smile of forgiveness. What we understand we pardon. Men and women are lovable in spite of, largely no doubt because of, their mistakes.

But also men and women are irresistibly funny. Söderberg has almost exactly the mood of Jaques in *As You Like It*. But whereas Jaques is dry, Söderberg is sly, with an ingenuous slyness that never, as with Sterne, slips off into a leer. How he enjoys letting his people amuse us, in watching with us their self-important gestures, the eternal passions that fade away in a month or a year, their curious delusions about fame and money and respectability! If these people could see themselves! And as we look, we may perhaps be a little mortified to see ourselves. How foolishly we have wasted our energies and annoyed those about us, for what? Perhaps we shall be a little more lenient to the faults of others from now on. The laughter which Söderberg evokes is thoughtful laughter.

Are we then given no positive impulse, is there no meaning in life, nothing worth striving for? “Perhaps not,” says Söderberg. And yet, pessimist though he is, he has a reticent pride of his own. He cannot, we feel, tell a lie, cannot force anyone in his stories to do or think anything that is not in character. Furthermore, he adumbrates through the philosophy of Martin the ideal of writing “so that each and all who really cared to could understand him.” And, like most of Söderberg’s simple statements, that means considerably more than appears on the surface.

Enough, perhaps more than enough, has been said to indicate the mood for best enjoying *Martin Birck*. To call further attention to details would only tend to spoil the pleasure of those attempered to appreciate it. I must return to the original statement that the reader’s reaction to it will be peculiarly personal. For myself, I differ almost completely from the author in his conclusions about life, I object strongly to his rather supine attitude, yet I admire and love him. I find him as brilliant as the modern French masters, and much more kindly. He has given me more than have nine-tenths of the worthy authors with whom I agree. There is in him a strict sense of truth, a tenderness, a humor which put him definitely on the side of the angels. He will annoy, will scandalize, many excellent people, but I am afraid I am not sorry that he should. He has been called the enfant terrible of Swedish literature. Perhaps we have been taking him too seriously; no doubt he himself will think so. After all, there is something perennially fascinating about a naughty child.

C. W. S.

===Chinese===

他到底给了我们什么？首先，也许是清清楚楚看见自然与人类的快乐，和更进一步凭想象进入两者本质的更大的快乐。他的真实有着理解之美。我们发现生活无须理想化便是美的；它只须被实现。随之而来的是，他给了我们一种对这一显现的同情，同惠特曼的不无相似，因为那是接受的同情。在“人对人做了什么”的认知里有一种悲伤的调子，有时几乎有悲剧的深度，与它同来的是一个宽恕的微笑。我们理解了的，我们就宽恕。男男女女是可爱的，尽管有——多半正因为有——他们的种种过错。

可男男女女也照样可笑得叫人抵抗不住。瑟德尔贝里的心境几乎同《皆大欢喜》（*As You Like It*）里的杰奎斯一模一样。只是杰奎斯干枯，瑟德尔贝里狡黠，带着一种天真的狡黠，从不像斯特恩那样滑成狞笑。他多么乐意让他笔下的人逗我们乐，同我们一起看他们那些自命不凡的手势，那些一个月一年就冷下去的永恒的激情，他们关于名声、金钱和体面的古怪妄想！这些人要是能看见自己！而我们看着，也许看见自己时会有一点窘。我们多么愚蠢地糟蹋了精力、搅扰了周围的人，为了什么呢？从今往后，我们对别人的过错也许会宽容一点。瑟德尔贝里引出的笑，是深思的笑。

那么我们就没有得到一点积极的推动，人生就没有意义，没有值得奋斗的东西吗？“也许没有。”瑟德尔贝里说。然而他虽是悲观主义者，却自有一种缄默的自豪。我们觉得他撒不了谎，不能强迫他故事里的任何人做任何不合性格的事、想任何不合性格的念头。而且，他借马丁的哲学暗示了一个理想：写作，“要让每一个真正愿意懂的人都能够懂他”。而像瑟德尔贝里多数的简单话一样，这一句的含义也远比表面多。

要点出以怎样的心境去读《马丁·比尔克》最相宜，以上说的也许够了，也许已经太多。再在细节上啰嗦，只会败坏那些调好了兴味的人的乐趣。我必须回到开头的话：读者对它的反应将格外地因人而异。就我自己说，在人生结论上我几乎全然不同意作者，我强烈反对他那种相当懈怠的态度，然而我钦佩他，爱他。我觉得他同法国现代大师们一样才气焕发，而且和善得多。他给我的，超过十分之九与我意见相投的可敬作家。他身上有一种严格的真实感，一种温柔，一种幽默，把它们断然放在天使们的一边。他会惹恼、会开罪许多正人君子，可他应该这样，我怕我并不难过。有人称他为瑞典文学的“恐怖顽童”。也许我们一直把他看得太重；他自己无疑也会这么想。毕竟，一个顽皮的孩子身上，自有一种常年不衰的迷人之处。

C. W. S.

## The Old Street / 老街

## The White Cap / 白帽

## The Winter Night / 冬夜

### V / 五

===Original===

But when the lamp was lighted and they sat around the table, each with his own work or book or paper, Martin went off and sat in a corner. For he had suddenly become sad without knowing why. There he sat in the dark, staring in at the circle of yellow light in which the others sat and talked, while he felt himself outside, abandoned and forgotten.

It did not help that Maria hunted out an old volume of *Near and Far* to show him Garibaldi and the war in Poland and Emperor Napoleon III with his pointed mustaches; he had seen them all many times. Nor did it help that she gave him a piece of paper and taught him to fold it into the shape of a saltcellar, a crow, or a catamaran; for, though he did not know it, Martin only longed for someone to say or do something that would make him cry. It was therefore he sat moody and silent, listening to the rain that whipped against the window, for it had begun to rain again, and the wind shook the glass.

What was that? Did he suddenly hear father say to mother: “Perhaps you’re right that we ought to try to sell the piano and buy a pianino on instalment. It goes out of tune in a couple of weeks, and a pianino would be prettier.”

Martin gave a start at the words “sell the piano.” He had no clear idea of what a pianino was, but he didn’t believe it could be a real piano; he pictured it rather as something that was worked with a handle. He didn’t believe any other instrument could sound as beautiful as their piano. He loved every dent and every crack in the red mahogany frame, for he himself had made most of them, and he remembered almost every key from its special color. Sell the piano! To his ears it sounded like something impossible. It was almost as if he had heard his parents calmly sitting and talking about selling grandmother and buying an aunt instead.

Martin began to cry before he knew it.

“Mamma,” said Maria, “Martin’s crying.”

===Chinese===

可是灯一点起来，大家围桌坐下，各干各的活，或看书看报，马丁却走开，坐在一个角落里。因为他忽然没来由地难过起来。他坐在暗处，瞪着眼望那一圈黄灯光，别人坐在那圈里谈着话，他却觉得自己在外头，被撇下了，被忘掉了。

玛丽亚翻出一部旧的《近与远》（*Near and Far*），指给他看加里波第、波兰的战争、蓄着尖胡子的拿破仑三世皇帝，也没有用；那些他见过许多遍了。她给他一张纸，教他折成盐碟、乌鸦或双体船的样子，也没有用；因为马丁自己虽然不知道，其实只盼有谁说点什么、做点什么，好让他哭出来。所以他坐在那里闷闷地不出声，听雨抽打着窗子——又下起雨来了，风摇得玻璃直响。

那是什么？他是不是忽然听见父亲对母亲说：“也许你说得对，咱们该试试把钢琴卖了，分期买个皮亚尼诺。它几个星期就走音，皮亚尼诺也好看些。”

“卖钢琴”三个字叫马丁一惊。皮亚尼诺是什么，他没有清楚的概念，可他不信那能是真正的钢琴；他倒把它想成一种带摇柄摇的东西。他不信还有哪件乐器能响得像他们的钢琴那样好听。他爱红桃花心木琴架上每一处凹痕、每一道裂缝，因为大半是他自己弄的；他记得几乎每一个琴键各自的颜色。卖钢琴！在他听来，这像一件不可能的事。简直像听见父母心平气和地坐在那里商量，把外祖母卖了，另买一位姨妈回来。

马丁不知不觉哭起来了。

“妈妈，”玛丽亚说，“马丁哭了。”

===Original===

“What are you crying for, Martin?” his mother asked.

Martin only sobbed.

“He’s tired and sleepy,” declared grandmother. “He’d better go to bed.”

---

While Martin, still sobbing, made the rounds to say good night, Lotta came in with the tea-tray. She had a very solemn expression as she said, “I’m sorry to have to tell you that Heggbom is dead.”

Everything became silent in the room. Martin stopped crying.

===Chinese===

“你哭什么呀，马丁？”母亲问。

马丁只是抽抽噎噎。

“他又累又困，”外祖母断言，“最好送他上床。”

---

马丁还抽噎着，挨个儿去道晚安；这时洛塔端着茶盘进来了。她一副极郑重的表情，说：“很遗憾，我不得不告诉你们：海格博姆死了。”

屋里一下子静了。马丁停止了哭。

===Original===

Grandmother clasped her hands together: “Well, and has he really passed away? Has it come that suddenly?﻿ ﻿… Glory be! and has he passed away? Ah, ’twas the brandy!﻿ ﻿… But it was for the best that he should die, though ’twill be hard for the missus; he was the porter, anyway, and maintained his wife and children.”

“He died just at seven,” said Lotta.

But when no one said anything she went out into the kitchen again.

“It might be a good idea to send out a list to the neighbors and start a little subscription,” said mother.

Martin was sent to bed. His mother sat at the side of his bed and said prayers with him. He was let off with “God Who hast us in Thy care,” because he was so tired. Otherwise he used to say “Our Father” and “Lord, let Thy blessing rest upon us” besides.

---

===Chinese===

外祖母双手一合：“嗐，他真的去了？就这么突然？……荣耀归于主！他真的去了？唉，是白兰地啊！……不过他去了倒也好，只是太太要苦了；他好歹是门房，养活着老婆孩子。”

“他七点整断的气。”洛塔说。

见谁也不接话，她又回厨房去了。

“要不要给邻居们发一张单子，起个小募捐，”母亲说。

马丁被打发上床。母亲坐在床边领他做祷告。只念了《眷顾我们的上帝》就放过了他，因为他太乏了。平常除了这个，他还要念《我们在天上的父》和《主啊，愿你的祝福降临我们》。

---

===Original===

Martin lay awake a long time listening to the rain as it plashed against the window, for he was not at all sleepy; he had only said so to get out of the long prayers that he didn’t understand. It is impossible for a little child to associate any idea with such expressions as “hallowed be Thy name” or “Thy kingdom come.” He lay thinking about Heggbom and wondering if he could get to heaven. He always smelled of brandy.

Martin was afraid of the dark. When Lotta came in with a lighted candle to fix something in the room, he asked her to let the candle stay.

“You must sleep, Martin,” said Lotta. “Heggbom will come and bite you if you don’t.”

With that she went out and took the candle.

Martin began to cry afresh. The wind whistled in the window chinks, every now and then a gate was shut with a bang, and a dog howled outside. Before mother drew the curtains Martin thought there was a red glow in the sky. Perhaps there was a fire in South Stockholm.﻿ ﻿…

There was turmoil and clamor down on the street. Drunken men coming out of the tavern﻿—blows and screams. Heavy steps on the pavement, someone running and someone pursuing﻿—and a cry of “Police, police!”

Martin drew the covers over his head and cried himself to sleep.

===Chinese===

马丁久久睡不着，听雨点噼噼啪啪打在窗上，因为他一点也不困；他那么说，只是为了躲开那些他听不懂的冗长祷告。让小孩子把什么观念同“愿你的名为圣”、“愿你的国降临”这样的话联在一起，是不可能的。他躺着想海格博姆，想他能不能上天堂。他身上总有白兰地味。

马丁怕黑。洛塔端着点着的蜡烛进来收拾什么的时候，他求她把蜡烛留下。

“你得睡觉，马丁，”洛塔说，“不然海格博姆要来咬你。”

说完她就出去，带走了蜡烛。

马丁又哭起来。风在窗缝里呼啸，不时一扇门砰地关上，外面有狗在嚎。母亲拉窗帘之前，马丁觉得天上有红光。也许是南城着火了。……

街上乱哄哄地闹起来。醉汉从小酒馆里出来——殴打和尖叫。人行道上沉重的脚步，有人跑，有人追——还有一声喊：“警察，警察！”

马丁把被子蒙上头，哭着睡着了。

### VI / 六

===Original===

White winter came with sleigh bells and snow and ice-flowers on the windowpane. “They are the dead summer flowers come back again,” said Martin’s mother. Evergreen forests out in the country came from the darkness and solitude into the city streets and squares, and when the Christmas bells rang in the holy day, there stood in Martin’s home a dark and timid fir which smelt of the woods, till evening came and it stood aglitter with candles, white candles and colored candles, and was covered with winter apples and sugarplums with mottoes which were so stupid that even Martin and Maria could see how stupid they were. All the glory of Christmas passed﻿—it was like turning the page of a picture-book﻿—and the star of New Year’s Eve was burning across the white roofs, and people said to each other, “Good night, and thanks for the year!” With a shivering sensation Martin thought of the line of gray winter days that were waiting, to which he could see no ending, for it was interminably long till summer, and still longer till next Christmas.

New Year’s morning he was waked while it was still dark to go to early service. Half asleep he scrambled through the snow by the side of his parents, and as they came around the corner, there stood the church like a giant lantern shining out across the white square where people were crawling in across the snow from all directions. Within the church was the organ’s roar and singing and many shining candles, and Martin felt happy and good and thought this was just the right way to begin the new year; and when the minister began to preach, he went straight to sleep. But when he woke up, the pale hue of dawn was shining in through the windows in the cupola and his mother roused him with, “Now we’ll go home and drink our coffee.”

===Chinese===

白色的冬天来了，带着雪橇铃、雪和窗玻璃上的冰花。“它们是死了的夏天的花又回来了。”马丁的母亲说。郊外的常绿树林从黑暗和寂寞里进了城，来到街道和广场上；圣诞的钟声敲出圣日的时候，马丁家的屋子里立起一棵黑黢黢、怯生生的枞树，散发着树林的气味，等到晚上，它便点起蜡烛闪闪发亮——白的蜡烛和彩色的蜡烛——挂满冬天的苹果和带格言的糖球，那些格言蠢得连马丁和玛丽亚都看得出有多蠢。圣诞的一切荣华过去了——像翻过一页画册——除夕的星在白屋顶上空烧着，人们互道：“晚安，谢谢这一年！”马丁打着寒噤，想着排成一长列等在那里的灰色的冬天的日子，看不到头，因为到夏天长得没有边，到下一个圣诞更长。

新年早晨，天还黑他就被叫起来去望早弥撒。他半睡半醒地跟在父母身边蹚着雪走，拐过街角，教堂立在那里，像一盏巨大的灯笼，照着白雪的广场，人们从四面八方爬雪过来进堂。教堂里是管风琴的轰鸣和歌唱，许多明晃晃的蜡烛；马丁觉得又幸福又善良，心想这样开始新的一年再好不过；等牧师开始讲道，他就径直睡着了。可等他醒来，黎明的苍白颜色已经从圆顶的窗子照进来，母亲推醒他：“现在咱们回家喝咖啡去。”

===Original===

So then they went home, their hearts full of the most beautiful intentions, for Martin understood without telling that it was this sort of thing the minister had preached about. Later in the morning Martin and Maria were sent around on the New Year’s visits to Uncle Jan and Aunt Louisa and other uncles and aunts, where they were given cakes and wine and sugarplums from the Christmas trees. But at Uncle Abraham’s there was no Christmas tree, for he was a widower and had no children but lived alone with an old housekeeper. Uncle Abraham was a doctor and had often cured Martin and Maria of measles and scarlatina and pains in the chest. He had a black beard and a long crooked nose, for he was a Jew. He had also a parrot that could swear in French, and a black tomcat. The cat was named Kolmodin and he was the cleverest cat in the world, for when he was outside the office door and wanted to get in, he didn’t mew as other cats do, but got up on his hind feet, caught his claws in the bell-cord, and pulled it hard. This year when Martin and Maria came to wish Uncle Abraham a Happy New Year, he was sitting alone with his bottle of wine on the table playing chess with himself.

The room was large and half dark and full of books. Outside the snow was falling in great flakes. Uncle Abraham stuffed their pockets full of goodies, made the parrot swear in French, and was very cordial; but he didn’t say much, and in front of the fire which glowed in the porcelain stove sat the cat Kolmodin staring gloomily at his master. Martin and Maria stood silent and looked at each other with a feeling of oppression. For they had more than once heard their parents say that Uncle Abraham was not a happy man and that he never was really cheerful.

===Chinese===

于是他们回家去，心里装满了最美的意图，因为马丁不用人说就明白，牧师讲的就是这一类事。上午晚些时候，马丁和玛丽亚被打发出去拜年，到扬叔叔和路易莎婶婶家，还有别的叔叔婶婶家，处处给他们圣诞树上的糕点、酒和糖球。可亚伯拉罕叔叔家没有圣诞树，因为他是个鳏夫，没有孩子，同一个老管家单独过活。亚伯拉罕叔叔是医生，常治好马丁和玛丽亚的麻疹、猩红热和胸口疼。他有一把黑胡子和一个长长的弯鼻子，因为他是犹太人。他还有一只会用法文骂人的鹦鹉，和一只黑公猫。猫叫科尔莫丁（Kolmodin），是世界上最聪明的猫：它在外间门外想进去时，不像别的猫那样喵喵叫，而是用后腿立起来，把爪子钩住铃绳，用力一拽。这一年马丁和玛丽亚来给亚伯拉罕叔叔拜年时，他正独自坐在桌上那瓶酒旁边，自己同自己下棋。

房间很大，半暗，满是书。外面落着鹅毛大雪。亚伯拉罕叔叔把他们两个的口袋塞满了好吃的，让鹦鹉表演法文骂人，非常热诚；可他话不多，瓷炉里火正红，猫科尔莫丁坐在火前，忧郁地瞪着主人。马丁和玛丽亚一声不响地站着，带着一种压抑的感觉对望。因为他们不止一次听父母说过，亚伯拉罕叔叔不是个幸福的人，他从来没有真正快活过。

### VII / 七

===Original===

So now it was the new year. The almanac which Martin had given his father for Christmas had a red cover, whereas the old one had been blue. Martin also found to his surprise and disappointment that this was the only difference he could see between the new year and the old, that the days passed as they had passed before with ringing of bells and snow and a somber sky, with weariness of the old games and the old stories, and with the longing to be big. He longed for that time but feared it too. For his mother had often pointed at the ragman who had seen better days and said that if Martin wouldn’t eat his porridge or his beer-soup and otherwise be a good and obedient boy, he would come to be just such a ragman when he was big. When he heard his mother talk so, he would feel a tightening of the chest and would see himself slinking in through the gate at dusk with a pack on his back and poking in the ash barrel with a black stick, while father and mother and sister and grandmother were sitting together around the lamp as before. For it never occurred to him to think that his home could be broken up and dispersed.

Snow fell, a great deal of snow. The drifts grew, and it became sparklingly cold. Martin had to keep indoors with his alphabet book and multiplication tables, with his color-box and jumping-jacks and all splendid things﻿—already faded﻿—which Christmas had left behind. Among the jumping-jacks there was one called the Red Turk which he was fonder of than the others, because Uncle Abraham, who had given it to him, had said it was the jolliest jumping-jack in all the world. “You see,” he had said one evening, “in itself it is neither amusing nor remarkable that an old pasteboard man kicks about when one pulls the strings. But the Red Turk is no common pasteboard man; he can think and choose the same as we. And when you jerk the strings and he begins to prance, he says to himself: ‘I am a being with free will, I kick just as I want to and exclusively for my own entertainment. Hoho! there’s nothing so delightful as to kick.’ But when you stop jerking the string, he decides that he is tired and says to himself: ‘To the deuce with the kicking! The finest thing there is is to hang on a hook on the wall and stay entirely still.’ Yes, he is the jolliest jumping-jack in the world.”

===Chinese===

这样，新年到了。马丁圣诞送给父亲的年历是红封皮的，旧的那本是蓝的。马丁又惊奇又失望地发现，这是新年同旧年之间他能看出的唯一的区别：日子照旧过去，有钟声、雪和阴沉的天，有对旧游戏旧故事的厌倦，有对长大的渴望。他渴望那个时辰，又怕它。因为母亲常常指着那个从前阔过的收破烂的说，马丁要是不喝粥、不喝啤酒汤，不好好听话，长大了就同那个收破烂的一模一样。听母亲这样说时，他胸口就发紧，就看见自己黄昏背着包袱溜进大门，拿一根黑棍子捅煤灰桶，而爸爸妈妈、姐姐和外祖母还照旧一起围灯坐着。因为他从没想到过，他的家也可能拆散。

下雪了，下好大的雪。雪堆高起来，天冷得耀眼。马丁只好守在屋里，同他的识字课本和乘法表、颜料盒和跳跳人，同圣诞留下的一切好东西——已经褪了色的——在一起。跳跳人里有一个叫红土耳其人，他比别的更得宠，因为送他的亚伯拉罕叔叔说过，它是全世界最快活的跳跳人。“你瞧，”有一天晚上他说，“一个纸板小人，一拉线就手舞足蹈，这件事本身既不好笑，也不稀奇。可红土耳其人不是寻常的纸板人；他会思想，会同我们一样做选择。你一扯线，他开始蹦跳，他就对自己说：‘我是个有自由意志的存在，我想怎么踢腿就怎么踢腿，纯粹为了自己取乐。嗬嗬！再没有比踢腿更快活的了。’可等你停止扯线，他就决定自己累了，对自己说：‘踢腿见鬼去吧！顶美的事就是挂在墙上的钩子上，一动不动。’是啊，它是全世界最快活的跳跳人。”

===Original===

Martin didn’t understand much of this, but he understood that the Red Turk was amusing and set greater store by him than ever.

---

So the days passed, and with Twelfth Night began small family parties with stripping of Christmas trees and shadow games and doll theaters and magic lanterns with colored pictures on a ghostly white sheet. On the way home the stars sparkled, and father pointed to the heaven and said, “That’s the Milky Way, and there is the Dipper.”

===Chinese===

这些话马丁不大懂，可他懂了红土耳其人好玩，比先前更宝贝它了。

---

日子就这样过去，主显节夜里开始了小小的家庭聚会：撤圣诞树、玩影子戏、演木偶戏、在惨白的床单上放映彩色画片的幻灯。回家路上星星闪闪，父亲指着天说：“那是银河，那是北斗。”

### VIII / 八

===Original===

But one morning when Martin awoke he saw that the heavens shone with a brighter blue than they had for a long time and that there was a dripping from the eaves and the naked branches of the pear tree. And while he was sitting up in bed looking out at the shining blue, Maria came in with a branch that seemed to blossom in a hundred colors; but it was not flowers﻿—it was tinted feathers. She flicked him with the branch and danced and sang that it was Shrove Tuesday and she had a holiday from school, hurrah! And there were to be buns with almond icing for dinner.

Then they took the feathers off the branch and dressed up in them and played Indians and white men, but they were both Indians.

But mother took the switch and set it in the window in a jug filled with water in the full sunlight. The room faced the east and this was the morning sun. And lo and behold! it wasn’t many days before brown-and-greenish buds came out here and there on the twigs, they swelled and grew larger, until one day they had broken out and changed into frail light-green leaves; the whole branch had become verdant, and it was spring.

---

One afternoon a beam of sunlight fell into the hall which faced the west.

===Chinese===

可是一天早晨，马丁醒来，看见天上亮着许久没有过的更亮的蓝，屋檐和梨树光秃秃的枝子上滴滴答答淌着水。他正坐在床上望着那亮闪闪的蓝色，玛丽亚进来了，拿着一根仿佛开出百种颜色的花来的树枝；可那不是花——是染了色的羽毛。她用树枝抽他一下，又跳又唱：今天是谢肉节，她学校放假了，乌拉！中午还有抹杏仁糖霜的甜面包吃。

随后他们把羽毛从枝子上捋下来，插在头上扮印第安人和白人玩，不过两个都当印第安人。

母亲却接过枝子，插在窗口一只盛满水的罐里，晒着满太阳。屋子朝东，这是早晨的太阳。你瞧！没过几天，枝条上这儿那儿就鼓出棕绿色的芽，胀着，长大，直到有一天绽开，变成了娇嫩的浅绿色叶子；整根枝条都绿了，春天来了。

---

一天下午，一束阳光落进朝西的门厅里。

===Original===

“Look at the sun, children,” said mother. “That’s our first afternoon sun this year.”

The sunbeam fell on the polished glass of the candelabra, where it broke and strewed rainbow-colored patches all over the room on the furniture and wallpaper. Just then father passed through the hall and set the three-sided bits of glass in motion with a slight blow of his hand. There was a tumultuous dance of the colored patches around the walls, a dance as of fluttering butterflies. Martin and Maria began a chase after them. They ran till they were flushed and hot, striking their hands against the walls, and when they saw a patch on their hand instead of on the wall paper, they screamed with delight, “Now I’ve got it!”

But in the next second it glided away, the sunbeam paled, and the butterflies, weary of fluttering and shining, departed﻿—Martin saw the last of them expire on his hand.

But it wasn’t spring yet after all.

The snow fell again, wet snow that melted at once and was dirty at once; again the bells rang in the black cupola, and it was Good Friday. Martin and Maria were in church, but they might not sit with their parents, for their parents sat far away in the choir in a multitude of solemn-looking people dressed in black. They were dressed in black themselves, father in a frock coat with a white cravat, and everything was black: the red on the pulpit and altar was gone, and there was black instead; the priests had black capes, a black cross rose menacingly from the leaden-hued cloud of the altarpiece far away in the dusk of the choir, and black-gray sky lay above all, staring in through the belfry windows of the cupola. Martin could not go to sleep as usual, because everything was so uncanny: the choir moaned and lamented, the minister looked sinister and forbidding and talked about blood, and a dog howled out in the churchyard.﻿ ﻿…

Martin was delighted with all this, although he didn’t realize it.

---

===Chinese===

“看太阳，孩子们，”母亲说，“这是今年第一束下午的太阳。”

阳光落在烛台擦得亮亮的玻璃上，在那里折断，把虹彩的光斑撒得满屋都是，家具上、墙纸上到处都是。正这时父亲穿过门厅，用手轻轻一碰，把那三棱的玻璃片碰得转起来。彩斑绕着四壁乱舞，像一群翻飞的蝴蝶。马丁和玛丽亚追起它们来。跑到满脸通红、浑身发热，手掌往墙上拍；等他们看见光斑落在了自己手上而不是墙纸上时，就欢喜得尖叫：“我逮着它了！”

可下一秒它又滑走了，阳光淡下去，蝴蝶们飞累了、闪累了，散了——马丁看着最后一只在自己手上熄灭。

可这到底还不是春天。

雪又下了起来，湿雪，落地就化，一化就脏；黑圆顶里的钟又敲起来，耶稣受难日到了。马丁和玛丽亚上了教堂，可不许同父母坐在一起，因为父母远远坐在唱诗班里，坐在一群穿黑衣、神情肃穆的人中间。他们自己也一身黑：父亲穿大礼服，系白领结；样样都是黑的：讲坛和祭坛上的红色不见了，换成了黑；牧师们披着黑斗篷，一幅黑十字架从祭坛画那铅色的云里森然立起，远远立在唱诗班的昏暗深处；黑灰色的天罩在这一切之上，从圆顶的钟楼窗子里瞪进来。马丁照例睡不着了，因为一切都那么阴森：唱诗班呜咽哀号，牧师面目凶恶森严，讲着血，教堂墓园外有狗在嚎。……

这一切让马丁喜欢极了，虽然他自己不知道。

---

===Original===

Spring at last, real spring.﻿ ﻿… It came first when the Royal Family drove out to the big park with their plumed and golden equipage. How the whole day shone, how radiant it was with blue and sunshine and spring around the chimneys and roofs, around the weathercock on the church tower! In Martin’s street the lindens were already out, and over the leaning fences hovered clouds of white blossom, cherry blossom, and hawthorn. On the square and along the Avenue the people thronged, the whole city was out in bright and gay-colored costumes, and in front of the Life Guards’ barracks stood the light blue guardsmen, whom Martin loved and worshiped, on duty with sabers drawn. The Royal Family drove past in a cloud of plumes and gold, the crowd cheered and Martin cheered, and then everybody went out to the park to drink fruit juices and mineral water at Bellmansruh. All around whined violins and street-organs, and Martin felt completely happy. But on the way back they stopped a moment to look at the Punch and Judy theater. The landscape was already beginning to darken, but people still flocked around the puppet theater where Punch was just going to beat his wife to death. Martin pressed close to his mother. He saw mouths open in a broad laugh around him in the dusk; he understood nothing, but the sound of the cudgel on the doll’s head frightened him﻿—were people laughing at that bad man there beating his wife? Then came the creditor, and him too Punch beat to death. The policeman and the devil he treated similarly, till finally Death lured him into his cauldron, and that was the end. Martin couldn’t laugh or weep either; he only stared abashed and terrified into this new world, which was so unlike his own. On the way home he was cold and tired. The sun was gone, it grew darker and darker; the king had long since driven home to his castle, and drunken men scuffled and bawled around him. The anemones which Martin had picked at the edge of the wood were withered, and he threw them away to be trampled into the mire.

But when he was home at last and it was night and Martin lay in his bed asleep, he dreamed that father hit mother on the head with a big cudgel.

===Chinese===

春天终于来了，真正的春天。……它最早是随着王室坐着插羽毛的金色车驾出巡大公园一起来的。这一天多么明亮，烟囱和屋顶四周、教堂钟楼的作风标四周，蓝的、亮的、春光闪闪！马丁街上的菩提树已经发芽，斜倚的栅栏上浮着一团团白花，樱桃花和山楂花。广场上和林荫道上人头攒动，全城的人都穿出鲜艳的衣裳；近卫军营房前站着浅蓝军装的卫兵——马丁热爱、崇拜的卫兵——持着出鞘的军刀站岗。王室在一团羽毛和金色里驶过，人群欢呼，马丁也欢呼，随后大家都到公园去，在贝尔曼斯鲁（Bellmansruh）喝果子汁和矿泉水。四下里小提琴和手摇风琴吱吱呀呀，马丁觉得完全幸福了。可是在回来的路上，他们停下来看了一会儿潘趣和朱迪木偶戏。天色已经暗下来，人们还围着那个小戏台，台上的潘趣正要把他老婆打死。马丁紧紧贴住母亲。他看见暮色里四周一张张嘴张大了笑；他什么都不懂，可棍子敲在木偶头上的声音吓住了他——人们是在笑那个打老婆的坏人吗？随后来了讨债的，潘趣也把他打死了。警察和魔鬼他同样办理，直到最后死神把他诱进锅里，一了百了。马丁笑不出，也哭不出；只是又羞又怕地瞪着这个新世界，它同他自己的世界多么不一样。回家路上他又冷又累。太阳没有了，天越来越黑；国王早就驾回宫去了，醉汉在他四周推搡叫嚷。马丁在林边摘的银莲花都蔫了，他把它们扔掉，任人踩进泥里。

可是等他终于到家、到了夜里、马丁在床上睡熟之后，他梦见父亲用一根大棍子打母亲的头。

### X / 十

===Original===

The wheel of the year had gone around, and it was again autumn.

In the city there was much that was new. Long Row was gone with its gardens and sheds; in its place a great brick building rose aloft, growing higher every day, obscuring both the lindens of Humlegård Park and the Observatory on its hill. Everywhere people were pulling down and building up, and dynamite blasts resounded every day in the district, which was now no longer to be called Ladgardsland but Östermalm. And Mrs. Heggbom had become a lady. If anybody called her by her former title, she would answer politely but decidedly, “Not any more!”

Martin went to school, but it was a modest little school and not nearly so terrible as he had thought. One had only to learn one’s lessons, and everything went well. And Martin felt with pride that his knowledge of the world was enlarged with every day. Space and time daily extended their boundaries before his eyes; the world was much bigger than he had dreamed and so old that his head grew giddy at the multitude of the years. If one looked ahead, time had no limits﻿—it ran out into a dizzying blue infinity; but if one traced it back, one at least found far back in the darkness a beginning, a place where one had to stop: six thousand years before the birth of Our Saviour it was that God had created the world. That stood clear and plain in Martin’s *Biblical History*, on the first page.

In six days He had made it. But the teacher said that days were longer at that time.

But if possibly the days of the creation had been a little longer than ordinary days, it was just the opposite with Methusalem’s nine hundred and sixty-nine years. “At that time, you see, they didn’t reckon the years as long as now,” the teacher said.

===Chinese===

年的轮子转了一圈，又到秋天了。

城里新东西很多。“长排”连同它的花园和棚屋没有了；原址上高高升起一座砖石大楼，一天比一天高，把胡姆勒花园的菩提树和山上的天文台都遮住了。到处有人拆了建、建了拆，这个区里每天响起炸药的轰响——如今它不该再叫拉德加尔兰（Ladgardsland），得叫奥斯特马尔姆了。海格博姆太太则成了太太。谁若还用她从前的称呼叫她，她就客气而坚决地回答：“不兴那样叫了！”

马丁上了学，不过那是所不起眼的小学校，远不像他想的那样可怕。只要把功课学会，一切就都顺当。马丁怀着得意感到，他对世界的知识一天天扩大。空间和时间天天在他眼前扩展疆界；世界比他梦想的大得多，年头多得叫人头昏。往前看，时间没有边际——一直伸进一片叫人晕眩的蓝色无穷里；可往回追，至少在黑暗的深处找得到一个开头，一个不得不停下的地方：我们的救主降生前六千年，上帝创造了世界。这在马丁的《圣经史》（*Biblical History*）里写得清清楚楚，就在第一页上。

他用六天造了它。可是老师说，那时候的日子比现在的长。

可倘若创世那六天也许比平常的日子长一点，那么玛土撒拉的九百六十九岁就恰恰相反了。“那时候的人，你们知道，计算年头不像现在这么长。”老师说。

===Original===

There was so much new to learn and digest; school had in reality none of those terrors with which Martin had arrayed it in his imagination.

But on the other hand the way to and from school was filled with all sorts of perils and adventures. Those ill-disposed beings who were called rowdies and who called Martin and his comrades stuck-ups might be in ambush around any corner. The worst of these rowdies were the fierce and formidable “marsh rowdies,” who would now and then leave their gloomy habitation in the tract between the Humlegård and Roslagstorg, the “Marsh,” to go on the war path. Their weapons were said to be lead balls on the end of short ropes. But more than these marsh rowdies, whom Martin had never seen and of whose existence he was not entirely sure, he feared the horrible Franz, who used to live in the Long Row and still resided in the same street. For this rowdy directed all his energies and intelligence toward embittering Martin’s life by day and even pursued him into his nocturnal dreams.

But one day when Martin was on his way for morning recess, he found two of his comrades in a fight with Franz at a street corner; in fact they had already overcome him, thrown him down, and were pummeling him with their fists. At this time Martin had begun to read Indian books, so that he at once saw in Franz a parallel to the noble redskin and did not want to miss so favorable a chance of making him his ally against other rowdies. He therefore advanced and represented to his comrades how cowardly it was to fight two against one, said that Franz lived in his street and was a very decent rowdy, and proposed that they let him go in peace. While he thus drew the attention of his comrades, Franz managed to get up and run away.

In return Martin got all the licking intended for Franz. Furthermore he had to endure the scorn of his comrades for being the friend of a rowdy. And the next time he met Franz on the street in front of the dyer’s gate, the latter tripped him so that he fell into the gutter, then gave him a bloody nose, tore his books apart, swore at him frightfully, and ran off.

He had not understood that he was supposed to be a noble redskin. But this Franz was not a rowdy of the usual sort; he was a thoroughly awful rowdy.

===Chinese===

要学要消化的新鲜事这么多；学校其实全然没有马丁想象中给自己披上的那些可怕。

可另一方面，上学放学的路上却充满种种危险和奇遇。那些不怀好意的家伙——人家管他们叫无赖，他们管马丁和他的同学叫臭美——可能埋伏在任何街角。这些无赖里最凶最可怕的是剽悍的“沼泽无赖”，他们不时离开自己那阴暗的巢穴——胡姆勒花园和罗斯拉斯托格（Roslagstorg）之间那片“沼泽”——出去开战。据说他们的武器是短绳头上拴的铅球。可是比这些从没见过、连存在与否都拿不准的沼泽无赖更叫马丁害怕的，是那个可怕的弗朗茨，他就住在“长排”，如今还住在原来那条街上。因为这个无赖把他全部的力气和聪明都用来折磨马丁的白昼，甚至追进他夜里的梦。

可是有一天，马丁早晨课间休息的路上，撞见两个同学在街角同弗朗茨打架；说真的，他们已经把他打倒，正用拳头捶他。这时马丁已经开始读印第安人的书了，于是立刻在弗朗茨身上看出一位高贵的红种人，不肯错过这么个好机会，要收他做同盟、一起去对付别的无赖。于是他走上前去，向同学们陈说两个打一个多么怯懦，说弗朗茨就住在他那条街上，是个很正派的无赖，提议大家放他和平地走。就在他这样吸引住同学们注意的时候，弗朗茨爬起来跑了。

作为回报，本来给弗朗茨的那顿揍全落到了马丁身上。此外他还得忍受同学们的讥笑，笑他给无赖当朋友。而下回他在染坊门口的街上遇见弗朗茨时，后者绊了他一跤，叫他摔进阳沟，然后打得他鼻子流血，撕了他的书，狠狠骂了他一顿，跑了。

他没有懂得人家本该把他当一位高贵的红种人。可这个弗朗茨不是寻常一路的无赖；他是个彻头彻尾、糟糕透顶的无赖。

### XI / 十一

===Original===

Martin entered the high school.

Here everything was strange and cold. Gray walls, long corridors. The school yard was like the desert of Sahara. When the bell rang for the first recess, Martin slipped off by himself so as to escape his new comrades. But the next recess they gathered around him in a ring, surveying him for a while in silence, till finally a little red-haired boy with a broad pate opened his mouth to ask, “What sort of devil are you?”

At these words Martin had a dark premonition that a new stage of his life was beginning. He had been as happy as a plant in the earth, as is every little child with kind parents and a good home. Now the doors were opened upon an entirely new world, a world where one could not get on by the same simple means that his father and mother had shown him: i.e., by being polite and friendly towards all he met and never taking advantage of others. Here the thing was to decide quickly and firmly in what case one should use one’s fists, in what one should take to one’s heels, and under what circumstances one could benefit by cunning and deceit. It was not long, either, before Martin got the way of things. He suddenly remembered various curses and ugly words that he had heard from the bridge-tender’s assistant in the country, and he missed no opportunity of fitting them in here and there in conversation with his associates wherever he thought they would go. In this way he became sooner acquainted with the other boys, and they in return enlightened him in much that a newcomer might find useful: e.g., which of the teachers flogged and which only gave bad marks; that the worst of all was Director Sundell, who had mirrors in his spectacles so that he saw what was done behind his back and always wore galoshes so that he couldn’t be heard in the corridors; that “Sausages” was decent, though he marked hard, but that “The Flea” was a damned sneak.

===Chinese===

马丁进了中学。

这里一切陌生而冰冷。灰墙，长长的走廊。校园像撒哈拉沙漠。第一堂课间休息的铃一响，马丁就独自溜开，躲开他的新同学。可第二回休息，他们围成一圈聚在他身边，默不作声地打量了他一阵，末了一个宽脑门的红头发小男孩开口问：“你是哪路魔鬼？”

听了这话，马丁心里暗暗预感到，他人生的一个新阶段开始了。他本来像土里的植物一样幸福，凡是有和气父母和良好家庭的小孩都这样。如今门朝一个全然陌生的世界打开了，一个不能靠父母教给他的那套简单法子混的世界：那就是对人客客气气、和和善善，决不占别人的便宜。这里要紧的是又快又硬地断定：什么时候该动拳头，什么时候该撒腿就跑，什么情形下用得上狡猾和欺骗。没过多久，马丁也摸着了门道。他忽然想起在乡下听守桥人的帮手说过的种种咒骂和难听话，抓住一切机会，在觉得合适的地方往同伙伴们的谈话里塞。这样他很快同别的男孩混熟了；他们则回报他，把一个新生也许觉得有用的许多事指点给他：比如哪几位老师打人，哪几位只给低分；最坏的要数校长松德尔（Sundell），眼镜里装着镜子，背后做什么都看得见，还总穿着套鞋，走在走廊里一点声音没有；“香肠”人不错，虽然打分紧，可“跳蚤”是个该死的告密精。

### XII / 十二

===Original===

So year was added to year, and the new buried the old, while Martin was slowly initiated into the twofold art of life, to learn and to forget. For as the gambler in order to keep on till the last coin has run through his trembling fingers must forget his losses in the hope of future gains, so humanity, the gambler by compulsion, finds that the greatest art is to forget and that upon this depends everything.

Martin forgot. The Red Turk, who had long since wearied of jumping, was as much forgotten as if he had never been. And Uncle Abraham, who had given him to Martin and who had hanged himself with a stove-cord one rainy day, when he didn’t find it worth the trouble to live any more, was soon forgotten as well, though he now and again came up in Martin’s dreams as a dark and disturbing riddle. But while the boy was forgetting, he learned. A third of the truth was transmitted by the teachers, and another third was given by his comrades, who soon helped him to lift the veil under which was hidden the Sixth Commandment and everything pertaining to it. They made free use of the Scriptures in their researches. They explained precisely what it was that Absolom did with his father’s concubines on the roof of the palace before all the people, and they reveled with Ezekiel over the abysmal sin of Ahala and Ahaliba. But although both of these thirds were given him with an admixture of errors and lies, and although the final third﻿—which was perhaps the most important and which it was his task to search out for himself sometime﻿—had not yet begun to occupy him; yet nevertheless every day widened the chinks experience tore through the spiderweb tissue of legend and dream with which friendly hands had fenced in his childhood, and more and more often through the cracks gaped the great empty void which is called the world.

===Chinese===

就这样一年加着一年，新的埋掉旧的，马丁慢慢地被领进了生活的双重技艺：学习和遗忘。因为赌徒为了赌到最后一枚钱币从发抖的指缝里漏光，必须忘掉输掉的，盼着赢回来的；人类这个被迫的赌徒也一样，发现最了不起的技艺就是遗忘，一切都系在这上头。

马丁忘了。早就跳累了的红土耳其人，忘得干干净净，仿佛从来没有过。亚伯拉罕叔叔——把他送给马丁的那位——在一个雨天里用炉绳上了吊，因为他觉得再活下去不值当费那个事，他很快也被忘了，虽然他还不时到马丁梦里来，像一道黑暗而扰人的谜。可男孩一面忘，一面学。三分之一的教学由老师们传授，另外三分之一由同学们给与，他们很快帮他掀开了遮着第六诫及其一切相关事项的帷幕。他们在研究中大量征引《圣经》。他们精确地解释押沙龙当着众人在王宫屋顶上对他父亲的妃嫔做了什么，还同以西结一起为阿荷拉和阿荷利巴的深渊之罪陶醉。虽然这三分之二都掺着错误和谎言，虽然最后那三分之一——多半是最要紧的、要留待他自己去找的那三分之一——还没有来纠缠他；然而每一天都在扩大经验在那张传说与梦的蛛网膜上撕开的裂缝——童年时，友好的手用这张网把他围了起来——而那叫做世界的巨大空无，也越来越多地从裂缝里瞪出来。

### XIII / 十三

===Original===

It was dusk now, almost dark, and Martin was still sitting in his rocking-chair at the table and could not get up energy to light the lamp. There was a little wine left in the bottle; he poured it into his glass and drank. He had raised the window to let the smoke drift out, and through the trampling of feet which rose from below like the sound of a hundred ticking clocks he heard the house door open and close again and steps going off down the street﻿—they were Henrik’s. Martin thought about his love and what he had said about it, and he was at once struck with the fact that at the mere touch of this bit of reality his own love affair evaporated and was gone like mist and dream. Harriet Skottë.﻿ ﻿… He asked himself: “If I should read in the paper tomorrow that she was engaged or married, or that she was dead﻿—what would that signify to me? Nothing, no reality lost, no expectation gone to shipwreck﻿—just a mood burst, which would soon have burst anyhow.”

He took from his pocket the letter he had written, tore it open, and read it again. “I’ll burn it,” he thought﻿—“but why burn it? I may be able to use it sometime in a story.”

He tossed it into a table drawer among other manuscripts. Then he sank again into reverie.

---

===Chinese===

这时已是黄昏，差不多黑了，马丁还坐在桌前的摇椅里，打不起精神去点灯。瓶里还剩一点酒；他倒进杯子，喝了。他把窗子推上去，好让烟散出去；楼下升起一片脚步声，像一百座钟滴答作响，透过这片声音，他听见楼门开了又关，脚步顺着街道远去——是亨里克的。马丁想着自己的爱情，想着亨利克方才说的那些话，随即吃了一惊：这一点点现实一碰，他自己的风流韵事竟像雾和梦一样蒸发掉了。哈丽特·斯科特。……他问自己：“倘若明天我在报上读到她订了婚、结了婚，或是死了——那对我意味着什么？什么也不意味，没有失去任何实在的东西，没有任何指望遭了难——只是一个心情破了，而这心情横竖很快也要破的。”

他从口袋里掏出那封写好的信，撕开封口，又读了一遍。“把它烧了吧，”他想——“可干吗烧掉？说不定哪天还能用在哪篇故事里。”

他把它丢进桌上一只抽屉，同别的稿子放在一起。随后他又沉入遐想。

---

===Original===

Suddenly his mother stood in the doorway. She held a lamp in her hand and was leaning forward, looking at him.

“You’re sitting in the dark,” she said. “Papa has gone out. May I sit with you here a while?”

Martin nodded. She set the lamp on the table, fetched a basket with her sewing, and sat down to sew.

She sat silent, bent over her work. At length she raised her eyes, large with tears and sleeplessness.

“Tell me, Martin,” she said; “you mustn’t be cross, but one day when you were out I couldn’t help pulling out a drawer of your table and glancing at your papers. Otherwise I should never know what you’re thinking about. And what I got hold of made me so worried that I had to sit down and cry. I didn’t understand it, I don’t know if it was supposed to be verse or what it was, but I thought it was only full of terrible blasphemies. I got so frightened, I almost thought for a moment that you were out of your head. I know I don’t understand anything, but so much I can still see, that you’ll never get anywhere with writing that way. You can write very finely, too, if you want to.”

Martin was silent. What should he answer? He divined, or at least supposed, that his mother had really wished to say something quite different, and that her saying he wouldn’t get on in the world was merely a forced expedient which she caught at when thoughts and words deserted her. She had of course felt and suspected that the poem she had found in the drawer was meant to be taken quite differently from the way she now feigned to think, she wanted him to explain himself, to talk to her about his thoughts. She was pounding at the door, “Let me in! don’t make me stand outside; I’m cold and it’s so lonely!” And yet he didn’t open the door, he couldn’t; he hadn’t fastened it, it had locked itself.

===Chinese===

母亲忽然站在门口。她手里端着一盏灯，俯身望着他。

“你摸黑坐着，”她说，“爸爸出去了。我可以在这儿陪你坐一会儿吗？”

马丁点点头。她把灯放在桌上，取来针线篮，坐下做针线。

她默默地坐着，俯在活计上。末了她抬起眼睛，那双眼睛因为泪水和不眠而变大了。

“告诉我，马丁，”她说，“你可别恼：有一天你出去，我忍不住拉开你桌子的抽屉，看了几眼你的稿子。不然我永远不知道你在想什么。我看到的那些叫我这般担心，只得坐下来哭。我看不懂，不知道那算不算诗，还是别的什么，我只觉得满纸都是可怕的渎神的话。我吓坏了，有一阵几乎以为你疯了。我知道我什么都不懂，可这一点我还看得出来：你这样写下去，永远不会有什么出息。你想写的话，也能写得很漂亮。”

马丁不作声。他该回答什么呢？他猜到——至少设想——母亲真正想说的其实是另一回事，她说他在世上混不出头，只是情急之下抓住的权宜之计，那时她的思想和言语都丢下了她。她当然感觉得到、也猜得到，她在抽屉里找到的那首诗，本该按全然不同的方式去领会，而不是她现在装出的那种想法；她要他解释自己，要他把自己的思想讲给她听。她在捶门：“让我进去！别让我站在外头；我冷，这儿太孤单！”可他没有开门，他开不了；门不是他闩上的，它是自己锁上的。

===Original===

What ought he to answer her? Her words had filled him with a deep discouragement. If he had any ambition, it was to write so that each and all who really cared to could understand him. He had no taste for any literary freemasonry; he did not believe in a literature for the élite, nor had he failed to observe how often it happened that no one wanted to be of the élite. Now it suddenly became clear to him how hopeless was his ideal: there was no art for all, there were no thoughts for all; on the contrary the simplest ideas in the clearest language were but seldom understood by others than those who were familiar beforehand with just that type of thought. How should he be able to speak with her about his thoughts, when her vocabulary, as the monotony of the years had developed it, did not even suffice to express what she herself thought and felt at the bottom of her heart? The god with whom his poem dealt was of course Spinoza’s god, the World Soul; but this god was merely an intellectual experiment, whereas hers﻿—his mother’s﻿—was at least a product of the imagination and as such had a bit more life and more blood. How should he explain that what she called blasphemies did not apply to her god? She would have answered that there was only one god. He knew all she would answer and say; therefore he remained silent and looked out of the window, listening to the Saturday tread of tired feet on the pavement, and the rain which began to fall against the windowpanes.

And as to what she had said about his future, what could he say? To that there was but one answer: to be successful, to become famous. And that answer he could not give. “If I win recognition some day,” he thought to himself, “a recognition such as would gratify her, it will be when she is no longer alive. So it always is. Why should I hope for an exception for her and me?” What was he to do? Ought he to put his arms around her neck, ought he to stroke her hair and kiss her? No, that wouldn’t seem natural. He didn’t care for that sort of deception and she didn’t either; he knew her; she wouldn’t be satisfied with that. She had asked, and it was an answer she awaited. He could answer nothing, and he was silent.

He was silent and felt at the same time how the silence burned in her breast, and though he could say nothing he sought instead with his glance to meet her eyes, those eyes which used to smile so bright and blue when they looked into his. It still happened sometimes in the midst of dinner or in the evening at the tea-table that she looked at him and nodded and smiled brightly as before, as mothers nod and smile to their little children before they are able to talk. Perhaps she had the feeling that time had gone in a circle, and that this smile was the only form of expression she still had in her power when she wished to communicate with her children. It was just so that he wished she could have looked at him and nodded and smiled, with a smile far beyond all the unimportant things which separated them.

But she did not smile now; she sat silent with hands crossed on her knees, and her eyes, generally so near to weeping, now stared tearless into the shadows as if they sought and asked, “Are all mothers as unhappy as I? As lonely? As deserted by their children?”

The lamp flame fluttered in the night wind. She rose and said good night, took the lamp, and went out.

===Chinese===

他该回答她什么呢？她的话使他充满深深的沮丧。他若还有什么抱负，那就是要写得让每一个真正愿意懂的人都能够懂他。他对任何文学的行会暗语都没有趣味；他不相信有什么为精英预备的文学，也不曾忽视这样的事多么常有：谁也不想做精英。此刻他忽然看清自己的理想多么无望：没有为所有人的艺术，没有为所有人的思想；恰恰相反，最简单的意思用最清楚的语言说出来，也难得被那些事先不熟悉这类思想的人所理解。她的词汇，随着年月的单调磨成了如今的样子，连表达她自己心底的所想所感都不够用，他还怎么能同她谈自己的思想呢？他诗里写的那个神当然是斯宾诺莎的神，世界灵魂；可那个神不过是一桩智力的试验，而她的——母亲的——神至少是想象的产物，因此反倒多一点血肉。他怎么解释她所谓的渎神的话并不针对她的神呢？她会回答：神只有一个。她要回答什么、要说什么他全知道；所以他保持沉默，望着窗外，听礼拜六疲倦的脚步踏过人行道，听雨点开始敲打窗玻璃。

至于她说的他的前途，他能说什么呢？那只有一个回答：成功，成名。而这个回答他给不出。“倘若有一天我赢得了承认，”他暗想，“赢得一种能叫她欢喜的承认，那时她已经不在人世了。事情从来如此。凭什么指望她和我就例外呢？”他怎么办呢？莫非搂住她的脖子，莫非抚她的头发、吻她？不，那显得不自然。他不欢喜那种欺哄，她也不欢喜；他了解她；那满足不了她。她问了，等的就是回答。他什么也回答不出，于是沉默。

他沉默着，同时觉出这沉默在她胸口里怎样烧着；他虽说不出话，却用目光去迎她的眼睛——那双眼睛从前望着他的时候，笑得又亮又蓝。如今有时在饭桌中间、或者傍晚的茶桌上，她还会那样望他、点头、明亮地微笑，像母亲对还不会说话的小孩子那样点头微笑。也许她有一种感觉：时间兜了一个圈子，当她想同儿女相通的时候，这种微笑是她仅剩的、还使得出的表达方式。他正希望她能那样望着他、点头微笑，那微笑远远越过把他们隔开的一切无关紧要的东西。

可她此刻没有笑；她默默地坐着，两手交叠在膝上，那双素来几乎含泪的眼睛，此刻无泪地瞪着暗处，仿佛在寻找、在发问：“做母亲的都像我这样不幸吗？这样孤单？这样被儿女撇下吗？”

灯焰在夜风里抖。她起身道了晚安，端起灯，出去了。

### XIV / 十四

===Original===

Martin still sat a long while at the window.

“Here time has stood still,” Henrik Rissler had said. “Yes, he was right. Here it stood still, time. It is by changes that one measures the course of time; I have nothing to measure it with. I shouldn’t even know it was Saturday today if I didn’t hear the tramping down there.”

An old story came to his mind. There was once a sinner who died one evening in his bed. Next morning he awoke in hell, rubbed his eyes, and called, “What’s the time?” But at his side stood the devil laughing and holding up before him a clock that had no hands. Time was over and eternity had set in.

“Eternity; no hurry any more.﻿ ﻿…

“Other people have day and night, workday and holiday, Christmas and Easter. For me it all flows into one. Am I then already living in eternity?”

And he thought on: “Tomorrow is Sunday. What does that mean for me? It means that tomorrow I am free from my ostensible work, and that I thus feel twice as strongly the demand of that which should be my real work. But if the weather is fine, I shall naturally go out for a walk.﻿ ﻿… So, anyhow, it won’t be a real Sunday no matter what I do. What a strange sort of work I have taken upon me! Wouldn’t it be better to give it up while there is still time, to submit to the rules that hold for other men? One is never done with this, there is never a feeling of quiet and rest. Many a free Monday, but never a real Sunday, never any more!

===Chinese===

马丁还在窗前坐了很久。

“这儿的时间停滞不动。”亨利克·里斯勒说过。“是啊，他说得对。这儿的时间，停滞了。人凭变化来测量时间的进程；我没有什么可拿来量的。要不是听见底下的脚步声，我连今天是礼拜六都不会知道。”

他想起一个老故事。从前有个罪人，一天晚上死在床上。第二天早晨他在地狱里醒来，揉揉眼睛，喊道：“几点了？”可他身边站着魔鬼，笑着，朝他举起一座没有指针的钟。时间完了，永恒开始了。

“永恒；再也不用着急了。……

“别人有白天黑夜，有工作日和节日，有圣诞和复活节。我这里全流成了一片。莫非我已经活在永恒里了？”

他接着想：“明天是礼拜天。这对我意味着什么？意味着明天我可以摆脱我那表面上的工作，于是那件本该是我真正工作的事的要求，就加倍沉重地压下来。可要是天气好，我自然要出去走走。……反正不管我做什么，这都不是一个真正的礼拜天。我揽到自己身上的这是怎样一种奇怪的工作啊！趁还来得及放弃它、去服从对别人生效的规矩，岂不更好？这件事永远做不完，永远没有安静和休息的感觉。多少个闲散的礼拜一，却再没有一个真正的礼拜天，再也没有了！

===Original===

“My ostensible and my real work﻿—how long shall I be able to keep up this illusion? The truth is I’m in a good way to get a permanent job, that in eight or ten years I could become a regular clerk, and in forty years would get my discharge with a pension. My poor mother would be able to spare herself a deal of trouble if she saw all that clearly as I do now. But she imagines in the innocence of her heart that what I write on a few scraps of paper at night will hinder my advancement, for she has no conception of the boundless indifference of men of ideas. To hurt my prospects I should be forced to write personal abuse about my superiors, and why should I do that? They are good-natured men and have got me gratuities and commissions although others deserved them better. They have certainly taken an interest in me. I am not the sort of fellow to put a torpedo under the ark; they have felt that instinctively, and they are presumably right.”

He felt that he would eventually be lost in the multitude. He could not escape the thought that he was at bottom like all the rest; and whether this was his rightful fate, or whether he was too exceptional to be effective among exceptions, he felt only that routine held him every day more tightly a prisoner and that he was going to be lost in the crowd. And the other thing﻿—his poetry; what was that and whither could it lead? Once when he had needed money he had collected a bundle of his poems and gone around to the publishers. A couple of them had wanted to print the volume but none had been willing to pay anything. “No,” he had answered very seriously, “do not count on my ambition!” When he had come home he had looked through these verses again; and again, as so many times before, he had found them uninspired and empty. Most of them were written so as to be sold at once to a magazine and showed that they were so written. And he said to himself, “How absurd it is for a man to make a business of ideas when he has no sure means of subsistence! As clever as the way the minister at a funeral sermon transforms the dead man’s means of livelihood into a mission in life. But existence knows how briskly and mercilessly to transform a mission in life into a means of livelihood for a man with no income. Yet supposing this should be a real means of livelihood﻿—but no, it won’t be; distaste and weariness will come, one will tire of the whole thing and sink back, down into the crowd.

“Down into the crowd; one will do as the others do, there will at least be no more need of conjuring tricks, one will get back his sense of time, one will have Sundays and weekdays, work and rest, real rest.﻿ ﻿…”

---

===Chinese===

“我那表面上的和真正的工作——这错觉我还能维持多久？实情是，我正稳稳走在谋得一个终身差事的路上，八年后十年后能当上正式文书，四十年后领一笔养老金退职。我可怜的母亲若能像我现在这样看得清清楚楚，就能省去许多操心和烦恼。可她凭她心地里的天真，以为我夜里在几张纸片上写的东西会妨碍我的升迁，因为她全然想象不到有思想的人有多无边无际的冷漠。要伤我的前程，除非我去写文章人身攻击我的上司；我干吗要那样做？他们都是好心人，虽然别人更配，他们还是给我弄到了津贴和差事。他们确实对我有关照之意。我不是那种往方舟底下放鱼雷的人；这一点他们凭本能感觉到了，而且多半没有感觉错。”

他感到自己终究要消失在人群里。他逃不脱这个念头：自己骨子里同其余所有人一样；至于这是否他命该如此，还是他例外得连在例外者中间都施展不开，他只觉得日常事务一天比一天更紧地把他锁住，他就要消失在人群里了。而那另一样东西——他的诗；它是什么，又能引到哪里去？有一回他需要钱，把他的诗凑成一束，跑了几家出版社。有两家愿意印这一册，可谁也不肯出一文钱。“不，”他非常郑重地回答，“别指望我的虚荣心！”回到家，他把这些诗又看了一遍；同从前许多次一样，他又觉得它们没有灵气，空空的。多半写的时候就存心立刻卖给杂志，而且一看就知道是这么写的。他对自己说：“一个人没有可靠的生计，却拿思想做买卖，这是多么荒唐！聪明得像牧师在葬礼布道里把死人的营生说成人生的使命。可是存在知道，对一个没有进项的人，它把人生使命变回营生时有多么利索、多么无情。不过，就算这当真是可靠的营生——不，它成不了；厌腻和疲倦会来的，人会腻了这一切，沉下去，落回人群里。

“落回人群里；人家干什么自己也干什么，至少再也不用变戏法，人能找回时间的感觉，有礼拜天和礼拜一，有劳作和休息，真正的休息。……”

---

===Original===

The night air streamed in cold through the window, he shivered but couldn’t make himself raise his arms and shut the sash. The rain fell steadily, and, as often happened when he was very tired, his thoughts began to go into meter and rhyme:

> I sit alone in the darkness

 And hear the falling rain,

 I hear the drops come plashing

 Against the windowpane.

> A grief on my heart lies heavy,

 My labored breath comes fast.

 Drop after drop my youthtime

 Is trickling, trickling past.

===Chinese===

夜里的冷空气从窗口涌进来，他打了个寒噤，却抬不起胳膊去关窗。雨不停地下，而每逢他非常疲倦的时候，他的思想便像惯常那样，自己合起了节拍和韵脚：

> 我独坐在黑暗里

 听着落雨的声音，

 我听见雨点噼啪

 打在窗玻璃上。

> 一件伤心事压在心头，

 我的呼吸急促艰难。

> 我青春的时光一滴又一滴，

 正淅淅沥沥地淌干。
